## Proof, mathematical argument, problem solving and modelling

This file covers the four methods of proof, the language of logic and sets, and the three overarching themes that every A-level paper assesses: argument and proof (OT1), problem solving (OT2) and modelling (OT3).

### Overview

Proof by deduction, proof by exhaustion and disproof by counter example are AS content. Proof by contradiction, including the two classic proofs (the square root of 2 is irrational, and there are infinitely many primes), is A-level only.

The overarching themes are not a separate topic: they are skills used in every question. They appear here so each one has a home.

#### Board differences

- AQA 7357: A1 for proof, plus the overarching themes OT1.1 to OT3.5 written into the spec.
- Edexcel 9MA0: Pure topic 1.1. The overarching themes are in the DfE content, not numbered in the Edexcel content table.
- OCR H240: 1.01a to 1.01d. 1.01b names the logical connectives (implies, is implied by, if and only if) explicitly.
- OCR MEI H640: Mp1 to Mp3 for proof. Ma1 (algebraic vocabulary and notation) is covered here and in file 02.

### OT1 Mathematical argument, language and proof

*Refs: AQA OT1.1 to OT1.5 · MEI Ma1. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Present a mathematical argument as a chain of steps, each following from the last.
- Use the symbols ⇒ (implies), ⇐ (is implied by) and ⇔ (if and only if) correctly.
- Use set notation: ∈, ∉, ⊂, ∪, ∩, ∅, {x : ...}, and the sets ℕ, ℤ, ℚ, ℝ.
- Know what a function is: a rule that gives exactly one output for each input in its domain. The set of outputs is the range.
- Read an argument and find the step where it goes wrong.

#### Explained

**Implication.** "P ⇒ Q" means: whenever P is true, Q is true. It does not say Q ⇒ P. For example, x = 3 ⇒ x² = 9 is true, but x² = 9 ⇒ x = 3 is false, because x could be −3. When both directions hold you write P ⇔ Q, read "P if and only if Q" (sometimes "iff").

**Equals versus identical.** "=" says two things are equal for the values in question. "≡" says they are equal for every value of the variable: (x + 1)² ≡ x² + 2x + 1.

**Set notation.** Solution sets are often written in set notation:

```latex
\{x : x < -2\} \cup \{x : x > 5\} \qquad \{x : -1 \le x \le 4\}
```

The union ∪ means "or" (either set), and the intersection ∩ means "and" (both at once).

**Number sets.**

| Symbol | Set | Examples |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ℕ | natural numbers | 1, 2, 3, ... |
| ℤ | integers | ..., −2, −1, 0, 1, 2, ... |
| ℚ | rational numbers, p/q with p, q integers and q ≠ 0 | 3/4, −5, 0.1 |
| ℝ | real numbers | all of the above, plus √2, π, e |

**Critiquing an argument.** Common faults in a "proof": dividing by something that might be zero, squaring both sides (which can create false solutions), assuming the result you are trying to prove, or checking a few cases and claiming the result for all.

#### Worked example

A student "proves" that 1 = 2 as follows. Let a = b. Then a² = ab, so a² − b² = ab − b², so (a + b)(a − b) = b(a − b), so a + b = b, so 2b = b, so 2 = 1. Find the error.

1. Since a = b, the factor a − b equals 0.
2. Going from (a + b)(a − b) = b(a − b) to a + b = b divides both sides by a − b, which is division by zero. That step is invalid.

Which of ⇒, ⇐, ⇔ goes between "n is a multiple of 6" and "n is even"?

1. If n is a multiple of 6, n = 6k = 2(3k), which is even. So the first ⇒ the second.
2. 4 is even but not a multiple of 6, so the reverse fails.
3. Answer: n is a multiple of 6 ⇒ n is even.

#### Common mistakes

- Writing ⇔ when only one direction is true.
- Using "=" between lines of working that are not equal, for example writing "x² − 4 = 0 = x = 2".
- Forgetting that a function must give one output per input: y = ±√x is not a function.

### OT2 Mathematical problem solving

*Refs: AQA OT2.1 to OT2.7. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Spot the structure of a problem: which topic it really is, and what can be simplified.
- Build an extended argument for an unstructured problem (one with no step-by-step parts).
- Interpret answers in the context of the original problem.
- Know that many problems cannot be solved exactly and need numerical methods (see file 17).
- Judge how accurate and reliable an answer is.
- Follow the problem solving cycle: specify the problem, collect information, process and represent, interpret, and then go round again if needed.
- Read information from diagrams and draw your own.

#### Explained

An unstructured question gives you a goal and no route. A reliable approach:

1. Write down what you know and what you want, in symbols.
2. Draw a diagram. Label it.
3. Ask which tools link the two: an equation, a formula, a known result.
4. Work forwards from what you know and backwards from what you want until the two meet.
5. Check the answer: is it the right size, the right sign, in the right units? Does it answer the question that was asked?

#### Worked example

A rectangle has perimeter 40 cm. Show that its area cannot exceed 100 cm².

1. Let the sides be x and 20 − x (half the perimeter is 20).
2. Area A = x(20 − x) = 20x − x².
3. Complete the square: A = 100 − (x − 10)².
4. (x − 10)² ≥ 0 for all real x, so A ≤ 100, with equality when x = 10 (a square).

Two numbers add to 12 and their squares add to 80. Find them.

1. x + y = 12 and x² + y² = 80.
2. (x + y)² = x² + 2xy + y², so 144 = 80 + 2xy and xy = 32.
3. The numbers are roots of t² − 12t + 32 = 0, so (t − 4)(t − 8) = 0.
4. The numbers are 4 and 8.

#### Common mistakes

- Giving a number without saying what it means in context (for example "x = 10" instead of "the rectangle is a 10 cm square").
- Not stating that an answer from a numerical method is approximate.

### OT3 Mathematical modelling

*Refs: AQA OT3.1 to OT3.5. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Turn a real situation into a mathematical model, making simplifying assumptions.
- Use the model with suitable inputs and interpret its outputs in context.
- Refine a model when its outputs do not match reality.
- State and criticise modelling assumptions.

#### Explained

A model is a simplified mathematical description. In mechanics, a car might be modelled as a particle (no size), a road as smooth (no friction), a rope as light and inextensible. In statistics, the number of faulty items in a batch might be modelled with a binomial distribution, which assumes each item is faulty independently with the same probability. In pure maths, a population might be modelled by P = P₀e^{kt}.

Each assumption has a cost. Ask two questions:

1. What does the assumption let me do? (For example, "particle" means I can ignore rotation.)
2. What does it ignore, and how would the answer change if I removed it? (For example, ignoring air resistance makes a ball travel further than it really would.)

Refining means changing an assumption, or adding a term, and checking whether the new model fits the data better.

#### Worked example

A model for the value V (in pounds) of a car t years after purchase is V = 18 000e^{−0.15t}.

(a) Find the value after 4 years. V = 18 000e^{−0.6} = 9878.83..., about £9880.

(b) Give one criticism of the model. It predicts the car is never worth £0 and is still worth over £400 after 25 years, while a very old car may be scrapped (worth nothing) or become a collectable (worth more). It also assumes the same percentage loss every year, whereas cars often lose more value in the first year.

(c) Suggest a refinement. Add a constant floor, V = 16 500e^{−0.15t} + 1500, so the value levels off at a scrap or trade-in value of £1500 (the starting value is still £18 000).

#### Common mistakes

- Listing an assumption without saying how it affects the answer.
- Criticising a model for something it was never meant to include.

### A1 Proof

*Refs: AQA A1 · Edexcel 1.1 · OCR 1.01a, 1.01b, 1.01c, 1.01d · MEI Mp1, Mp2, Mp3. Level: AS & A (part A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- Proof by deduction: start from known facts or definitions and reach the result by logical steps.
- Proof by exhaustion: split the possibilities into a finite number of cases and check every one.
- Disproof by counter example: one example where the statement fails is enough to show it is false.
- (A-level only) Proof by contradiction: assume the statement is false and show this leads to something impossible. This includes proving √2 is irrational and that there are infinitely many primes, and applying the method to new statements.

#### Explained

**Useful forms for integers.** Let n be any integer.

| Type of number | Form |
| --- | --- |
| even | 2n |
| odd | 2n + 1 |
| consecutive integers | n, n + 1, n + 2 |
| consecutive odd numbers | 2n + 1, 2n + 3 |
| multiple of k | kn |
| remainder r on division by k | kn + r |

**Deduction.** Write the general case using these forms, then rearrange until the result is clear. End with a sentence that states what has been shown.

**Exhaustion.** Useful when there are only a few cases, or when every integer falls into one of a few types (even or odd; remainder 0, 1 or 2 on division by 3).

**Counter example.** To disprove "for all x, P(x)", find one x that makes P(x) false. Show the working that it is false.

**Contradiction (A-level only).** Write "Assume, for contradiction, that ..." with the negation of the statement. Reason until you reach a statement that contradicts the assumption or a known fact. Conclude "this is a contradiction, so the original statement is true."

The negation of "for all x, P(x)" is "there is some x for which P(x) is false". The negation of "there is no largest prime" is "there is a largest prime".

#### Worked example

**Deduction.** Prove that the sum of any three consecutive integers is a multiple of 3.

1. Let the integers be n, n + 1, n + 2 for some integer n.
2. Sum = 3n + 3 = 3(n + 1).
3. n + 1 is an integer, so the sum is a multiple of 3.

**Deduction.** Prove that the difference between the squares of two consecutive odd numbers is a multiple of 8.

1. Let the numbers be 2n + 1 and 2n + 3.
2. (2n + 3)² − (2n + 1)² = (4n² + 12n + 9) − (4n² + 4n + 1) = 8n + 8 = 8(n + 1).
3. This is 8 times an integer, so it is a multiple of 8.

**Exhaustion.** Prove that n² + n is even for every integer n.

1. Case 1, n even: n = 2k. n² + n = 4k² + 2k = 2(2k² + k), which is even.
2. Case 2, n odd: n = 2k + 1. n² + n = (2k + 1)(2k + 2) = 2(2k + 1)(k + 1), which is even.
3. Every integer is even or odd, so n² + n is always even.

**Exhaustion.** Prove that no square number ends in the digit 7.

1. The last digit of n² depends only on the last digit of n.
2. Last digits 0 to 9 give squares ending in 0, 1, 4, 9, 6, 5, 6, 9, 4, 1.
3. None of these is 7, so no square number ends in 7.

**Counter example.** Show that "2ⁿ + 1 is prime for every positive integer n" is false.

1. n = 3: 2³ + 1 = 9 = 3 × 3, which is not prime.
2. So the statement is false.

**Completing the square in a proof.** Prove that x² − 6x + 11 > 0 for all real x.

1. x² − 6x + 11 = (x − 3)² + 2.
2. (x − 3)² ≥ 0, so (x − 3)² + 2 ≥ 2 > 0.

**(A-level only) Contradiction: √2 is irrational.**

1. Assume, for contradiction, that √2 is rational. Then √2 = a/b where a and b are integers with no common factor and b ≠ 0.
2. Square: 2 = a²/b², so a² = 2b². So a² is even, which means a is even (an odd number squared is odd).
3. Write a = 2c. Then 4c² = 2b², so b² = 2c², so b² is even and b is even.
4. Now a and b are both even, so they share the factor 2. This contradicts "no common factor".
5. So √2 is irrational.

**(A-level only) Contradiction: there are infinitely many primes.**

1. Assume, for contradiction, that there are finitely many primes, p₁, p₂, ..., pₙ.
2. Let N = p₁p₂...pₙ + 1.
3. Dividing N by any pᵢ leaves remainder 1, so no pᵢ is a factor of N.
4. N > 1, so N has at least one prime factor. That prime is not in the list. This contradicts the assumption that the list contains every prime.
5. So there are infinitely many primes.

Note: N itself need not be prime (2 × 3 × 5 × 7 × 11 × 13 + 1 = 30 031 = 59 × 509). The argument only needs N to have a prime factor missing from the list.

**(A-level only) Contradiction applied to a new statement.** Prove that if n² is odd then n is odd, for integer n.

1. Assume, for contradiction, that n² is odd and n is even.
2. n = 2k, so n² = 4k² = 2(2k²), which is even.
3. This contradicts n² being odd. So n is odd.

**(A-level only)** Prove that there are no positive integers x and y with x² − y² = 1.

1. Assume there are. Then (x − y)(x + y) = 1.
2. x + y is a positive integer, and the product is 1, so x + y = 1 and x − y = 1.
3. Then x = 1 and y = 0, but y must be positive. Contradiction.

#### Common mistakes

- Proving a general statement by checking examples. Examples never prove "for all"; they can only disprove.
- Using the same letter for two different numbers: "let the two odd numbers be 2n + 1 and 2n + 1" proves a result only about equal numbers. Use 2m + 1 and 2n + 1.
- Missing a case in exhaustion, for example checking positive integers but not zero or negatives.
- Not writing a concluding sentence.
- (A-level only) Assuming the wrong negation at the start of a contradiction proof.
- (A-level only) In the primes proof, claiming N must be prime.

### Check questions

1. Insert ⇒, ⇐ or ⇔ between "x > 3" and "x² > 9" (x real). Explain your choice. [2 marks]
2. Prove that the sum of any two odd numbers is even. [2 marks]
3. Prove that the product of any two consecutive integers is even. [3 marks]
4. Find a counter example to show that "if a > b then a² > b²" is false for real a and b. [2 marks]
5. Prove by exhaustion that for every integer n, n³ − n is a multiple of 3. [4 marks]
6. Prove that 4x² − 12x + 10 > 0 for all real x. [3 marks]
7. Explain the error in this argument: "x² = 4x, so dividing by x gives x = 4. So the only solution is x = 4." [2 marks]
8. A student says: "For any prime p, p² + 2 is not prime." Prove or disprove this. [2 marks]
9. (A-level only) Prove by contradiction that √3 is irrational. You may use the fact that if 3 divides a² then 3 divides a. [5 marks]
10. (A-level only) Prove by contradiction that, for any integer n, if n² is not a multiple of 4 then n is odd. [3 marks]
11. A model for the height h metres of a ball t seconds after it is thrown up is h = 1.5 + 12t − 4.9t². State one modelling assumption and one way the model could be refined. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. x > 3 ⇒ x² > 9 (1); reverse fails, e.g. x = −4 gives x² = 16 > 9 but x < 3 (1).
2. Let the numbers be 2m + 1 and 2n + 1 (1); sum = 2m + 2n + 2 = 2(m + n + 1), which is even (1).
3. Let the integers be n and n + 1 (1); one of them is even, or exhaustion: n = 2k gives 2k(2k + 1), n = 2k + 1 gives (2k + 1)(2k + 2) = 2(2k + 1)(k + 1) (1); both even, conclusion (1).
4. For example a = 1, b = −3 (1); a > b but a² = 1 < 9 = b² (1).
5. n³ − n = (n − 1)n(n + 1) or cases by remainder mod 3 (1); n = 3k gives 3k(9k² − 1) (1); n = 3k + 1 gives (3k + 1)(9k² + 6k) = 3(3k + 1)(3k² + 2k) (1); n = 3k + 2 gives (3k + 2)(3k + 1)(3k + 3), which has factor 3, and conclusion (1).
6. 4(x² − 3x) + 10 (1); = 4(x − 1.5)² + 1 (1); square ≥ 0 so expression ≥ 1 > 0 (1).
7. Dividing by x assumes x ≠ 0 (1); x = 0 is also a solution (1).
8. Disprove: p = 3 gives 11, which is prime (1); so the statement is false (1).
9. Assume √3 = a/b in lowest terms (1); a² = 3b², so 3 divides a² and so a (1); a = 3c gives 9c² = 3b², b² = 3c² (1); so 3 divides b (1); a and b share factor 3, contradiction, so √3 irrational (1).
10. Assume n² is not a multiple of 4 and n is even (1); n = 2k, n² = 4k², a multiple of 4 (1); contradiction, so n is odd (1).
11. Assumption, e.g. no air resistance, or ball modelled as a particle (1); refinement, e.g. include air resistance, or use a more accurate value of g (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/maths/02-algebra-indices-surds-quadratics-and-inequalities.md -->

## Algebra: indices, surds, quadratics, simultaneous equations and inequalities

This file covers the algebra that the rest of the course is built on: the laws of indices, surds, quadratic functions and the discriminant, simultaneous equations, and linear and quadratic inequalities.

### Overview

Everything here is AS content and is assumed in every later topic. Much of it extends GCSE Higher; the new parts are the discriminant conditions, quadratics "in a function of x", inequalities written in set notation, and shading regions for inequalities in two variables.

#### Board differences

- AQA B1 to B5 and Edexcel 2.1 to 2.5 follow the DfE wording point for point.
- OCR H240 splits these into 1.02a to 1.02i. Its order is indices, surds, simultaneous equations, then quadratics, then inequalities.
- OCR MEI H640 uses Ma1 to Ma13. Ma1 (vocabulary such as "identity", "expression", "coefficient") and Ma6 (the meaning of points of intersection) are named separately.

### B1 Laws of indices

*Refs: AQA B1 · Edexcel 2.1 · OCR 1.02a · MEI Ma1, Ma12, Ma13. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The laws of indices for all rational powers, including negative, zero and fractional indices.

```latex
a^m \times a^n = a^{m+n} \qquad a^m \div a^n = a^{m-n} \qquad (a^m)^n = a^{mn}
```

```latex
a^0 = 1 \qquad a^{-n} = \frac{1}{a^n} \qquad a^{\frac{1}{n}} = \sqrt[n]{a} \qquad a^{\frac{m}{n}} = \left(\sqrt[n]{a}\right)^m
```

#### Explained

A negative power means "one over". A fractional power means a root: the denominator is the root, the numerator is the power. Work out the root first, because the numbers stay smaller.

Writing terms as powers of x is the key step before differentiating or integrating. For example:

```latex
\frac{3}{\sqrt{x}} = 3x^{-\frac{1}{2}} \qquad \frac{x^2 + 4}{x^3} = x^{-1} + 4x^{-3} \qquad \sqrt[3]{x^2} = x^{\frac{2}{3}}
```

Equations with the unknown in the power can often be solved by writing both sides as powers of the same base.

#### Worked example

Evaluate 32^{−3/5}.

1. Fifth root of 32 is 2.
2. 2³ = 8.
3. The negative power gives 1/8.

Simplify (4x⁶y⁻²)^{3/2}.

1. 4^{3/2} = (√4)³ = 8.
2. (x⁶)^{3/2} = x⁹ and (y⁻²)^{3/2} = y⁻³.
3. Answer: 8x⁹/y³.

Solve 9^{x+1} = 27^{x}.

1. Write as powers of 3: 3^{2x+2} = 3^{3x}.
2. 2x + 2 = 3x, so x = 2.

Write (2√x − 1)² / x in the form ax^p + bx^q + cx^r.

1. Expand: 4x − 4√x + 1.
2. Divide each term by x: 4 − 4x^{−1/2} + x^{−1}.

#### Common mistakes

- Writing 3x^{−2} as 1/(3x²). The power applies only to x, so it is 3/x².
- Adding the powers when terms are added: x² + x³ is not x⁵.
- Writing √(x² + 9) as x + 3.

### B2 Surds

*Refs: AQA B2 · Edexcel 2.2 · OCR 1.02b · MEI Ma10, Ma11. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Simplify surds using √(ab) = √a × √b and √(a/b) = √a / √b.
- Rationalise denominators of the forms 1/√a and 1/(a ± √b).

#### Explained

To simplify √n, split off the largest square factor: √72 = √36 × √2 = 6√2.

To rationalise a denominator a + √b, multiply top and bottom by a − √b (the conjugate). The product is a difference of two squares, so the surd disappears:

```latex
(a+\sqrt{b})(a-\sqrt{b}) = a^2 - b
```

#### Worked example

Simplify √50 + √18 − √8.

1. 5√2 + 3√2 − 2√2 = 6√2.

Rationalise 6/√3.

1. Multiply by √3/√3: 6√3/3 = 2√3.

Write (5 + √3)/(2 − √3) in the form a + b√3.

1. Multiply top and bottom by 2 + √3.
2. Top: (5 + √3)(2 + √3) = 10 + 5√3 + 2√3 + 3 = 13 + 7√3.
3. Bottom: 4 − 3 = 1.
4. Answer: 13 + 7√3.

A rectangle has area 10 cm² and width (√5 + 1) cm. Find its length in the form a + b√5.

1. Length = 10/(√5 + 1). Multiply by (√5 − 1)/(√5 − 1).
2. 10(√5 − 1)/(5 − 1) = 10(√5 − 1)/4 = (5√5 − 5)/2.
3. Length = −5/2 + (5/2)√5 cm.

#### Common mistakes

- √a + √b is not √(a + b).
- Forgetting to multiply the numerator by the conjugate as well.

### B3 Quadratic functions

*Refs: AQA B3 · Edexcel 2.3 · OCR 1.02d, 1.02e, 1.02f · MEI Ma2, Ma3. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Sketch y = ax² + bx + c: shape, roots, y-intercept, turning point.
- The discriminant b² − 4ac and what its sign says about the roots.
- Complete the square to find the turning point.
- Solve quadratics by factorising, completing the square or the formula.
- Solve quadratics in a function of x, such as x⁴ − 5x² + 4 = 0 or 2^{2x} − 6 × 2^x + 8 = 0.

#### Explained

**Discriminant.** For ax² + bx + c = 0:

| b² − 4ac | Roots | Graph |
| --- | --- | --- |
| > 0 | two distinct real roots | crosses the x-axis twice |
| = 0 | one repeated root | touches the x-axis |
| < 0 | no real roots | does not meet the x-axis |

"Real roots" (at least one) means b² − 4ac ≥ 0.

**Completing the square.**

```latex
ax^2 + bx + c = a\left(x + \frac{b}{2a}\right)^2 + c - \frac{b^2}{4a}
```

In practice, take out a, halve the coefficient of x, and adjust the constant. For y = a(x + p)² + q the turning point is (−p, q). It is a minimum if a > 0 and a maximum if a < 0.

**Quadratic formula.**

```latex
x = \frac{-b \pm \sqrt{b^2 - 4ac}}{2a}
```

**Hidden quadratics.** If an equation contains u and u² for some expression u (such as x² and x⁴, or √x and x, or 3^x and 3^{2x}), substitute u, solve the quadratic in u, then go back to x. Reject values that are impossible, such as a negative value of 3^x or of √x.

#### Worked example

Write 2x² − 12x + 23 in the form a(x + b)² + c and state the minimum point of y = 2x² − 12x + 23.

1. 2(x² − 6x) + 23.
2. 2[(x − 3)² − 9] + 23 = 2(x − 3)² + 5.
3. Minimum point (3, 5).

Find the values of k for which kx² + 4x + (k − 3) = 0 has two distinct real roots.

1. b² − 4ac > 0: 16 − 4k(k − 3) > 0.
2. 16 − 4k² + 12k > 0, so 4k² − 12k − 16 < 0, so k² − 3k − 4 < 0.
3. (k − 4)(k + 1) < 0, so −1 < k < 4.
4. Also k ≠ 0, otherwise the equation is not quadratic. Answer: −1 < k < 4, k ≠ 0.

Solve x⁴ − 13x² + 36 = 0.

1. Let u = x²: u² − 13u + 36 = 0, (u − 4)(u − 9) = 0.
2. x² = 4 or 9, so x = ±2 or ±3.

Solve 3^{2x} − 4 × 3^{x} + 3 = 0.

1. Let u = 3^x: u² − 4u + 3 = 0, (u − 1)(u − 3) = 0.
2. 3^x = 1 gives x = 0; 3^x = 3 gives x = 1.

Solve x − 5√x + 6 = 0.

1. Let u = √x: u² − 5u + 6 = 0, u = 2 or 3.
2. x = 4 or 9.

Find the set of values of k for which x² + (k − 2)x + k = 0 has real roots. (This uses the inequality method in B5.)

1. Real roots: (k − 2)² − 4k ≥ 0, so k² − 8k + 4 ≥ 0.
2. Critical values: k = (8 ± √48)/2 = 4 ± 2√3.
3. The quadratic in k is positive outside its roots: k ≤ 4 − 2√3 or k ≥ 4 + 2√3.
4. Check with k = 0 (in the set): x² − 2x = 0 has roots 0 and 2, which are real.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting to multiply the "subtracted square" by a when a ≠ 1.
- Using b² − 4ac > 0 when the question says "real roots" (which allows equal roots, so ≥ 0).
- Forgetting the condition that the x² coefficient is non-zero.
- Keeping impossible roots, such as 2^x = −3.

### B4 Simultaneous equations

*Refs: AQA B4 · Edexcel 2.4 · OCR 1.02c · MEI Ma4, Ma5, Ma6. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Solve two linear equations by elimination or substitution.
- Solve one linear and one quadratic equation by substitution.
- Interpret solutions as points of intersection of graphs. The number of solutions is the number of intersections; a repeated root means the line is a tangent.

#### Explained

For a linear and a quadratic: rearrange the linear equation for one variable, substitute into the quadratic, solve, then substitute back into the linear equation (not the quadratic, which would give extra values).

The discriminant of the resulting quadratic tells you how a line and a curve meet: two points, a tangent (one point), or no meeting.

#### Worked example

Solve y = 2x − 1 and x² + xy = 10.

1. Substitute: x² + x(2x − 1) = 10, so 3x² − x − 10 = 0.
2. (3x + 5)(x − 2) = 0, so x = 2 or x = −5/3.
3. Use the linear equation: x = 2 gives y = 3; x = −5/3 gives y = −13/3.
4. Solutions (2, 3) and (−5/3, −13/3).

Find the value of c for which the line y = 3x + c is a tangent to the curve y = x² − x + 2.

1. x² − x + 2 = 3x + c gives x² − 4x + (2 − c) = 0.
2. Tangent means one repeated root: 16 − 4(2 − c) = 0.
3. 16 − 8 + 4c = 0, so c = −2.

Solve 2x + 3y = 7 and 5x − 2y = 8.

1. Multiply: 4x + 6y = 14 and 15x − 6y = 24.
2. Add: 19x = 38, x = 2. Then 3y = 3, y = 1.

#### Common mistakes

- Substituting back into the quadratic and getting extra, wrong pairs.
- Not pairing each x with its own y.

### B5 Inequalities

*Refs: AQA B5 · Edexcel 2.5 · OCR 1.02g, 1.02h, 1.02i · MEI Ma7, Ma8, Ma9. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Solve linear and quadratic inequalities in one variable, including ones with brackets and fractions.
- Write answers with "and" and "or", or in set notation.
- Represent inequalities such as y > x + 1 and y > ax² + bx + c as regions on a graph.

#### Explained

**Linear inequalities.** Solve like equations, but reverse the sign when you multiply or divide by a negative number.

**Quadratic inequalities.** Always use a sketch:

1. Rearrange to f(x) > 0 (or <, ≤, ≥).
2. Find the critical values: the roots of f(x) = 0.
3. Sketch y = f(x).
4. Read off where the graph is above (> 0) or below (< 0) the axis.

For a positive x² coefficient, "< 0" gives the region between the roots (one interval, use "and"), and "> 0" gives the two outer regions (use "or").

```latex
(x-1)(x-5) < 0 \iff 1 < x < 5 \iff \{x : 1 < x < 5\}
```

```latex
(x-1)(x-5) > 0 \iff x < 1 \text{ or } x > 5 \iff \{x : x < 1\} \cup \{x : x > 5\}
```

**Inequalities with x in the denominator.** Do not multiply by an expression that might be negative. Multiply both sides by the square of the denominator, which is positive.

**Regions.** Draw the boundary curve: a dashed line for < or > (points on it are not included), solid for ≤ or ≥. Shade the side that satisfies the inequality; test a point such as (0, 0) if unsure. Exam questions say whether to shade the wanted or unwanted region, so read carefully.

#### Worked example

Solve 3 − 2x ≤ 11.

1. −2x ≤ 8, so x ≥ −4 (sign reverses).

Solve 2x² + 5x − 3 ≥ 0.

1. (2x − 1)(x + 3) = 0 at x = 1/2 and x = −3.
2. Positive x² coefficient: the graph is above the axis outside the roots.
3. x ≤ −3 or x ≥ 1/2. In set notation, {x : x ≤ −3} ∪ {x : x ≥ 1/2}.

Find the set of values of x satisfying both 4x − 3 > 5 and x² − 7x + 10 < 0.

1. First: x > 2.
2. Second: (x − 2)(x − 5) < 0, so 2 < x < 5.
3. Both: 2 < x < 5, or {x : 2 < x < 5}.

Solve 6/x > 2 for x ≠ 0.

1. Multiply by x² (positive): 6x > 2x².
2. 2x² − 6x < 0, so 2x(x − 3) < 0.
3. 0 < x < 3.

Describe the region that satisfies y ≥ x² − 4 and y < x + 2.

1. Solid parabola y = x² − 4 (roots ±2, vertex (0, −4)); the region is on or above it.
2. Dashed line y = x + 2; the region is below it.
3. They meet where x² − 4 = x + 2: x² − x − 6 = 0, x = 3 or x = −2, at (3, 5) and (−2, 0).
4. The region is the area enclosed between the parabola and the line for −2 ≤ x ≤ 3, including the parabola boundary but not the line.

#### Common mistakes

- Writing the answer to "> 0" as one combined inequality such as 5 < x < 1, which is impossible.
- Multiplying by x when x could be negative.
- Using solid lines for strict inequalities.

### Check questions

1. Simplify (27x⁶)^{−2/3}. [2 marks]
2. Solve 4^{x} = 8^{x−1}. [3 marks]
3. Express (3 − √2)/(1 + √2) in the form a + b√2. [3 marks]
4. (a) Write x² + 10x + 18 in the form (x + p)² + q. (b) Hence state the coordinates of the turning point of y = x² + 10x + 18 and solve x² + 10x + 18 = 0, giving exact answers. [5 marks]
5. The equation x² + 2kx + 3k = 0 has equal roots. Find the possible values of k. [3 marks]
6. Solve 2x^{2/3} − 7x^{1/3} + 3 = 0. [4 marks]
7. Solve the simultaneous equations x − y = 4 and x² + y² = 40. [5 marks]
8. Find the set of values of k for which the line y = kx − 2 does not meet the curve y = x² + 2x + 2. [5 marks]
9. Solve 3x² + 2x − 8 < 0, giving your answer in set notation. [3 marks]
10. Solve (x − 2)/(x + 1) < 1 for x ≠ −1. [4 marks]
11. Sketch and shade the region where y ≤ 6 − x − x² and y ≥ 0. Give the coordinates where the boundaries meet. [4 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. 27^{−2/3} = 1/9 (1); x^{−4}, so 1/(9x⁴) (1).
2. 2^{2x} = 2^{3x−3} (1); 2x = 3x − 3 (1); x = 3 (1).
3. Multiply by (1 − √2)/(1 − √2) (1); numerator 3 − 3√2 − √2 + 2 = 5 − 4√2, denominator −1 (1); −5 + 4√2 (1).
4. (a) (x + 5)² − 7 (2: one for each of p and q). (b) (−5, −7) (1); (x + 5)² = 7 (1); x = −5 ± √7 (1).
5. 4k² − 12k = 0 (1); 4k(k − 3) = 0 (1); k = 0 or k = 3 (1).
6. u = x^{1/3}: 2u² − 7u + 3 = 0 (1); (2u − 1)(u − 3) = 0, u = 1/2 or 3 (1); x = u³ (1); x = 1/8 or 27 (1).
7. x = y + 4 (1); (y + 4)² + y² = 40, 2y² + 8y − 24 = 0 (1); y² + 4y − 12 = 0, (y + 6)(y − 2) = 0 (1); y = 2, x = 6 (1); y = −6, x = −2 (1).
8. x² + 2x + 2 = kx − 2 gives x² + (2 − k)x + 4 = 0 (1); no meeting: (2 − k)² − 16 < 0 (1); (2 − k)² < 16, so −4 < 2 − k < 4 (1); critical values k = −2, 6 (1); −2 < k < 6 (1).
9. (3x − 4)(x + 2) (1); critical values −2, 4/3 (1); {x : −2 < x < 4/3} (1).
10. Multiply by (x + 1)²: (x − 2)(x + 1) < (x + 1)² (1); (x + 1)[(x − 2) − (x + 1)] < 0, so −3(x + 1) < 0 (1); x + 1 > 0 (1); x > −1 (1).
11. Parabola roots x = −3 and x = 2, maximum at (−1/2, 25/4) (1); solid curve and solid axis (1); region between the curve and the x-axis for −3 ≤ x ≤ 2 shaded correctly (1); (−3, 0) and (2, 0) (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/maths/04-functions-and-graphs.md -->

## Functions and graphs

This file covers sketching curves, proportion, solving equations with graphs, the modulus function, the language of functions (domain and range), composite and inverse functions, transformations of graphs, and using functions as models.

### Overview

AS: sketching polynomials and y = a/x, y = a/x², asymptotes, proportion, solving equations from intersections, and the four single transformations. A-level only: the modulus function and its graph, composite and inverse functions, combinations of transformations, and functions in modelling.

#### Board differences

- AQA B7, B8, B9, B11 (and OT1.4 for the definition of a function); Edexcel 2.7, 2.8, 2.9, 2.11.
- OCR H240: 1.02l to 1.02x and 1.02z. 1.02l (the modulus of a number) is listed separately from 1.02s (the graph of |ax + b|).
- OCR MEI H640: graphs and sketching are MC1 to MC8; functions, modulus and modelling are Mf3 to Mf8; proportion is Ma14. MEI's MC5 asks you to use stationary points when sketching, which links to file 12.

### B7 Graphs of functions

*Refs: AQA B7 · Edexcel 2.7 · OCR 1.02l, 1.02m, 1.02n, 1.02o, 1.02p, 1.02q, 1.02r, 1.02s, 1.02t · MEI MC1, MC2, MC3, MC4, MC5, MC6, Ma14, Mf6, Mf7. Level: AS & A (part A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- Sketch polynomial curves from their factorised form, showing where they meet the axes.
- Sketch y = a/x and y = a/x², with their vertical and horizontal asymptotes.
- Interpret the solution of an equation as the x-coordinates of intersection points, and use intersections to solve equations.
- Understand proportional relationships (y ∝ x, y ∝ x², y ∝ 1/x and so on) and their graphs.
- (A-level only) The modulus |x|, the graph of y = |ax + b|, and solving simple modulus equations and inequalities.

#### Explained

**Polynomials.** For y = a(x − p)(x − q)(x − r):

- The ends: for a cubic with a > 0, y → −∞ as x → −∞ and y → +∞ as x → +∞. For a < 0 it is the other way round. A quartic with positive leading coefficient goes up at both ends.
- A single factor (x − p) means the curve crosses the axis at x = p.
- A squared factor (x − p)² means it touches the axis there (a turning point on the axis).
- A cubed factor (x − p)³ means it crosses with a flat point of inflection.
- Put x = 0 to find the y-intercept.

**Reciprocal graphs.**

| Curve | Shape | Asymptotes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| y = a/x, a > 0 | two branches, in quadrants 1 and 3 | x = 0 and y = 0 |
| y = a/x, a < 0 | quadrants 2 and 4 | x = 0 and y = 0 |
| y = a/x², a > 0 | two branches, both above the x-axis | x = 0 and y = 0 |

An asymptote is a line the curve approaches but never meets as x or y tends to infinity.

**Intersections.** The solutions of f(x) = g(x) are the x-coordinates where y = f(x) and y = g(x) meet. A sketch tells you how many real solutions there are even when you cannot solve the equation exactly.

**Proportion.**

```latex
y \propto x^n \iff y = kx^n \text{ for some constant } k
```

y ∝ x gives a straight line through the origin. y ∝ 1/x gives a reciprocal curve. To find k, substitute a known pair of values.

**(A-level only) Modulus.** |x| is the size of x, ignoring the sign: |x| = x if x ≥ 0 and |x| = −x if x < 0. To sketch y = |ax + b|, sketch y = ax + b and reflect the part below the x-axis in the x-axis. The result is a V shape with its vertex on the x-axis at x = −b/a.

To solve |f(x)| = g(x), solve f(x) = g(x) and −f(x) = g(x), then check each answer in the original equation (g(x) must not be negative). A sketch shows how many solutions to expect. For inequalities, sketch both sides and read off where one is above the other, or square both sides when both sides are moduli.

#### Worked example

Sketch y = (x − 1)²(x + 2).

1. Cubic with positive leading coefficient: low on the left, high on the right.
2. Crosses at x = −2; touches at x = 1.
3. y-intercept: (1)(2) = 2.
4. The curve rises from bottom left, crosses at (−2, 0), reaches a maximum between −2 and 1, comes down to touch the axis at (1, 0) (a minimum), then rises.

Sketch y = −2/x² and state its asymptotes.

1. Always negative, so both branches are below the x-axis.
2. Symmetrical about the y-axis.
3. Asymptotes x = 0 and y = 0.

By sketching y = x³ and y = 4 − x, show that x³ + x − 4 = 0 has exactly one real root.

1. y = x³ is increasing everywhere; y = 4 − x is decreasing everywhere.
2. An increasing and a decreasing graph can meet at most once, and they do meet (at x = 1, x³ = 1 < 3 = 4 − x; at x = 2, x³ = 8 > 2), so there is exactly one intersection.
3. So x³ = 4 − x, which rearranges to x³ + x − 4 = 0, has exactly one real root, between 1 and 2.

The time T for a pendulum swing is proportional to the square root of its length L. T = 2 s when L = 1 m. Find T when L = 2.25 m.

1. T = k√L. 2 = k × 1, so k = 2.
2. T = 2√2.25 = 2 × 1.5 = 3 s.

(A-level only) Solve |2x − 3| = 5.

1. 2x − 3 = 5 gives x = 4.
2. 2x − 3 = −5 gives x = −1.

(A-level only) Solve |x − 1| = 2x + 3.

1. x − 1 = 2x + 3 gives x = −4. Check: 2x + 3 = −5, which is negative, so reject.
2. −(x − 1) = 2x + 3 gives −x + 1 = 2x + 3, so x = −2/3. Check: |−5/3| = 5/3 and 2(−2/3) + 3 = 5/3 ✓.
3. One solution, x = −2/3.

(A-level only) Solve |2x + 1| > |x − 1|.

1. Both sides are non-negative, so square: 4x² + 4x + 1 > x² − 2x + 1.
2. 3x² + 6x > 0, so 3x(x + 2) > 0.
3. x < −2 or x > 0.

#### Common mistakes

- Drawing a squared factor as a crossing point.
- Letting a curve cross its asymptote or bend away from it.
- (A-level only) Not checking modulus solutions, so keeping ones where the right-hand side is negative.
- (A-level only) Squaring both sides when one side could be negative.

### B8 Composite and inverse functions

*Refs: AQA B8, OT1.4 · Edexcel 2.8 · OCR 1.02u, 1.02v · MEI Mf3, Mf4, Mf5. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) A function maps each input in its domain to exactly one output. The range is the set of outputs.
- (A-level only) One-one functions (each output from one input) and many-one functions (some outputs from more than one input).
- (A-level only) Composite functions: fg(x) means f(g(x)): apply g first, then f.
- (A-level only) Inverse functions: f⁻¹ undoes f. Only one-one functions have inverses. The graph of y = f⁻¹(x) is the reflection of y = f(x) in the line y = x. The domain of f⁻¹ is the range of f, and the range of f⁻¹ is the domain of f.

#### Explained

**Domain and range.** Domains are usually given with the function, for example f(x) = √(x − 2), x ≥ 2. The range follows from the graph. For a quadratic, complete the square to find the minimum or maximum value.

**Composite functions.** Work from the inside out. For fg(x) to exist, every output of g must be in the domain of f.

**Inverse functions.** To find f⁻¹(x):

1. Write y = f(x).
2. Rearrange to make x the subject.
3. Swap x and y (or write f⁻¹(x) in terms of x).
4. State the domain of f⁻¹ (the range of f).

A many-one function such as x² on all real x has no inverse. Restricting the domain (for example to x ≥ 0) makes it one-one.

Note: ff⁻¹(x) = f⁻¹f(x) = x. The points where y = f(x) meets y = f⁻¹(x) lie on y = x when f is increasing.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) f(x) = 2x + 3 and g(x) = x², both for all real x. Find fg(x), gf(x) and solve fg(x) = 11.

1. fg(x) = f(x²) = 2x² + 3.
2. gf(x) = g(2x + 3) = (2x + 3)².
3. 2x² + 3 = 11, so x² = 4, x = ±2.

(A-level only) f(x) = x² − 4x + 1, x ≥ 2. Find the range of f, and find f⁻¹(x), stating its domain.

1. f(x) = (x − 2)² − 3. For x ≥ 2 the minimum is −3, so the range is f(x) ≥ −3.
2. y = (x − 2)² − 3, so (x − 2)² = y + 3, and x − 2 = +√(y + 3) (positive root because x ≥ 2).
3. f⁻¹(x) = 2 + √(x + 3), with domain x ≥ −3 (and range f⁻¹(x) ≥ 2).

(A-level only) f(x) = 3/(x − 1), x > 1. Find f⁻¹(x) and state its domain.

1. y = 3/(x − 1), so x − 1 = 3/y, so x = 1 + 3/y.
2. f⁻¹(x) = 1 + 3/x.
3. For x > 1, f(x) takes every positive value, so the domain of f⁻¹ is x > 0.

(A-level only) Explain why g(x) = x² + 1, x ∈ ℝ, has no inverse.

1. g(2) = g(−2) = 5, so g is many-one.
2. Only one-one functions have inverses.

#### Common mistakes

- Applying f first when working out fg(x). The function next to x, g, acts first.
- Writing f⁻¹(x) as 1/f(x). The inverse function is not the reciprocal.
- Forgetting the domain of the inverse, or giving the domain of f instead.
- Taking ± when the restricted domain picks one sign.

### B9 Transformations of graphs

*Refs: AQA B9 · Edexcel 2.9 · OCR 1.02w, 1.02x · MEI MC7, MC8. Level: AS & A (part A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- The effect of y = f(x) + a, y = f(x + a), y = af(x) and y = f(ax) on the graph of y = f(x).
- (A-level only) Combinations of these transformations.

#### Explained

| Equation | Transformation | Point (x, y) moves to |
| --- | --- | --- |
| y = f(x) + a | translation a up, vector (0, a) | (x, y + a) |
| y = f(x + a) | translation a to the left, vector (−a, 0) | (x − a, y) |
| y = af(x) | stretch parallel to the y-axis, scale factor a | (x, ay) |
| y = f(ax) | stretch parallel to the x-axis, scale factor 1/a | (x/a, y) |
| y = −f(x) | reflection in the x-axis | (x, −y) |
| y = f(−x) | reflection in the y-axis | (−x, y) |

The rule to remember: changes inside the bracket act on x and do the opposite of what they look like; changes outside act on y and do what they look like.

Asymptotes move with the graph. A horizontal translation moves vertical asymptotes; a vertical translation moves horizontal asymptotes.

**(A-level only) Combinations.** For y = af(bx + c) + d, a safe order is: translate horizontally by −c, then stretch horizontally by 1/b, then stretch vertically by a, then translate vertically by d. Easier still: track a few key points, applying "inside" changes to x and "outside" changes to y.

#### Worked example

The curve y = f(x) has a maximum at (2, 8) and passes through (4, 6). Find the images of these points under each transformation.

1. y = f(x − 3): (5, 8) and (7, 6).
2. y = 2f(x): (2, 16) and (4, 12).
3. y = f(2x): (1, 8) and (2, 6).
4. y = −f(x): (2, −8), now a minimum, and (4, −6).

Describe the transformation that maps y = 1/x to y = 1/x + 2, and state the asymptotes of the new curve.

1. Translation by vector (0, 2).
2. Asymptotes x = 0 and y = 2.

(A-level only) For the same f, find the images of (2, 8) and (4, 6) under y = f(2x) − 3.

1. Inside: x is halved. Outside: subtract 3 from y.
2. (2, 8) → (1, 5). (4, 6) → (2, 3).

(A-level only) Describe a sequence of transformations mapping y = x² to y = 3(x − 1)² + 4.

1. Translation by vector (1, 0).
2. Stretch parallel to the y-axis, scale factor 3.
3. Translation by vector (0, 4).

#### Common mistakes

- Moving y = f(x + 3) three units to the right. It moves left.
- Using scale factor a for y = f(ax). It is 1/a.
- Forgetting to move the asymptotes.

### B11 Functions in modelling

*Refs: AQA B11 · Edexcel 2.11 · OCR 1.02z · MEI Mf8. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) Use functions to model real situations, choosing a sensible domain and interpreting the range.
- (A-level only) Comment on the limitations of a model and suggest refinements.

#### Explained

A model function usually only makes sense on part of its natural domain. A model for the height of a ball is negative after it lands, so the domain must stop there. A model for the cost of making n items applies only to whole n. Linear, quadratic, exponential, trigonometric and reciprocal functions are all common models. When you criticise a model, say what it predicts that is unrealistic, and why.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) A ball's height is modelled by h(t) = 20t − 5t², where t is in seconds and h in metres. State a suitable domain and find the range.

1. h = 0 when t = 0 or t = 4. The ball is in the air for 0 ≤ t ≤ 4.
2. h(t) = 20 − 5(t − 2)², so the maximum height is 20 m at t = 2.
3. Range 0 ≤ h ≤ 20.

(A-level only) A company models its weekly profit P (£) from selling a product at price x (£) as P = −40x² + 2400x − 20 000. Find the price that gives the most profit and give one limitation.

1. P = −40(x − 30)² + 16 000.
2. Maximum profit £16 000 at a price of £30.
3. Limitation: it assumes demand depends only on price and that costs stay the same; for prices above £50 (or below £10) the model gives a loss, which may not happen if fixed costs can be cut.

#### Common mistakes

- Giving the natural domain (all real numbers) for a model.
- Criticising a model vaguely ("it is not accurate") without saying what is unrealistic.

### Check questions

1. Sketch y = (x + 1)(x − 2)², showing the coordinates of the points where it meets the axes. [3 marks]
2. Sketch y = 3/x and y = x + 2 on the same axes. Find the coordinates of their points of intersection. [5 marks]
3. y is inversely proportional to x². y = 5 when x = 2. Find y when x = 4. [3 marks]
4. (A-level only) Solve |3x − 1| = x + 5. [4 marks]
5. (A-level only) Solve |x + 1| ≥ 2x. [4 marks]
6. (A-level only) f(x) = x² + 1 and g(x) = 3x − 2, for all real x. (a) Find fg(x) and gf(x). (b) Solve fg(x) = gf(x), giving exact answers. [5 marks]
7. (A-level only) f(x) = (2x + 1)/(x − 3), x ≠ 3. Find f⁻¹(x) and state its domain. [4 marks]
8. (A-level only) h(x) = (x − 1)² + 2, x ≥ 1. (a) State the range of h. (b) Find h⁻¹(x) and its domain. (c) Describe how the graphs of h and h⁻¹ are related. [5 marks]
9. A curve y = f(x) has a maximum at (2, 5) and meets the y-axis at (0, 3). Give the coordinates of the maximum and the y-intercept on: (a) y = f(x − 3); (b) y = 2f(x); (c) y = f(2x). [6 marks]
10. (A-level only) Describe fully a sequence of transformations that maps y = x² onto y = (x + 3)² − 2. Hence state the coordinates of the vertex of y = (x + 3)² − 2. [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Cubic shape with positive leading coefficient (1); crosses at (−1, 0) and touches at (2, 0) (1); y-intercept (0, 4) (1).
2. Correct reciprocal curve and line (1); 3/x = x + 2 gives x² + 2x − 3 = 0 (1); (x + 3)(x − 1) = 0 (1); x = 1, y = 3 (1); x = −3, y = −1 (1).
3. y = k/x² (1); k = 20 (1); y = 20/16 = 1.25 (1).
4. 3x − 1 = x + 5 gives x = 3 (1); −(3x − 1) = x + 5 gives x = −1 (1); both checked: right-hand sides 8 and 4 are positive (1); x = 3 or x = −1 (1).
5. For x ≥ −1: x + 1 ≥ 2x gives x ≤ 1 (1); so −1 ≤ x ≤ 1 (1); for x < −1: −x − 1 ≥ 2x gives x ≤ −1/3, so all x < −1 work (1); combined: x ≤ 1 (1).
6. (a) fg(x) = (3x − 2)² + 1 = 9x² − 12x + 5 (1); gf(x) = 3x² + 1 (1). (b) 6x² − 12x + 4 = 0, so 3x² − 6x + 2 = 0 (1); formula (1); x = 1 ± √3/3 (1).
7. y(x − 3) = 2x + 1 (1); x(y − 2) = 3y + 1 (1); f⁻¹(x) = (3x + 1)/(x − 2) (1); domain x ≠ 2 (1).
8. (a) h(x) ≥ 2 (1). (b) (x − 1)² = y − 2, x = 1 + √(y − 2) (1); h⁻¹(x) = 1 + √(x − 2) (1); domain x ≥ 2 (1). (c) Reflections of each other in y = x (1).
9. (a) (5, 5) and (3, 3) (2). (b) (2, 10) and (0, 6) (2). (c) (1, 5) and (0, 3) (2).
10. Translation by vector (−3, −2), or by (−3, 0) then (0, −2) (2); vertex (−3, −2) (1).
