## Algebra: polynomials, rational expressions and partial fractions

This file covers expanding and factorising polynomials, dividing a polynomial by a linear expression, the factor theorem, simplifying algebraic fractions, and splitting a fraction into partial fractions.

### Overview

AS: expanding, collecting terms, factorising, simple algebraic division and the factor theorem. A-level only: simplifying rational expressions (algebraic fractions) and partial fractions. Partial fractions are used again in integration (file 15) and in the binomial expansion (file 07).

#### Board differences

- AQA B6 and B10; Edexcel 2.6 and 2.10. Edexcel's guidance limits division to dividing by (ax + b) or (ax − b).
- OCR H240: 1.02j (AS), 1.02k and 1.02y (A-level only).
- OCR MEI H640: Mf1 and Mf2 (AS); Ma15 and Ma16 (A-level only).
- All four boards limit partial fractions to denominators with linear factors, including a squared linear factor, and at most three partial fractions.

### B6 Polynomials and the factor theorem

*Refs: AQA B6 · Edexcel 2.6 · OCR 1.02j, 1.02k · MEI Mf1, Mf2, Ma16. Level: AS & A (part A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- Expand brackets, collect like terms, and factorise polynomials.
- Divide a polynomial by a linear expression (x ± a or ax ± b).
- The factor theorem: (x − a) is a factor of f(x) if and only if f(a) = 0. More generally, (ax − b) is a factor if and only if f(b/a) = 0.
- (A-level only) Simplify rational expressions by factorising and cancelling, and by algebraic division by linear expressions.

#### Explained

**Factor theorem.** If f(a) = 0, then x = a is a root, so (x − a) is a factor. To factorise a cubic:

1. Try small values (±1, ±2, ±3, and fractions from factors of the constant over factors of the leading coefficient) until f(a) = 0.
2. Divide by (x − a) to get a quadratic.
3. Factorise the quadratic.

**Division.** Three equivalent methods: long division, comparing coefficients, or the grid (box) method. Comparing coefficients is quick: write f(x) = (x − a)(px² + qx + r) + R and match the powers of x.

The result can be written as

```latex
f(x) = (\text{divisor}) \times (\text{quotient}) + \text{remainder}
```

**Sketching cubics.** Once fully factorised, a cubic's roots and y-intercept give its sketch (see file 04).

**(A-level only) Rational expressions.** A rational expression is one polynomial divided by another. Factorise top and bottom fully, then cancel common factors (whole brackets, never single terms). To add or subtract, use a common denominator. To divide, multiply by the reciprocal.

#### Worked example

Show that (x − 3) is a factor of f(x) = 2x³ − 3x² − 11x + 6 and factorise f(x) fully.

1. f(3) = 54 − 27 − 33 + 6 = 0, so (x − 3) is a factor.
2. Compare coefficients: 2x³ − 3x² − 11x + 6 = (x − 3)(2x² + bx − 2). The x² terms give −3 = b − 6, so b = 3.
3. Check the x terms: (x − 3)(2x² + 3x − 2) gives −2x − 9x = −11x ✓.
4. 2x² + 3x − 2 = (2x − 1)(x + 2).
5. f(x) = (x − 3)(2x − 1)(x + 2).

Factorise g(x) = 2x³ + 3x² − 8x + 3 fully.

1. Constant 3, leading coefficient 2, so try ±1, ±3, ±1/2, ±3/2.
2. g(1) = 2 + 3 − 8 + 3 = 0, so (x − 1) is a factor.
3. Divide: 2x³ + 3x² − 8x + 3 = (x − 1)(2x² + 5x − 3).
4. 2x² + 5x − 3 = (2x − 1)(x + 3).
5. g(x) = (x − 1)(2x − 1)(x + 3).

Divide x³ − 2x² + 4 by (x − 3).

1. Long division: x³ ÷ x = x²; x²(x − 3) = x³ − 3x²; subtract to get x² + 0x.
2. x² ÷ x = x; x(x − 3) = x² − 3x; subtract to get 3x + 4.
3. 3x ÷ x = 3; 3(x − 3) = 3x − 9; subtract to get 13.
4. x³ − 2x² + 4 = (x − 3)(x² + x + 3) + 13.

f(x) = x³ + ax² + bx − 6. (x − 1) is a factor and f(−1) = 4. Find a and b.

1. f(1) = 0: 1 + a + b − 6 = 0, so a + b = 5.
2. f(−1) = 4: −1 + a − b − 6 = 4, so a − b = 11.
3. Adding: 2a = 16, a = 8, b = −3.

(A-level only) Simplify (x² − 9)/(2x² + 5x − 3).

1. Top: (x − 3)(x + 3). Bottom: (2x − 1)(x + 3).
2. Cancel (x + 3): (x − 3)/(2x − 1).

(A-level only) Write as a single fraction: 3/(x + 2) − 1/(x − 1).

1. Common denominator (x + 2)(x − 1).
2. [3(x − 1) − (x + 2)] / [(x + 2)(x − 1)] = (2x − 5)/[(x + 2)(x − 1)].

(A-level only) Simplify (x³ + 2x² − 5x − 6)/(x + 1).

1. f(−1) = −1 + 2 + 5 − 6 = 0, so the division is exact.
2. x³ + 2x² − 5x − 6 = (x + 1)(x² + x − 6).
3. Answer: x² + x − 6, or (x + 3)(x − 2).

#### Common mistakes

- Using f(3) to test for the factor (x + 3). The factor (x + 3) needs f(−3) = 0.
- Cancelling terms rather than factors: (x² + 3)/(x + 3) does not simplify to x + 1.
- Forgetting a zero placeholder for a missing power in long division (x³ − 2x² + 4 has no x term).

### B10 Partial fractions

*Refs: AQA B10 · Edexcel 2.10 · OCR 1.02y · MEI Ma15. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) Split a proper rational function into partial fractions where the denominator has linear factors, possibly one squared linear factor, with at most three terms and numerators that are constants.

The forms are:

```latex
\frac{px+q}{(ax+b)(cx+d)} \equiv \frac{A}{ax+b} + \frac{B}{cx+d}
```

```latex
\frac{px^2+qx+r}{(ax+b)(cx+d)(ex+f)} \equiv \frac{A}{ax+b} + \frac{B}{cx+d} + \frac{C}{ex+f}
```

```latex
\frac{px^2+qx+r}{(ax+b)(cx+d)^2} \equiv \frac{A}{ax+b} + \frac{B}{cx+d} + \frac{C}{(cx+d)^2}
```

#### Explained

Partial fractions undo "adding fractions". The method:

1. Write the identity with unknown constants A, B, C.
2. Multiply through by the full denominator to get an identity between polynomials.
3. Substitute values of x that make factors zero, to find constants one at a time.
4. For a repeated factor, one constant will not be found this way; compare coefficients (often of x² or of the constant term) instead.
5. Check by substituting a convenient value such as x = 0.

This is an identity: it is true for every x, which is why you can substitute any value you like.

#### Worked example

Express (5x + 1)/[(x − 1)(x + 2)] in partial fractions.

1. 5x + 1 ≡ A(x + 2) + B(x − 1).
2. x = 1: 6 = 3A, so A = 2.
3. x = −2: −9 = −3B, so B = 3.
4. Answer: 2/(x − 1) + 3/(x + 2).

Express (2x² + 9x − 3)/[x(x − 1)(x + 3)] in partial fractions.

1. 2x² + 9x − 3 ≡ A(x − 1)(x + 3) + Bx(x + 3) + Cx(x − 1).
2. x = 0: −3 = −3A, so A = 1.
3. x = 1: 8 = 4B, so B = 2.
4. x = −3: 18 − 27 − 3 = −12 = 12C, so C = −1.
5. Answer: 1/x + 2/(x − 1) − 1/(x + 3).

Express (2x² + 2)/[(x + 1)(x − 1)²] in partial fractions.

1. 2x² + 2 ≡ A(x − 1)² + B(x + 1)(x − 1) + C(x + 1).
2. x = 1: 4 = 2C, so C = 2.
3. x = −1: 4 = 4A, so A = 1.
4. Compare x² coefficients: 2 = A + B, so B = 1.
5. Check x = 0: left side 2; right side A − B + C = 1 − 1 + 2 = 2 ✓.
6. Answer: 1/(x + 1) + 1/(x − 1) + 2/(x − 1)².

Express (x + 4)/[(2x + 1)(x − 1)] in partial fractions.

1. x + 4 ≡ A(x − 1) + B(2x + 1).
2. x = 1: 5 = 3B, so B = 5/3.
3. x = −1/2: 3.5 = −1.5A, so A = −7/3.
4. Answer: −7/[3(2x + 1)] + 5/[3(x − 1)].

#### Common mistakes

- Missing the B/(cx + d) term when (cx + d)² is in the denominator. A repeated factor needs both the single and the squared term.
- Arithmetic slips when substituting fractions such as x = −1/2. Check with x = 0 at the end.
- Trying partial fractions on an improper fraction (degree of top ≥ degree of bottom) without dividing first.

### Check questions

1. Use the factor theorem to show that (x + 1) is a factor of x³ + 2x² − 5x − 6, and hence factorise it fully. [4 marks]
2. f(x) = 2x³ + px² − 13x + 6, and (x − 2) is a factor of f(x). (a) Find p. (b) Solve f(x) = 0. [5 marks]
3. Show that (x + 2) is not a factor of x³ − 3x + 5. [2 marks]
4. Find the quotient and remainder when 2x³ + x² − 7x + 1 is divided by (x − 2). [3 marks]
5. (A-level only) Simplify (x² + x − 6)/(x² − 4). [3 marks]
6. (A-level only) Simplify [(2x² − 7x − 4)/(x² − 16)] ÷ [(2x + 1)/(x + 4)]. [3 marks]
7. (A-level only) Express (4x + 5)/[(x + 1)(x + 2)] in partial fractions. [3 marks]
8. (A-level only) Express (7x + 1)/[(x − 1)(x + 1)²] in partial fractions. [4 marks]
9. (A-level only) Express (3x² − 4x − 1)/[x(x − 1)(x + 1)] in partial fractions. [4 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. f(−1) = −1 + 2 + 5 − 6 = 0, so (x + 1) is a factor (1); divide to get x² + x − 6 (1); (x + 3)(x − 2) (1); f(x) = (x + 1)(x + 3)(x − 2) (1).
2. (a) f(2) = 16 + 4p − 26 + 6 = 0 (1); p = 1 (1). (b) 2x³ + x² − 13x + 6 = (x − 2)(2x² + 5x − 3) (1); = (x − 2)(2x − 1)(x + 3) (1); x = 2, 1/2, −3 (1).
3. f(−2) = −8 + 6 + 5 = 3 (1); not zero, so not a factor (1).
4. Division attempted (1); quotient 2x² + 5x + 3 (1); remainder 7 (1). Check: f(2) = 16 + 4 − 14 + 1 = 7.
5. Numerator (x + 3)(x − 2) (1); denominator (x − 2)(x + 2) (1); (x + 3)/(x + 2) (1).
6. 2x² − 7x − 4 = (2x + 1)(x − 4) and x² − 16 = (x − 4)(x + 4) (1); multiply by (x + 4)/(2x + 1) (1); everything cancels to 1 (1).
7. 4x + 5 ≡ A(x + 2) + B(x + 1) (1); A = 1 (1); B = 3, so 1/(x + 1) + 3/(x + 2) (1).
8. 7x + 1 ≡ A(x + 1)² + B(x − 1)(x + 1) + C(x − 1) (1); x = 1 gives A = 2 (1); x = −1 gives C = 3 (1); x² coefficients give B = −2: 2/(x − 1) − 2/(x + 1) + 3/(x + 1)² (1).
9. 3x² − 4x − 1 ≡ A(x − 1)(x + 1) + Bx(x + 1) + Cx(x − 1) (1); x = 0: −1 = −A, A = 1 (1); x = 1: −2 = 2B, B = −1 (1); x = −1: 6 = 2C, C = 3, so 1/x − 1/(x − 1) + 3/(x + 1) (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/maths/12-differentiation-basics-and-applications.md -->

## Differentiation: first principles, powers of x and applications

This file covers what a derivative is, differentiation from first principles, differentiating powers of x, second derivatives, and using derivatives for tangents, normals, stationary points, increasing and decreasing functions, optimisation, concavity and points of inflection. Other functions and the chain, product and quotient rules are in file 13.

### Overview

AS: the derivative as the gradient of a tangent and as a limit, sketching gradient functions, first principles for xⁿ with small positive integer n, differentiating xⁿ for rational n, second derivatives, tangents and normals, stationary points and where a function increases or decreases. A-level only: first principles for sin x and cos x, convex and concave sections, and points of inflection.

#### Board differences

- AQA G1 to G3 (and the xⁿ part of G2); Edexcel 7.1 to 7.3.
- OCR H240: 1.07a to 1.07e, 1.07g, 1.07i, 1.07m to 1.07o (AS); 1.07f, 1.07h and 1.07p (A-level only).
- OCR MEI H640: Mc1 to Mc9 (AS) and Mc17, Mc18 (A-level only). MEI also lists curve sketching using stationary points (MC5, AS) and points of inflection (MC9, A-level). MEI asks you to deduce the units of a rate of change from a graph (in its notes for Mc3).

### G1 The derivative, first principles and the second derivative

*Refs: AQA G1 · Edexcel 7.1 · OCR 1.07a, 1.07b, 1.07c, 1.07d, 1.07e, 1.07f, 1.07g, 1.07h · MEI Mc1, Mc2, Mc3, Mc4, Mc6, Mc11, Mc17. Level: AS & A (part A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- The derivative f′(x), or dy/dx, is the gradient of the tangent to y = f(x) at a general point, and is the rate of change of y with respect to x.
- The gradient of the tangent is the limit of the gradient of a chord as the chord gets shorter:

```latex
f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h}
```

- Differentiate small positive integer powers of x from first principles.
- Sketch the gradient function y = f′(x) from a sketch of y = f(x).
- The second derivative f″(x), or d²y/dx², is the rate of change of the gradient.
- (A-level only) Differentiate sin x and cos x from first principles.
- (A-level only) A curve is convex where f″(x) > 0 (it bends upwards; the gradient increases) and concave where f″(x) < 0.

#### Explained

**Chord to tangent.** Take two points on the curve, (x, f(x)) and (x + h, f(x + h)). The chord between them has gradient [f(x + h) − f(x)]/h. As h → 0 the second point slides towards the first and the chord becomes the tangent.

**Sketching the gradient function.**

- Where y = f(x) has a stationary point, y = f′(x) crosses or touches the x-axis.
- Where f is increasing, f′ is above the x-axis; where f is decreasing, f′ is below.
- Where f is steepest, f′ has a turning point.

**Units.** If y is in metres and x in seconds, dy/dx is in metres per second, and d²y/dx² is in metres per second per second.

**(A-level only) First principles for sin x.** Use sin(x + h) = sin x cos h + cos x sin h and the small angle results: as h → 0, (sin h)/h → 1 and (cos h − 1)/h → 0. (From cos h ≈ 1 − h²/2, (cos h − 1)/h ≈ −h/2, which tends to 0.)

#### Worked example

Differentiate x² from first principles.

1. [(x + h)² − x²]/h = (2xh + h²)/h = 2x + h.
2. As h → 0, this tends to 2x. So the derivative is 2x.

Differentiate x³ from first principles.

1. (x + h)³ = x³ + 3x²h + 3xh² + h³.
2. [(x + h)³ − x³]/h = 3x² + 3xh + h².
3. As h → 0, this tends to 3x².

(A-level only) Prove from first principles that the derivative of sin x is cos x.

1. [sin(x + h) − sin x]/h = [sin x cos h + cos x sin h − sin x]/h.
2. = sin x × (cos h − 1)/h + cos x × (sin h)/h.
3. As h → 0, (cos h − 1)/h → 0 and (sin h)/h → 1.
4. The limit is cos x.

The curve y = f(x) has a maximum at x = −1 and a minimum at x = 2, and f(x) → ∞ as x → ∞. Describe y = f′(x).

1. f′(x) = 0 at x = −1 and x = 2.
2. f increases for x < −1 and x > 2, so f′ is positive there; f′ is negative between −1 and 2.
3. f′ looks like a U-shaped parabola crossing the x-axis at −1 and 2.

#### Common mistakes

- In first principles, letting h = 0 before cancelling h from the denominator.
- Forgetting to write the limit (lim, h → 0) in a first principles proof.

### G2 Differentiating xⁿ

*Refs: AQA G2 · Edexcel 7.2 · OCR 1.07i · MEI Mc5. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

```latex
\frac{d}{dx}\left(x^n\right) = nx^{n-1} \quad \text{for all rational } n
```

- Constant multiples, sums and differences: differentiate term by term. The derivative of a constant is 0.

#### Explained

Before differentiating, write every term as a power of x: roots as fractional powers, and x in a denominator as a negative power. Split a fraction with a single term in its denominator into separate terms. Expand brackets first.

#### Worked example

Differentiate y = 4x³ − 6/x + 2√x.

1. y = 4x³ − 6x⁻¹ + 2x^{1/2}.
2. dy/dx = 12x² + 6x⁻² + x^{−1/2}.

Differentiate y = (x² + 3)/√x.

1. y = x^{3/2} + 3x^{−1/2}.
2. dy/dx = (3/2)x^{1/2} − (3/2)x^{−3/2}.

Find f″(x) for f(x) = x⁴ − 2x^{1/2}.

1. f′(x) = 4x³ − x^{−1/2}.
2. f″(x) = 12x² + (1/2)x^{−3/2}.

#### Common mistakes

- Differentiating a quotient term by term without first splitting it.
- Subtracting 1 from a negative power wrongly: x⁻² becomes −2x⁻³, not −2x⁻¹.

### G3 Applications of differentiation

*Refs: AQA G3 · Edexcel 7.3 · OCR 1.07m, 1.07n, 1.07o, 1.07p · MEI Mc7, Mc8, Mc9, Mc18, MC5, MC9. Level: AS & A (part A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- The tangent at a point has gradient f′(x); the normal is perpendicular to the tangent.
- Stationary points: where dy/dx = 0. Classify them as maxima or minima using the second derivative (negative: maximum; positive: minimum) or the sign of the gradient either side.
- A function is increasing where f′(x) > 0 and decreasing where f′(x) < 0. (Some boards use ≥ and ≤.)
- Solve optimisation problems.
- (A-level only) A point of inflection is where the curve changes between convex and concave, so f″(x) changes sign. If f′(x) = 0 there too, it is a stationary point of inflection.

#### Explained

**Tangent and normal at x = a.** Find y at a, find the gradient m = f′(a), then use y − y₁ = m(x − x₁). The normal has gradient −1/m.

**Stationary points.**

1. Differentiate and solve f′(x) = 0.
2. Find the y-coordinates.
3. Classify. If f″(a) = 0 the second derivative test fails; look at the sign of f′(x) just either side of a.

| Gradient left of a | Gradient right of a | Type |
| --- | --- | --- |
| + | − | maximum |
| − | + | minimum |
| + | + or − then − | stationary point of inflection (same sign both sides) |

**Optimisation.** Write the quantity to be maximised or minimised in terms of one variable (use a constraint to remove the other), differentiate, set to zero, solve, and show it is a maximum or minimum.

**(A-level only) Inflection.** Solve f″(x) = 0, then check f″ changes sign. f″ = 0 alone is not enough: y = x⁴ has f″(0) = 0 but is convex on both sides.

#### Worked example

Find the tangent and normal to y = x³ − 3x + 1 at x = 2.

1. y = 8 − 6 + 1 = 3.
2. dy/dx = 3x² − 3 = 9 at x = 2.
3. Tangent: y − 3 = 9(x − 2), so y = 9x − 15.
4. Normal: y − 3 = −(1/9)(x − 2), so x + 9y − 29 = 0.

Find and classify the stationary points of y = x³ − 6x² + 9x + 1, and state where the function is decreasing.

1. dy/dx = 3x² − 12x + 9 = 3(x − 1)(x − 3) = 0, so x = 1 or x = 3.
2. x = 1: y = 5. x = 3: y = 1.
3. d²y/dx² = 6x − 12: −6 at x = 1 (maximum (1, 5)); +6 at x = 3 (minimum (3, 1)).
4. Decreasing where 3(x − 1)(x − 3) < 0: 1 < x < 3.

An open box is made from a 12 cm by 12 cm square of card by cutting squares of side x cm from each corner and folding. Find the maximum volume.

1. V = x(12 − 2x)² = 4x³ − 48x² + 144x, for 0 < x < 6.
2. dV/dx = 12x² − 96x + 144 = 12(x − 2)(x − 6) = 0. In range, x = 2.
3. d²V/dx² = 24x − 96 = −48 at x = 2, so a maximum.
4. V = 2 × 8² = 128 cm³.

(A-level only) Find the stationary points and points of inflection of y = x⁴ − 4x³.

1. dy/dx = 4x³ − 12x² = 4x²(x − 3) = 0 at x = 0 and x = 3.
2. d²y/dx² = 12x² − 24x = 12x(x − 2).
3. At x = 3: d²y/dx² = 36 > 0, minimum (3, −27).
4. At x = 0: d²y/dx² = 0. dy/dx is negative just either side of 0 (4x² ≥ 0 and x − 3 < 0), so (0, 0) is a stationary point of inflection.
5. d²y/dx² also changes sign at x = 2, so (2, −16) is a non-stationary point of inflection.
6. Convex for x < 0 and x > 2; concave for 0 < x < 2.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the gradient of the tangent for the normal.
- Concluding "point of inflection" just because f″ = 0.
- Not checking the answer to an optimisation question is a maximum (or minimum), or not checking it is in the allowed range.

### Check questions

1. Differentiate 3x² from first principles. [3 marks]
2. Find dy/dx for y = 5x⁴ − 3/x² + 4∛x. [3 marks]
3. Find dy/dx for y = (2x − 1)²/x. [3 marks]
4. The curve y = x² − 5x + 4 passes through P(4, 0). (a) Find the equation of the normal at P. (b) Find the other point where the normal meets the curve. [6 marks]
5. Find and classify the stationary points of y = 2x³ + 3x² − 12x + 7. [6 marks]
6. Find the set of values of x for which f(x) = x³ − 3x² − 9x is increasing. [3 marks]
7. A closed cylinder has volume 250π cm³. Show that its surface area is A = 2πr² + 500π/r, and find the minimum surface area, justifying that it is a minimum. [7 marks]
8. (A-level only) Find the intervals on which y = x⁴ − 6x² is concave, and the coordinates of its points of inflection. [5 marks]
9. (A-level only) Prove from first principles that the derivative of cos x is −sin x. [4 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. [3(x + h)² − 3x²]/h (1); = 6x + 3h (1); limit as h → 0 is 6x (1).
2. 20x³ (1); + 6x⁻³ (1); + (4/3)x^{−2/3} (1).
3. y = 4x − 4 + x⁻¹ (1); dy/dx = 4 − x⁻² (2).
4. (a) dy/dx = 2x − 5 = 3 at x = 4 (1); normal gradient −1/3 (1); x + 3y − 4 = 0 (1). (b) x² − 5x + 4 = (4 − x)/3, so 3x² − 14x + 8 = 0 (1); (3x − 2)(x − 4) = 0 (1); (2/3, 10/9) (1).
5. dy/dx = 6x² + 6x − 12 = 6(x + 2)(x − 1) (1); x = −2 and x = 1 (1); y = 27 and y = 0 (1); d²y/dx² = 12x + 6 (1); at x = −2 it is −18, maximum (−2, 27) (1); at x = 1 it is 18, minimum (1, 0) (1).
6. f′(x) = 3x² − 6x − 9 = 3(x − 3)(x + 1) (1); f′(x) > 0 (1); x < −1 or x > 3 (1).
7. πr²h = 250π, so h = 250/r² (1); A = 2πr² + 2πrh = 2πr² + 500π/r (1); dA/dr = 4πr − 500π/r² (1); = 0 gives r³ = 125, r = 5 (1); d²A/dr² = 4π + 1000π/r³ > 0, minimum (1); h = 10 (1); A = 150π cm² (1).
8. y″ = 12x² − 12 (1); y″ < 0 for −1 < x < 1, so concave there (1); y″ changes sign at x = ±1 (1); (1, −5) (1); (−1, −5) (1).
9. [cos(x + h) − cos x]/h = [cos x cos h − sin x sin h − cos x]/h (1); = cos x (cos h − 1)/h − sin x (sin h)/h (1); limits 0 and 1 as h → 0 (1); derivative −sin x (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/maths/05-coordinate-geometry-lines-and-circles.md -->

## Coordinate geometry: straight lines and circles

This file covers the equation of a straight line in its different forms, parallel and perpendicular lines, straight line models, the equation of a circle, and the circle properties used to find tangents, chords and centres.

### Overview

All AS content. Parametric equations, the other part of coordinate geometry, are in file 06.

#### Board differences

- AQA C1, C2; Edexcel 3.1, 3.2.
- OCR H240: 1.03a to 1.03f.
- OCR MEI H640 lists the basic skills separately: gradient (Mg1), distance (Mg2), midpoint (Mg3), and intersections of a line with a curve (Mg8) and with a circle (Mg9).

### C1 Straight lines

*Refs: AQA C1 · Edexcel 3.1 · OCR 1.03a, 1.03b, 1.03c · MEI Mg1, Mg2, Mg3, Mg4, Mg5, Mg6, Mg7. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The gradient between two points, the distance between them, and the midpoint.
- The forms y = mx + c, y − y₁ = m(x − x₁) and ax + by + c = 0.
- Parallel lines have equal gradients. Perpendicular lines have gradients whose product is −1.
- Find where two lines meet by solving simultaneously.
- Use straight lines as models.

```latex
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} \qquad d = \sqrt{(x_2-x_1)^2 + (y_2-y_1)^2} \qquad M = \left(\frac{x_1+x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1+y_2}{2}\right)
```

```latex
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1) \qquad m_1 m_2 = -1 \text{ for perpendicular lines}
```

#### Explained

The form y − y₁ = m(x − x₁) is the quickest way to write a line when you know its gradient and one point. Questions often ask for the answer as ax + by + c = 0 with a, b, c integers: multiply out fractions and collect every term on one side.

The perpendicular gradient is the negative reciprocal: if m = 2/3, the perpendicular gradient is −3/2.

The perpendicular bisector of AB passes through the midpoint of AB and is perpendicular to AB. Every point on it is the same distance from A as from B.

In a straight line model y = mx + c, the gradient is the rate of change (for example, cost per unit) and the intercept is the value when x = 0 (for example, a fixed charge).

#### Worked example

Find the equation of the line through (2, −1) and (6, 7), in the form ax + by + c = 0.

1. m = (7 − (−1))/(6 − 2) = 8/4 = 2.
2. y + 1 = 2(x − 2), so y = 2x − 5.
3. 2x − y − 5 = 0.

Find the perpendicular bisector of A(1, 3) and B(5, −1).

1. Midpoint (3, 1).
2. Gradient of AB = −4/4 = −1, so the perpendicular gradient is 1.
3. y − 1 = 1(x − 3), so y = x − 2.

Find the point where 2x + 3y = 12 meets y = x − 1.

1. 2x + 3(x − 1) = 12, so 5x = 15, x = 3.
2. y = 2. The point is (3, 2).

Show that the points P(0, 1), Q(4, 3) and R(2, 7) form a right-angled triangle.

1. Gradient PQ = 2/4 = 1/2. Gradient QR = 4/(−2) = −2.
2. (1/2)(−2) = −1, so PQ is perpendicular to QR. The right angle is at Q.

A plumber charges £40 for a 1-hour job and £100 for a 3-hour job. Model the charge C as a linear function of the time t hours and interpret the constants.

1. Gradient = (100 − 40)/(3 − 1) = 30. C − 40 = 30(t − 1), so C = 30t + 10.
2. £30 per hour, plus a £10 fixed call-out charge.
3. The model may not hold for very short jobs, where a minimum charge is likely.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the perpendicular gradient as −m instead of −1/m.
- Leaving fractions in "ax + by + c = 0 with integer coefficients".
- Subtracting coordinates in a different order on top and bottom of the gradient formula.

### C2 Circles

*Refs: AQA C2 · Edexcel 3.2 · OCR 1.03d, 1.03e, 1.03f · MEI Mg8, Mg9, Mg10, Mg11. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The circle with centre (a, b) and radius r has equation

```latex
(x-a)^2 + (y-b)^2 = r^2
```

- Complete the square to find the centre and radius from the expanded form x² + y² + 2fx + 2gy + c = 0.
- Use these properties:
  - the angle in a semicircle is a right angle;
  - the perpendicular from the centre to a chord bisects the chord;
  - the radius at a point is perpendicular to the tangent at that point.
- Find where a line meets a circle; use the discriminant to decide whether a line is a tangent, cuts the circle twice, or misses it.

#### Explained

**Centre and radius.** Group x terms and y terms and complete the square on each. If the number on the right-hand side is not positive, the equation is not a circle.

**Tangent at a point.** Find the gradient of the radius to the point; the tangent's gradient is its negative reciprocal; use y − y₁ = m(x − x₁).

**Finding the centre from three points.** The perpendicular bisector of any chord passes through the centre. Find the perpendicular bisectors of two chords and solve them simultaneously.

**Diameters.** If AB is a diameter, the centre is the midpoint of AB and the radius is half of AB. If angle ACB = 90°, then AB is a diameter of the circle through A, B and C.

**Line and circle.** Substitute the line into the circle to get a quadratic. Two roots: the line cuts the circle (a chord). One repeated root: tangent. No real roots: no intersection.

#### Worked example

Find the centre and radius of x² + y² − 6x + 4y − 12 = 0.

1. (x − 3)² − 9 + (y + 2)² − 4 − 12 = 0.
2. (x − 3)² + (y + 2)² = 25.
3. Centre (3, −2), radius 5.

Find the equation of the tangent to this circle at (6, 2).

1. Check: (6 − 3)² + (2 + 2)² = 9 + 16 = 25 ✓.
2. Radius gradient: (2 − (−2))/(6 − 3) = 4/3. Tangent gradient −3/4.
3. y − 2 = −(3/4)(x − 6), so 4y − 8 = −3x + 18, so 3x + 4y − 26 = 0.

A circle has diameter with ends (−1, 4) and (5, −4). Find its equation.

1. Centre = midpoint = (2, 0).
2. Radius = half of √(6² + 8²) = 10/2 = 5.
3. (x − 2)² + y² = 25.

Find where the line y = x + 1 meets the circle x² + y² = 13.

1. x² + (x + 1)² = 13, so 2x² + 2x − 12 = 0, so x² + x − 6 = 0.
2. (x + 3)(x − 2) = 0. x = 2, y = 3 or x = −3, y = −2.
3. Points (2, 3) and (−3, −2).

Find the values of k for which y = kx + 5 is a tangent to x² + y² = 5.

1. x² + (kx + 5)² = 5, so (1 + k²)x² + 10kx + 20 = 0.
2. Tangent: discriminant = 0. 100k² − 80(1 + k²) = 0.
3. 20k² = 80, so k = ±2.

The points A(0, 0), B(8, 0) and C(0, 6) lie on a circle. Find its equation.

1. Angle BAC is 90° (the axes are perpendicular), so BC is a diameter.
2. Centre = midpoint of BC = (4, 3). Radius = half of √(64 + 36) = 5.
3. (x − 4)² + (y − 3)² = 25.

A circle has centre (0, 0) and radius 10. A chord is 6 units from the centre. Find its length.

1. The perpendicular from the centre bisects the chord, forming a right-angled triangle with hypotenuse 10 and one side 6.
2. Half-chord = √(100 − 36) = 8.
3. Chord length 16.

#### Common mistakes

- Sign errors: (x + 3)² + (y − 1)² = 16 has centre (−3, 1).
- Giving r² as the radius.
- Using the gradient of the radius as the tangent gradient.

### Check questions

1. Find the equation of the line through (3, −2) perpendicular to 2x − 5y + 1 = 0. Give it in the form ax + by + c = 0 with integer coefficients. [4 marks]
2. A is (−2, 1) and B is (4, 9). (a) Find the length of AB. (b) Find the equation of the perpendicular bisector of AB. [5 marks]
3. A circle has equation x² + y² + 8x − 2y − 8 = 0. Find its centre and radius. [3 marks]
4. The point P(−1, 5) lies on the circle in question 3. Find the equation of the tangent at P. [4 marks]
5. Find the values of k for which the line y = x + k is a tangent to x² + y² = 8. [4 marks]
6. A(1, 2) and B(7, 10) are the ends of a diameter of a circle. (a) Find the equation of the circle. (b) Show that C(0, 9) lies on the circle, and explain why angle ACB is 90°. [5 marks]
7. Hiring a hall costs £120 for 30 guests and £170 for 50 guests. Assuming a linear model, find the cost formula, interpret its constants, and give one limitation. [4 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Gradient of given line 2/5 (1); perpendicular gradient −5/2 (1); y + 2 = −(5/2)(x − 3) (1); 5x + 2y − 11 = 0 (1).
2. (a) √(6² + 8²) (1) = 10 (1). (b) Midpoint (1, 5) (1); gradient AB = 4/3, perpendicular −3/4 (1); 3x + 4y − 23 = 0 (1).
3. (x + 4)² + (y − 1)² = 25 (1); centre (−4, 1) (1); radius 5 (1).
4. Gradient of radius (5 − 1)/(−1 + 4) = 4/3 (1); tangent gradient −3/4 (1); y − 5 = −(3/4)(x + 1) (1); 3x + 4y − 17 = 0 (1).
5. x² + (x + k)² = 8, so 2x² + 2kx + k² − 8 = 0 (1); discriminant 4k² − 8(k² − 8) = 0 (1); 64 − 4k² = 0 (1); k = ±4 (1).
6. (a) Centre (4, 6) (1); radius 5 (1); (x − 4)² + (y − 6)² = 25 (1). (b) (0 − 4)² + (9 − 6)² = 16 + 9 = 25 (1); AB is a diameter, and the angle in a semicircle is 90° (1).
7. Gradient 50/20 = 2.5 (1); C = 2.5n + 45 (1); £45 fixed charge and £2.50 per guest (1); limitation, e.g. a hall has a maximum capacity, or there may be bulk discounts (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/maths/06-parametric-equations.md -->

## Parametric equations

This file covers curves given by a pair of equations x = f(t), y = g(t), converting them to Cartesian form, finding points and intersections, and using parametric equations as models. Differentiating parametric equations is in file 13.

### Overview

All A-level only. Parametric equations reappear in differentiation (file 13), in kinematics where position is given as a function of time (file 25), and in projectiles (file 26).

#### Board differences

- AQA C3, C4; Edexcel 3.3, 3.4; OCR H240 1.03g, 1.03h.
- OCR MEI H640: Mg12 to Mg14 and Mg16. Mg14 names the parametric form of a circle (x = a + r cos θ, y = b + r sin θ) explicitly. The parametric gradient, Mg15, is in file 13.

### C3 Parametric equations of curves

*Refs: AQA C3 · Edexcel 3.3 · OCR 1.03g · MEI Mg12, Mg13, Mg14. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) A parameter t (or θ) gives both coordinates: x = f(t), y = g(t). Each value of t gives one point.
- (A-level only) Convert to Cartesian form by eliminating the parameter.
- (A-level only) Find the domain and range of the Cartesian form from the range of t.
- (A-level only) Find where a parametric curve meets a line or an axis.

#### Explained

**Eliminating the parameter.** Two main methods:

1. Make t the subject of the simpler equation and substitute into the other.
2. If the equations involve sin t and cos t, use an identity such as sin²t + cos²t = 1, or a double angle formula (file 10).

**Circles.**

```latex
x = a + r\cos\theta,\; y = b + r\sin\theta \iff (x-a)^2 + (y-b)^2 = r^2
```

**Domain and range.** The Cartesian equation may describe more of a curve than the parametric one. If x = t² then x ≥ 0 only, even though the Cartesian equation might make sense for negative x. Always state the domain the parameter allows.

**Intersections.** To find where the curve meets the line ax + by = c, substitute the parametric expressions into the line to get an equation in t. Solve it, then find x and y from each t. To find where it meets the x-axis, solve y(t) = 0.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) Find the Cartesian equation of x = t + 1, y = t².

1. t = x − 1.
2. y = (x − 1)².

(A-level only) Find the Cartesian equation of x = 2 + 5cos θ, y = −1 + 5sin θ, and describe the curve.

1. cos θ = (x − 2)/5 and sin θ = (y + 1)/5.
2. cos²θ + sin²θ = 1 gives (x − 2)² + (y + 1)² = 25.
3. A circle with centre (2, −1) and radius 5.

(A-level only) Find the Cartesian equation of x = 1/t, y = t + 2, t ≠ 0.

1. t = 1/x.
2. y = 1/x + 2, x ≠ 0.

(A-level only) A curve has x = sin t, y = cos 2t for 0 ≤ t ≤ π/2. Find its Cartesian equation and domain.

1. cos 2t = 1 − 2sin²t (file 10), so y = 1 − 2x².
2. For 0 ≤ t ≤ π/2, sin t goes from 0 to 1, so 0 ≤ x ≤ 1.

(A-level only) The curve x = t², y = 2t meets the line y = x − 3. Find the points of intersection.

1. 2t = t² − 3, so t² − 2t − 3 = 0, (t − 3)(t + 1) = 0.
2. t = 3: (9, 6). t = −1: (1, −2).

(A-level only) A curve has x = t² + 1, y = 2t for −1 ≤ t ≤ 2. Find the Cartesian equation, and the ranges of x and y.

1. t = y/2, so x = y²/4 + 1.
2. y goes from −2 to 4. x = t² + 1 is smallest at t = 0 (x = 1) and largest at t = 2 (x = 5), so 1 ≤ x ≤ 5.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting the restriction the parameter places on x or y.
- Not squaring the whole expression: from cos θ = (x − 2)/5, cos²θ = (x − 2)²/25.
- Giving only t values when the question asks for points.

### C4 Parametric equations in modelling

*Refs: AQA C4 · Edexcel 3.4 · OCR 1.03h · MEI Mg16. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) Use parametric equations to model motion and shapes, with t often meaning time.
- (A-level only) Interpret the parameter and the restrictions on it in context.

#### Explained

Parametric models are natural for motion: the horizontal and vertical positions are each functions of time. A ball thrown from ground level might have x = ut, y = vt − 4.9t². The flight ends when y = 0 again, which fixes the largest sensible t.

Shapes such as a running track, a cam or a fairground ride are often described parametrically using sin and cos. A model is only valid where its assumptions hold: no air resistance, a flat surface, a rigid object.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) A ball is kicked from ground level. Its position after t seconds is x = 20t, y = 15t − 4.9t² (metres). Find how far it travels horizontally before landing, and find the Cartesian equation of its path.

1. Lands when y = 0: t(15 − 4.9t) = 0, so t = 15/4.9 = 3.06 s (3 s.f.).
2. x = 20 × 3.061... = 61.2 m (3 s.f.).
3. t = x/20, so y = 15(x/20) − 4.9(x/20)² = 0.75x − 0.01225x².
4. Valid for 0 ≤ x ≤ 61.2.

(A-level only) A Ferris wheel seat moves with x = 12sin(πt/10), y = 14 − 12cos(πt/10), where t is in minutes and y is height above the ground in metres. Find the highest point and the time for one revolution.

1. y is greatest when cos(πt/10) = −1: y = 26 m.
2. One revolution: πt/10 = 2π, so t = 20 minutes.
3. The lowest height is 14 − 12 = 2 m, which is the boarding platform height.

#### Common mistakes

- Using negative t or times after landing.
- Using degrees where the model uses radians.

### Check questions

1. (A-level only) Find the Cartesian equation of x = 2t − 1, y = t² + 3. [3 marks]
2. (A-level only) A curve has x = 4cos θ, y = 4sin θ − 1. Show that it is a circle and give its centre and radius. [3 marks]
3. (A-level only) Find the Cartesian equation of x = 3/t, y = t − 1 (t ≠ 0), in the form y = f(x). [3 marks]
4. (A-level only) A curve has x = t², y = t³ for t ≥ 0. Find its Cartesian equation in the form y = f(x). [2 marks]
5. (A-level only) The curve x = t − 2, y = t² meets the line y = 2x + 7. Find the coordinates of the points of intersection. [5 marks]
6. (A-level only) A curve has x = sin t, y = cos 2t for −π/2 ≤ t ≤ π/2. Show that y = 1 − 2x², and state the domain of x. [4 marks]
7. (A-level only) A ball is thrown so that x = 12t and y = 1.5 + 9t − 4.9t², in metres, t seconds after release. (a) Find the time when the ball hits the ground. (b) Find the horizontal distance travelled. (c) Give one limitation of the model. [6 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. t = (x + 1)/2 (1); substitute (1); y = (x + 1)²/4 + 3 (1).
2. cos θ = x/4, sin θ = (y + 1)/4 (1); x² + (y + 1)² = 16 (1); centre (0, −1), radius 4 (1).
3. t = 3/x (1); y = 3/x − 1 (1); or y = (3 − x)/x (1).
4. t = x^{1/2} (1); y = x^{3/2} (1).
5. t² = 2(t − 2) + 7 (1); t² − 2t − 3 = 0 (1); t = 3 or t = −1 (1); (1, 9) (1); (−3, 1) (1).
6. cos 2t = 1 − 2sin²t (1); substitute sin t = x (1); y = 1 − 2x² (1); −1 ≤ x ≤ 1 (1).
7. (a) 4.9t² − 9t − 1.5 = 0 (1); formula (1); t = 1.99 s, rejecting the negative root (1). (b) x = 12 × 1.99 = 23.9 m (1) (accept 23.8 to 24.0). (c) One limitation, e.g. air resistance ignored, or the ball treated as a particle, or g taken as exactly 9.8 (2).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/maths/13-differentiation-further-techniques.md -->

## Differentiation: standard functions, chain, product and quotient rules, implicit and parametric

This file covers differentiating exponentials, logarithms and trigonometric functions, the chain, product and quotient rules, connected rates of change, inverse functions, and implicit and parametric differentiation. The basics (xⁿ, tangents, stationary points) are in file 12.

### Overview

All A-level only. The results here are used constantly in integration (file 15), differential equations (file 16) and the Newton-Raphson method (file 17).

#### Board differences

- AQA G2 (the non-power part), G4, G5; Edexcel 7.2, 7.4, 7.5.
- OCR H240: 1.07j to 1.07l, 1.07q to 1.07s.
- OCR MEI H640: Mc10 to Mc16, and Mg15 for the gradient of a parametric curve.
- Edexcel's 7.4 guidance includes the derivatives of cosec x, sec x and cot x. They follow from the quotient rule, and the other boards' formula books give them too, so they are included here for everyone.
- Only the first derivative is needed for implicit and parametric differentiation (all boards).

### G2 Derivatives of standard functions

*Refs: AQA G2 · Edexcel 7.2 · OCR 1.07j, 1.07k, 1.07l · MEI Mc10, Mc11. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) The standard derivatives (x in radians for the trig functions):

| f(x) | f′(x) |
| --- | --- |
| e^{kx} | ke^{kx} |
| a^{kx} | ka^{kx} ln a |
| ln x | 1/x |
| sin kx | k cos kx |
| cos kx | −k sin kx |
| tan kx | k sec²kx |
| sec x | sec x tan x |
| cosec x | −cosec x cot x |
| cot x | −cosec²x |

- (A-level only) Sums, differences and constant multiples of these.

#### Explained

aˣ = e^{x ln a}, so its derivative is ln a × e^{x ln a} = aˣ ln a.

ln(kx) = ln k + ln x, so its derivative is also 1/x: the constant k disappears.

The trig derivatives only hold in radians. In degrees, extra factors of π/180 appear.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) Differentiate y = 3e^{−2x} + 5 ln x − 4 sin 3x.

1. dy/dx = −6e^{−2x} + 5/x − 12 cos 3x.

(A-level only) Differentiate y = 2ˣ and find the gradient at x = 3.

1. dy/dx = 2ˣ ln 2.
2. At x = 3: 8 ln 2 = 5.55.

(A-level only) Differentiate y = cos(x/2) + tan 4x.

1. dy/dx = −(1/2) sin(x/2) + 4 sec²4x.

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) Writing the derivative of aˣ as xa^{x−1}.
- (A-level only) Writing the derivative of ln 5x as 5/x.

### G4 Chain, product and quotient rules; rates of change

*Refs: AQA G4 · Edexcel 7.4 · OCR 1.07q, 1.07r · MEI Mc12, Mc13, Mc14, Mc15. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) Chain rule, for a function of a function:

```latex
\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{dy}{du} \times \frac{du}{dx}
```

- (A-level only) Product rule, for y = uv:

```latex
\frac{dy}{dx} = u\frac{dv}{dx} + v\frac{du}{dx}
```

- (A-level only) Quotient rule, for y = u/v:

```latex
\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{v\dfrac{du}{dx} - u\dfrac{dv}{dx}}{v^2}
```

- (A-level only) Connected rates of change, using the chain rule with time.
- (A-level only) Derivative of an inverse function:

```latex
\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\;dx/dy\;}
```

#### Explained

**Chain rule in practice.** Differentiate the outside function, leaving the inside alone, then multiply by the derivative of the inside. Common patterns:

```latex
\frac{d}{dx}\left[f(x)\right]^n = n\left[f(x)\right]^{n-1}f'(x) \qquad \frac{d}{dx}e^{f(x)} = f'(x)e^{f(x)} \qquad \frac{d}{dx}\ln f(x) = \frac{f'(x)}{f(x)}
```

**Which rule?** A product of two functions of x: product rule. A function inside another: chain rule. A fraction with x top and bottom: quotient rule (or write it as a product with a negative power). Many questions need two rules together.

**Connected rates of change.** Write the rate you want as a product of rates you know, for example dr/dt = (dV/dt) ÷ (dV/dr). Write a formula linking the two variables, differentiate it, and substitute.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) Differentiate y = (3x² − 1)⁵.

1. dy/dx = 5(3x² − 1)⁴ × 6x = 30x(3x² − 1)⁴.

(A-level only) Differentiate y = √(1 + 4x) and y = ln(x² + 1) and y = e^{sin x}.

1. (1 + 4x)^{1/2}: (1/2)(1 + 4x)^{−1/2} × 4 = 2/√(1 + 4x).
2. ln(x² + 1): 2x/(x² + 1).
3. e^{sin x}: cos x e^{sin x}.

(A-level only) Differentiate y = x²e^{3x}.

1. u = x², v = e^{3x}; u′ = 2x, v′ = 3e^{3x}.
2. dy/dx = 3x²e^{3x} + 2xe^{3x} = xe^{3x}(3x + 2).

(A-level only) Differentiate y = x³ ln x and find the exact x-coordinate of its stationary point.

1. dy/dx = 3x² ln x + x³ × (1/x) = x²(3 ln x + 1).
2. x > 0, so 3 ln x + 1 = 0, ln x = −1/3, x = e^{−1/3}.

(A-level only) Differentiate y = (2x + 1)/(x − 3).

1. dy/dx = [(x − 3)(2) − (2x + 1)(1)]/(x − 3)² = −7/(x − 3)².

(A-level only) Prove that the derivative of tan x is sec²x.

1. tan x = sin x/cos x.
2. Quotient rule: [cos x cos x − sin x(−sin x)]/cos²x = (cos²x + sin²x)/cos²x = 1/cos²x = sec²x.

(A-level only) Prove that the derivative of sec x is sec x tan x.

1. sec x = (cos x)⁻¹. Chain rule: −(cos x)⁻² × (−sin x) = sin x/cos²x.
2. = (1/cos x)(sin x/cos x) = sec x tan x.

(A-level only) The volume of a sphere increases at 10 cm³ s⁻¹. Find the rate of increase of the radius when r = 5 cm.

1. V = (4/3)πr³, so dV/dr = 4πr² = 100π at r = 5.
2. dr/dt = (dV/dt)/(dV/dr) = 10/(100π) = 1/(10π) = 0.0318 cm s⁻¹.

(A-level only) Find dy/dx when x = y³ + y.

1. dx/dy = 3y² + 1.
2. dy/dx = 1/(3y² + 1).

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) Forgetting to multiply by the derivative of the inside function.
- (A-level only) Getting the order in the quotient rule wrong: it is v u′ − u v′ on top.
- (A-level only) Writing d/dx(e^{x²}) as e^{x²}.

### G5 Implicit and parametric differentiation

*Refs: AQA G5 · Edexcel 7.5 · OCR 1.07s · MEI Mc16, Mg15. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) Differentiate a relation between x and y without making y the subject (implicit differentiation), for first derivatives only.
- (A-level only) For a curve given by x = f(t), y = g(t):

```latex
\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{dy/dt}{dx/dt}
```

#### Explained

**Implicit.** Differentiate every term with respect to x. Terms in x only: as usual. Terms in y: differentiate with respect to y, then multiply by dy/dx (chain rule). Terms with both x and y: product rule. Then collect the dy/dx terms and factorise.

```latex
\frac{d}{dx}(y^2) = 2y\frac{dy}{dx} \qquad \frac{d}{dx}(xy) = x\frac{dy}{dx} + y \qquad \frac{d}{dx}(e^{y}) = e^{y}\frac{dy}{dx}
```

**Parametric.** Differentiate x and y with respect to t, then divide. The gradient is in terms of t. Find the point and gradient at a given t, then write the tangent or normal in x and y. Stationary points occur where dy/dt = 0 (with dx/dt ≠ 0). A vertical tangent occurs where dx/dt = 0 (with dy/dt ≠ 0).

#### Worked example

(A-level only) Find the gradient of x² + y² − 4x + 6y = 12 at (5, 1).

1. 2x + 2y(dy/dx) − 4 + 6(dy/dx) = 0.
2. dy/dx = (4 − 2x)/(2y + 6) = (2 − x)/(y + 3).
3. At (5, 1): −3/4.

(A-level only) Find the tangent to x³ + xy + y² = 7 at (1, 2).

1. 3x² + (x(dy/dx) + y) + 2y(dy/dx) = 0.
2. dy/dx = −(3x² + y)/(x + 2y) = −5/5 = −1 at (1, 2).
3. Tangent: y − 2 = −(x − 1), so y = 3 − x.

(A-level only) A curve has x = t², y = t³ − 3t. Find the stationary points, and the tangent where t = 2.

1. dx/dt = 2t, dy/dt = 3t² − 3. dy/dx = (3t² − 3)/(2t).
2. Stationary when 3t² − 3 = 0, t = ±1: points (1, −2) and (1, 2).
3. At t = 2: point (4, 2), gradient 9/4.
4. Tangent: y − 2 = (9/4)(x − 4), so 9x − 4y − 28 = 0.

(A-level only) The ellipse x = 2cos θ, y = 3sin θ. Find the gradient at θ = π/4.

1. dy/dx = 3cos θ/(−2sin θ) = −(3/2)cot θ.
2. At θ = π/4: −3/2.

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) Forgetting the dy/dx when differentiating a y term.
- (A-level only) Differentiating xy as x(dy/dx) only, missing the + y.
- (A-level only) Writing the parametric gradient as (dx/dt)/(dy/dt).

### Check questions

1. (A-level only) Differentiate: (a) e^{−4x}; (b) 5ˣ; (c) ln(3x − 2); (d) cos 5x; (e) tan(x/3). [5 marks]
2. (A-level only) Differentiate y = x² sin 3x. [3 marks]
3. (A-level only) The curve y = ln x / x² has one stationary point. Find dy/dx and the exact coordinates of the stationary point. [6 marks]
4. (A-level only) Find dy/dx for y = 1/√(5 − 2x). [2 marks]
5. (A-level only) A curve has equation x = 3y² − 2y. Find dy/dx at the point (1, 1). [3 marks]
6. (A-level only) Find the equation of the tangent to x² − 2xy + 3y² = 6 at (−1, 1). [5 marks]
7. (A-level only) A curve has x = t³, y = t² − 4t. Find the equation of the normal at t = 1. [5 marks]
8. (A-level only) The side of a cube increases at 0.2 cm s⁻¹. Find the rate of increase of its volume when the side is 5 cm. [3 marks]
9. (A-level only) Show that the derivative of cosec x is −cosec x cot x. [3 marks]
10. (A-level only) Find the coordinates of the stationary point of y = xe^{−x} and determine its nature. [5 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. (a) −4e^{−4x} (1). (b) 5ˣ ln 5 (1). (c) 3/(3x − 2) (1). (d) −5 sin 5x (1). (e) (1/3)sec²(x/3) (1).
2. Product rule (1); 2x sin 3x (1); + 3x² cos 3x (1).
3. Quotient rule (1); dy/dx = (x − 2x ln x)/x⁴ = (1 − 2 ln x)/x³ (2); 1 − 2 ln x = 0, x = e^{1/2} (1); y = (1/2)/e = 1/(2e) (1); (√e, 1/(2e)) (1).
4. (5 − 2x)^{−1/2}, derivative −(1/2)(5 − 2x)^{−3/2} × (−2) (1); (5 − 2x)^{−3/2} (1).
5. dx/dy = 6y − 2 = 4 at y = 1 (2); dy/dx = 1/4 (1).
6. 2x − 2y − 2x(dy/dx) + 6y(dy/dx) = 0 (2); dy/dx = (2y − 2x)/(6y − 2x) = 4/8 = 1/2 at (−1, 1) (1); y − 1 = (1/2)(x + 1) (1); x − 2y + 3 = 0 (1).
7. dy/dx = (2t − 4)/(3t²) (1); = −2/3 at t = 1 (1); point (1, −3) (1); normal gradient 3/2 (1); 3x − 2y − 9 = 0 (1).
8. V = s³, dV/ds = 3s² = 75 (1); dV/dt = 75 × 0.2 (1) = 15 cm³ s⁻¹ (1).
9. cosec x = (sin x)⁻¹; derivative −(sin x)⁻² cos x (1); = −(1/sin x)(cos x/sin x) (1); = −cosec x cot x (1).
10. dy/dx = e^{−x} − xe^{−x} = e^{−x}(1 − x) (2); x = 1, y = 1/e (1); d²y/dx² = e^{−x}(x − 2) = −1/e < 0 at x = 1 (1); maximum (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/maths/07-binomial-expansion.md -->

## The binomial expansion

This file covers expanding (a + bx)ⁿ, first for positive whole numbers n, then for any rational n, with the condition for the infinite series to be valid and its use in approximations.

### Overview

AS: positive integer n, Pascal's triangle, n!, ⁿCᵣ, and the link to the binomial distribution (file 22). A-level only: negative and fractional n, the validity condition |bx/a| < 1, and approximations.

#### Board differences

- AQA D1; Edexcel 4.1.
- OCR H240: 1.04a and 1.04b (AS), 1.04c and 1.04d (A-level only).
- OCR MEI H640: Ms1 and Ms2 (AS), Ms3 to Ms5 (A-level only). MEI states that the proof of the general expansion is not required.

### D1 The binomial expansion

*Refs: AQA D1 · Edexcel 4.1 · OCR 1.04a, 1.04b, 1.04c, 1.04d · MEI Ms1, Ms2, Ms3, Ms4, Ms5. Level: AS & A (part A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- For a positive integer n:

```latex
(a+b)^n = a^n + \binom{n}{1}a^{n-1}b + \binom{n}{2}a^{n-2}b^2 + \dots + \binom{n}{r}a^{n-r}b^r + \dots + b^n
```

```latex
\binom{n}{r} = {}^nC_r = \frac{n!}{r!\,(n-r)!} \qquad n! = n(n-1)(n-2)\dots 2 \times 1, \quad 0! = 1
```

- The coefficients are the numbers in row n of Pascal's triangle.
- The same coefficients ⁿCᵣ give binomial probabilities.
- (A-level only) For any rational n and |x| < 1:

```latex
(1+x)^n = 1 + nx + \frac{n(n-1)}{2!}x^2 + \frac{n(n-1)(n-2)}{3!}x^3 + \dots
```

- (A-level only) For (a + bx)ⁿ, first take out aⁿ: (a + bx)ⁿ = aⁿ(1 + bx/a)ⁿ, valid for |bx/a| < 1, that is |x| < |a/b|.
- (A-level only) Use the expansion to approximate values.

#### Explained

**Positive integer n.** The expansion has n + 1 terms and stops. The general term is ⁿCᵣ aⁿ⁻ʳ bʳ. Powers of a go down while powers of b go up, and the powers in each term add to n.

Pascal's triangle rows 0 to 6:

| n | coefficients |
| --- | --- |
| 0 | 1 |
| 1 | 1 1 |
| 2 | 1 2 1 |
| 3 | 1 3 3 1 |
| 4 | 1 4 6 4 1 |
| 5 | 1 5 10 10 5 1 |
| 6 | 1 6 15 20 15 6 1 |

When b is negative or a fraction, put the whole of b in brackets before raising it to a power: (−2x)³ = −8x³.

**Link to probability.** If X ~ B(n, p), then P(X = r) = ⁿCᵣ pʳ(1 − p)ⁿ⁻ʳ, which is one term of the expansion of (p + (1 − p))ⁿ = 1. This is why the probabilities add to 1.

**(A-level only) Any rational n.** If n is negative or a fraction, the series never stops: the coefficients n(n − 1)(n − 2)... never reach zero. An infinite series only makes sense if it converges, which needs |x| < 1 in (1 + x)ⁿ.

The coefficient formula n(n − 1)...(n − r + 1)/r! is the same as ⁿCᵣ when n is a positive integer, but you cannot use the calculator's nCr button for negative or fractional n.

**(A-level only) Approximation.** For small x, a few terms give a good estimate. Choose x so it is small and makes the bracket match the number you want. The smaller x is, the fewer terms you need.

#### Worked example

Expand (2 + 3x)⁵ up to and including the term in x³.

1. 2⁵ = 32.
2. ⁵C₁ 2⁴(3x) = 5 × 16 × 3x = 240x.
3. ⁵C₂ 2³(3x)² = 10 × 8 × 9x² = 720x².
4. ⁵C₃ 2²(3x)³ = 10 × 4 × 27x³ = 1080x³.
5. (2 + 3x)⁵ = 32 + 240x + 720x² + 1080x³ + ...

Find the coefficient of x³ in (1 − 2x)⁸.

1. ⁸C₃(−2x)³ = 56 × (−8)x³.
2. Coefficient −448.

Find the term independent of x in (x + 2/x)⁶.

1. General term ⁶Cᵣ x⁶⁻ʳ(2/x)ʳ = ⁶Cᵣ 2ʳ x⁶⁻²ʳ.
2. Independent of x when 6 − 2r = 0, r = 3.
3. ⁶C₃ × 2³ = 20 × 8 = 160.

The coefficient of x² in (1 + x)ⁿ is 28. Find n.

1. n(n − 1)/2 = 28, so n² − n − 56 = 0, (n − 8)(n + 7) = 0.
2. n is a positive integer, so n = 8.

Use the expansion of (1 + x)⁸ to estimate 1.02⁸ to 5 decimal places.

1. x = 0.02: 1 + 8(0.02) + 28(0.02)² + 56(0.02)³ + ...
2. = 1 + 0.16 + 0.0112 + 0.000448 + 70(0.02)⁴ + ... = 1.171648 + 0.0000112 + ... = 1.1716592...
3. To 5 decimal places, 1.02⁸ ≈ 1.17166. The later terms are smaller than 0.0000002, so they cannot change this.

(A-level only) Expand (1 − 3x)^{1/2} up to x³ and state the validity.

1. n = 1/2 and x replaced by −3x.
2. 1 + (1/2)(−3x) + [(1/2)(−1/2)/2](−3x)² + [(1/2)(−1/2)(−3/2)/6](−3x)³.
3. Coefficients: −3/2; (−1/8)(9) = −9/8; (1/16)(−27) = −27/16.
4. (1 − 3x)^{1/2} = 1 − (3/2)x − (9/8)x² − (27/16)x³ + ..., valid for |x| < 1/3.

(A-level only) Expand (4 + x)^{−1/2} up to x².

1. (4 + x)^{−1/2} = 4^{−1/2}(1 + x/4)^{−1/2} = (1/2)(1 + x/4)^{−1/2}.
2. (1 + x/4)^{−1/2} = 1 + (−1/2)(x/4) + [(−1/2)(−3/2)/2](x/4)² = 1 − x/8 + 3x²/128.
3. Multiply by 1/2: 1/2 − x/16 + 3x²/256, valid for |x| < 4.

(A-level only) Use the expansion of (1 + x)^{1/2} to estimate √17.

1. √17 = √(16(1 + 1/16)) = 4(1 + 1/16)^{1/2}.
2. (1 + x)^{1/2} ≈ 1 + x/2 − x²/8 with x = 1/16: 1 + 0.03125 − 0.00048828 = 1.03076.
3. √17 ≈ 4 × 1.03076 = 4.1230 (the true value is 4.1231 to 4 d.p.).

(A-level only) Using partial fractions, expand (5x + 1)/[(x − 1)(x + 2)] up to x², and state the validity.

1. From file 03: (5x + 1)/[(x − 1)(x + 2)] = 2/(x − 1) + 3/(x + 2).
2. 2/(x − 1) = −2(1 − x)⁻¹ = −2(1 + x + x² + ...) = −2 − 2x − 2x².
3. 3/(x + 2) = (3/2)(1 + x/2)⁻¹ = (3/2)(1 − x/2 + x²/4) = 3/2 − 3x/4 + 3x²/8.
4. Sum: −1/2 − (11/4)x − (13/8)x².
5. Valid where both expansions are: |x| < 1 and |x| < 2, so |x| < 1.

#### Common mistakes

- Writing 3x² instead of (3x)² = 9x².
- Losing the minus sign: (−2x)³ = −8x³.
- (A-level only) Expanding (4 + x)ⁿ as if it were (1 + x)ⁿ, without taking out 4ⁿ.
- (A-level only) Getting the validity wrong: (2 − 5x)ⁿ needs |5x/2| < 1, that is |x| < 2/5.
- (A-level only) Using a value of x outside the range of validity to approximate.

### Check questions

1. Expand (3 − x)⁴ fully. [3 marks]
2. Find the coefficient of x⁴ in the expansion of (2 + x/2)¹⁰. [3 marks]
3. Find the term independent of x in (2x − 1/x²)⁶. [4 marks]
4. In the expansion of (1 + kx)⁷ the coefficient of x² is 84. Find the possible values of k. [3 marks]
5. Explain how the coefficients in the expansion of (p + q)⁵ are linked to the probabilities in the distribution B(5, p). [2 marks]
6. (A-level only) Expand (1 + 2x)⁻² up to and including the term in x³, and state the values of x for which it is valid. [4 marks]
7. (A-level only) (a) Expand √(9 − x) up to the term in x². (b) Use x = 0.09 to estimate √8.91 to 6 decimal places. [6 marks]
8. (A-level only) Show that (1 + x)/(1 − x) ≈ 1 + 2x + 2x² + 2x³ for small x. [3 marks]
9. (A-level only) (a) Express (4x + 5)/[(x + 1)(x + 2)] in partial fractions. (b) Hence expand it up to x², stating the validity. [7 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Coefficients 1, 4, 6, 4, 1 (1); powers of 3 and (−x) correct (1); 81 − 108x + 54x² − 12x³ + x⁴ (1).
2. ¹⁰C₄ × 2⁶ × (1/2)⁴ (1); 210 × 64/16 (1); 840 (1).
3. General term ⁶Cᵣ(2x)⁶⁻ʳ(−x⁻²)ʳ (1); power of x is 6 − 3r (1); r = 2 (1); 15 × 16 = 240 (1).
4. ⁷C₂k² = 21k² (1); 21k² = 84 (1); k = ±2 (1).
5. The coefficients are ⁵Cᵣ (1); P(X = r) = ⁵Cᵣ pʳ(1 − p)⁵⁻ʳ is the term in pʳ of (p + (1 − p))⁵, with q = 1 − p (1).
6. 1 + (−2)(2x) + [(−2)(−3)/2](2x)² + [(−2)(−3)(−4)/6](2x)³ (1); 1 − 4x (1); + 12x² − 32x³ (1); valid for |x| < 1/2 (1).
7. (a) 3(1 − x/9)^{1/2} (1); 1 − x/18 − x²/648 (1); 3 − x/6 − x²/216 (1). (b) √8.91 = √(9 − 0.09) (1); 3 − 0.015 − 0.0000375 (1); 2.984963 (1). (The true value is 2.984962 to 6 d.p.; the small difference comes from the terms left out.)
8. (1 − x)⁻¹ = 1 + x + x² + x³ + ... (1); (1 + x)(1 + x + x² + x³) (1); = 1 + 2x + 2x² + 2x³ + ... (1).
9. (a) 4x + 5 ≡ A(x + 2) + B(x + 1) (1); 1/(x + 1) + 3/(x + 2) (2). (b) (1 + x)⁻¹ = 1 − x + x² (1); 3/(x + 2) = (3/2)(1 + x/2)⁻¹ = 3/2 − 3x/4 + 3x²/8 (1); 5/2 − (7/4)x + (11/8)x² (1); valid for |x| < 1 (1).

## Sequences and series

This file covers sequences defined by a formula or by a recurrence relation, increasing, decreasing and periodic sequences, sigma notation, arithmetic and geometric sequences and series, the sum to infinity, and modelling with sequences.

### Overview

All A-level only (the binomial expansion, in file 07, is the only part of the DfE "sequences and series" heading that is in AS).

#### Board differences

- AQA D2 to D6; Edexcel 4.2 to 4.6; OCR H240 1.04e to 1.04k.
- OCR MEI H640 splits this into Ms6 to Ms17. Ms11 asks you to know the difference between convergent and divergent sequences, which the other boards cover under geometric series.
- Edexcel's guidance says you should be able to prove the sum formulae for both arithmetic and geometric series.

### D2 Sequences

*Refs: AQA D2 · Edexcel 4.2 · OCR 1.04e, 1.04f · MEI Ms6, Ms7, Ms10, Ms11. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) A sequence can be given by a formula for the nth term, uₙ = f(n), or by a recurrence relation uₙ₊₁ = f(uₙ) with a first term.
- (A-level only) Increasing: uₙ₊₁ > uₙ for all n. Decreasing: uₙ₊₁ < uₙ for all n. Periodic: the terms repeat in a cycle; the order (period) is the length of the cycle.
- (A-level only) A sequence converges if its terms approach a limit; otherwise it diverges.

#### Explained

A recurrence relation needs a starting value: "uₙ₊₁ = 2uₙ + 1, u₁ = 3" gives 3, 7, 15, 31, ...

To show a sequence given by a formula is increasing, show uₙ₊₁ − uₙ > 0 for every n.

A periodic sequence repeats, so to add many terms, add one cycle and multiply. Sequences such as uₙ = (−1)ⁿ or uₙ = cos(nπ/2) are periodic.

If a convergent recurrence uₙ₊₁ = f(uₙ) has limit L, then L = f(L). This is the idea behind fixed-point iteration (file 17).

#### Worked example

(A-level only) uₙ₊₁ = kuₙ + 3, u₁ = 2 and u₃ = 23. Find the possible values of k.

1. u₂ = 2k + 3. u₃ = k(2k + 3) + 3 = 2k² + 3k + 3.
2. 2k² + 3k + 3 = 23, so 2k² + 3k − 20 = 0, (2k − 5)(k + 4) = 0.
3. k = 5/2 or k = −4.

(A-level only) A sequence has u₁ = 2 and uₙ₊₁ = 1/(1 − uₙ). Show it is periodic and state its order.

1. u₂ = 1/(1 − 2) = −1. u₃ = 1/(1 − (−1)) = 1/2. u₄ = 1/(1 − 1/2) = 2.
2. u₄ = u₁, so the terms repeat 2, −1, 1/2, ... with order 3.

(A-level only) Show that uₙ = (2n + 1)/(n + 1) is increasing.

1. uₙ = 2 − 1/(n + 1).
2. As n increases, 1/(n + 1) decreases, so uₙ increases. It converges to 2.

#### Common mistakes

- Starting a recurrence from u₀ when u₁ is given, or the other way round.
- Calling a sequence increasing after checking only the first few terms.

### D3 Sigma notation

*Refs: AQA D3 · Edexcel 4.3 · OCR 1.04g · MEI Ms8, Ms9. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) A series is the sum of terms of a sequence.
- (A-level only) Sigma notation:

```latex
\sum_{r=1}^{n} u_r = u_1 + u_2 + \dots + u_n
```

- (A-level only) Useful result: the sum of the first n positive integers.

```latex
\sum_{r=1}^{n} r = \frac{n(n+1)}{2} \qquad \sum_{r=1}^{n} 1 = n
```

#### Explained

Read the limits carefully: Σ from r = 5 to 12 has 12 − 5 + 1 = 8 terms. You can split a sum: Σ(ar + b) = aΣr + Σb. To sum from r = k to n, work out Σ to n minus Σ to (k − 1).

#### Worked example

(A-level only) Evaluate Σ_{r=1}^{20} (3r + 1).

1. 3 × (20 × 21/2) + 20 = 3 × 210 + 20 = 650.

(A-level only) Evaluate Σ_{r=11}^{30} r.

1. Σ to 30 = 465. Σ to 10 = 55.
2. 465 − 55 = 410.

#### Common mistakes

- Counting terms as n − k instead of n − k + 1.
- Forgetting the constant term contributes n copies of itself.

### D4 Arithmetic sequences and series

*Refs: AQA D4 · Edexcel 4.4 · OCR 1.04h · MEI Ms12, Ms13. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) An arithmetic sequence has a constant difference d. With first term a:

```latex
u_n = a + (n-1)d \qquad S_n = \frac{n}{2}\left[2a + (n-1)d\right] = \frac{n}{2}(a + l)
```

where l is the last term.

- (A-level only) Prove the sum formula.

#### Explained

**Proof of the sum formula.** Write the sum forwards and backwards:

```latex
S_n = a + (a+d) + \dots + (a + (n-1)d)
```

```latex
S_n = (a + (n-1)d) + (a + (n-2)d) + \dots + a
```

Add the two lines term by term. Each pair sums to 2a + (n − 1)d, and there are n pairs, so 2Sₙ = n[2a + (n − 1)d], giving the formula.

Two facts about an arithmetic sequence give two simultaneous equations for a and d.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) The 3rd term of an arithmetic sequence is 11 and the 10th term is 32. Find a, d and S₂₀.

1. a + 2d = 11 and a + 9d = 32. Subtract: 7d = 21, d = 3, a = 5.
2. S₂₀ = 10[10 + 19 × 3] = 10 × 67 = 670.

(A-level only) For the same sequence, find the least n for which Sₙ > 1000.

1. (n/2)(10 + 3(n − 1)) > 1000, so n(3n + 7) > 2000, so 3n² + 7n − 2000 > 0.
2. Root: n = (−7 + √24 049)/6 = 24.68...
3. n = 25. Check: S₂₄ = 948 and S₂₅ = 1025.

(A-level only) Ali saves £50 in the first month and £5 more each month than the month before. How much has Ali saved after 2 years?

1. a = 50, d = 5, n = 24.
2. S₂₄ = 12[100 + 23 × 5] = 12 × 215 = £2580.

#### Common mistakes

- Using nd instead of (n − 1)d.
- Rounding n down when the question asks for the first n that exceeds a value.

### D5 Geometric sequences and series

*Refs: AQA D5 · Edexcel 4.5 · OCR 1.04i, 1.04j · MEI Ms14, Ms15, Ms16. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) A geometric sequence has a constant ratio r. With first term a:

```latex
u_n = ar^{n-1} \qquad S_n = \frac{a(1-r^n)}{1-r} = \frac{a(r^n - 1)}{r - 1} \quad (r \ne 1)
```

- (A-level only) If |r| < 1 the series converges and its sum to infinity is

```latex
S_\infty = \frac{a}{1-r}
```

- (A-level only) Modulus notation: |r| < 1 means −1 < r < 1.
- (A-level only) Prove the sum formula.

#### Explained

**Proof of the sum formula.**

```latex
S_n = a + ar + ar^2 + \dots + ar^{n-1}
```

```latex
rS_n = ar + ar^2 + \dots + ar^{n-1} + ar^n
```

Subtract: Sₙ − rSₙ = a − arⁿ, so Sₙ(1 − r) = a(1 − rⁿ), and divide by 1 − r.

**Sum to infinity.** If |r| < 1, rⁿ → 0 as n → ∞, so Sₙ → a/(1 − r). If |r| ≥ 1 the terms do not shrink and the series diverges.

**Finding n.** Inequalities such as rⁿ > k are solved with logarithms (file 11). If r < 1, ln r is negative, so dividing by it reverses the inequality.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) A geometric sequence has a = 48 and r = 1/2. Find u₆, S₁₀ and S∞.

1. u₆ = 48 × (1/2)⁵ = 1.5.
2. S₁₀ = 48(1 − (1/2)¹⁰)/(1/2) = 96(1 − 1/1024) = 95.90625.
3. S∞ = 48/(1/2) = 96.

(A-level only) The 2nd term of a geometric sequence is 6 and the 5th term is 162. Find a, r and S₈.

1. ar = 6 and ar⁴ = 162. Divide: r³ = 27, r = 3, a = 2.
2. S₈ = 2(3⁸ − 1)/(3 − 1) = 6560.

(A-level only) A geometric series has a = 2 and r = 1.5. Find the least n for which Sₙ > 500.

1. 2(1.5ⁿ − 1)/0.5 > 500, so 4(1.5ⁿ − 1) > 500, so 1.5ⁿ > 126.
2. n > ln 126 / ln 1.5 = 11.93, so n = 12.

(A-level only) The series 1 + 2x + 4x² + 8x³ + ... converges. Find the possible values of x and the sum.

1. r = 2x. Converges when |2x| < 1, that is −1/2 < x < 1/2.
2. S∞ = 1/(1 − 2x).

#### Common mistakes

- Using rⁿ instead of rⁿ⁻¹ for the nth term.
- Using S∞ when |r| ≥ 1.
- Forgetting to reverse the inequality when dividing by ln r with r < 1.

### D6 Sequences and series in modelling

*Refs: AQA D6 · Edexcel 4.6 · OCR 1.04k · MEI Ms17. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) Use arithmetic and geometric sequences to model situations such as savings, salaries, depreciation and populations, and comment on the model.

#### Explained

A fixed amount added each time gives an arithmetic model. A fixed percentage change each time gives a geometric model: an increase of p% gives r = 1 + p/100, a decrease gives r = 1 − p/100. Real situations rarely change by exactly the same amount every time, and models often break down in the long run (a population cannot grow forever).

#### Worked example

(A-level only) A starting salary of £24 000 rises by 3% each year. Find the total earned over the first 10 years.

1. Geometric with a = 24 000 and r = 1.03.
2. S₁₀ = 24 000(1.03¹⁰ − 1)/0.03 = £275 133 (nearest pound).

(A-level only) A runner covers 5 km in week 1 and increases the distance by 10% each week. Find the distance in week 8 and the total over 8 weeks. Comment on the model.

1. u₈ = 5 × 1.1⁷ = 9.74 km.
2. S₈ = 5(1.1⁸ − 1)/0.1 = 57.2 km.
3. The model cannot continue: in week 52 it would predict about 650 km in one week. A real plan would level off.

#### Common mistakes

- Using r = 0.03 instead of 1.03 for a 3% increase.
- Counting the first year as n = 0.

### Check questions

1. (A-level only) uₙ₊₁ = 2uₙ − 1 and u₁ = 3. Find u₂, u₃, u₄ and Σ_{r=1}^{4} uᵣ. [3 marks]
2. (A-level only) u₁ = 2 and uₙ₊₁ = (uₙ + 3)/(uₙ − 1). (a) Show the sequence is periodic and state its order. (b) Find Σ_{r=1}^{25} uᵣ. [4 marks]
3. (A-level only) An arithmetic series has first term 7 and 15th term 63. Find the common difference and the sum of the first 15 terms. [3 marks]
4. (A-level only) Evaluate Σ_{r=1}^{30} (4r − 3). [2 marks]
5. (A-level only) Prove that the sum of the first n terms of an arithmetic series with first term a and common difference d is (n/2)[2a + (n − 1)d]. [4 marks]
6. (A-level only) A geometric sequence has 3rd term 18 and 6th term 486. Find the first term, the common ratio and the sum of the first 10 terms. [4 marks]
7. (A-level only) A geometric series has a = 80 and r = 0.75. (a) Find S∞. (b) Find the least n for which Sₙ > 300. [5 marks]
8. (A-level only) The series 1 + (x − 1) + (x − 1)² + ... converges. (a) Find the set of possible values of x. (b) Given that the sum is 4, find x. [4 marks]
9. (A-level only) A runner trains 5 km in week 1 and increases by 10% each week. Find the total distance over the first 8 weeks, and explain why the model is unrealistic for a whole year. [4 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. 5, 9, 17 (2); sum 3 + 5 + 9 + 17 = 34 (1).
2. (a) u₂ = 5, u₃ = 8/4 = 2 (1); repeats with order 2 (1). (b) 13 terms equal 2 and 12 equal 5 (1); 26 + 60 = 86 (1).
3. 7 + 14d = 63, d = 4 (1); S₁₅ = (15/2)(7 + 63) (1) = 525 (1).
4. 4 × 465 − 90 (1) = 1770 (1).
5. Sₙ written forwards and backwards (1); adding pairs, each 2a + (n − 1)d (1); n pairs, so 2Sₙ = n[2a + (n − 1)d] (1); conclusion (1).
6. ar² = 18 and ar⁵ = 486 (1); r³ = 27, r = 3 (1); a = 2 (1); S₁₀ = 2(3¹⁰ − 1)/2 = 59 048 (1).
7. (a) 80/0.25 = 320 (1). (b) 320(1 − 0.75ⁿ) > 300 (1); 0.75ⁿ < 1/16 (1); n > ln(1/16)/ln 0.75 = 9.64 (1); n = 10 (1).
8. (a) |x − 1| < 1 (1); 0 < x < 2 (1). (b) 1/(1 − (x − 1)) = 1/(2 − x) = 4 (1); x = 7/4 (1).
9. Geometric, a = 5, r = 1.1 (1); S₈ = 5(1.1⁸ − 1)/0.1 (1) = 57.2 km (1); e.g. it predicts impossibly large weekly distances, since 5 × 1.1⁵¹ is about 650 km (1).
