## Trigonometry: functions, triangle rules, radians and small angles

This file covers sine, cosine and tangent for any angle, their graphs and symmetries, exact values, the sine and cosine rules, the area of a triangle, radian measure with arcs and sectors, and the small angle approximations. Identities and equations are in file 10.

### Overview

AS works in degrees: definitions for all angles, graphs, the sine and cosine rules, and area = ½ab sin C. A-level only: radians, arc length and sector area, exact values in radians, and the small angle approximations (which need radians).

#### Board differences

- AQA E1 to E3; Edexcel 5.1 to 5.3.
- OCR H240: 1.05a to 1.05c and 1.05f (AS); 1.05d, 1.05e and 1.05g (A-level only).
- OCR MEI H640: Mt1 to Mt4 (AS); Mt8 and Mt10 to Mt12 (A-level only). MEI puts exact values (Mt8) in the A-level part, in both degrees and radians; the other boards treat exact values in degrees as AS.

### E1 Trigonometric functions, the triangle rules and radians

*Refs: AQA E1 · Edexcel 5.1 · OCR 1.05a, 1.05b, 1.05c, 1.05d · MEI Mt1, Mt3, Mt4, Mt10, Mt11. Level: AS & A (part A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- Definitions of sin θ, cos θ and tan θ for any angle, using the unit circle: a point at angle θ anticlockwise from the positive x-axis has coordinates (cos θ, sin θ), and tan θ = sin θ / cos θ.
- The sine rule, cosine rule and the area of a triangle:

```latex
\frac{a}{\sin A} = \frac{b}{\sin B} = \frac{c}{\sin C} \qquad a^2 = b^2 + c^2 - 2bc\cos A \qquad \text{Area} = \tfrac{1}{2}ab\sin C
```

- (A-level only) Radians: 2π radians = 360°, so π radians = 180°.
- (A-level only) For a sector of radius r and angle θ radians:

```latex
s = r\theta \qquad A = \tfrac{1}{2}r^2\theta
```

#### Explained

**Any angle.** In the four quadrants the signs are:

| Quadrant | Angles | Positive |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1st | 0° to 90° | all of sin, cos, tan |
| 2nd | 90° to 180° | sin only |
| 3rd | 180° to 270° | tan only |
| 4th | 270° to 360° | cos only |

**Which rule?**

- Two angles and a side, or two sides and a non-included angle: sine rule.
- Two sides and the included angle, or three sides: cosine rule.
- For an angle from three sides, rearrange: cos A = (b² + c² − a²)/(2bc).

**The ambiguous case.** If you know two sides and a non-included angle, the sine rule can give two possible triangles, because sin x = sin(180° − x). Check whether the second angle, 180° minus the first answer, still fits (the angles must add to less than 180°).

**(A-level only) Radians.** One radian is the angle at the centre of a circle subtended by an arc equal in length to the radius. To convert, multiply degrees by π/180. Common values: 30° = π/6, 45° = π/4, 60° = π/3, 90° = π/2, 180° = π.

The sector formulas only work with θ in radians. A segment (the region between a chord and its arc) has area ½r²θ − ½r² sin θ = ½r²(θ − sin θ).

#### Worked example

In triangle ABC, A = 40°, B = 65° and a = 8 cm. Find b.

1. b/sin 65° = 8/sin 40°.
2. b = 8 sin 65° / sin 40° = 11.3 cm (3 s.f.).

In triangle ABC, a = 7 cm, b = 9 cm and A = 40°. Find the possible values of B.

1. sin B = 9 sin 40° / 7 = 0.8264.
2. B = 55.7° or B = 180° − 55.7° = 124.3°.
3. Both work, since 40° + 124.3° < 180°. There are two possible triangles.

Triangle PQR has sides 5, 7 and 9 cm. Find its largest angle.

1. The largest angle is opposite the 9 cm side.
2. cos θ = (5² + 7² − 9²)/(2 × 5 × 7) = −7/70 = −0.1.
3. θ = 95.7°.

Find the area of a triangle with sides 7 cm and 9 cm and included angle 110°.

1. Area = ½ × 7 × 9 × sin 110° = 29.6 cm².

(A-level only) A sector has radius 8 cm and area 48 cm². Find its angle and perimeter.

1. 48 = ½ × 64 × θ, so θ = 1.5 rad.
2. Arc = 8 × 1.5 = 12 cm. Perimeter = 12 + 8 + 8 = 28 cm.

(A-level only) Find the area of the segment cut off by a chord that subtends π/3 at the centre of a circle of radius 10 cm.

1. Sector: ½ × 100 × π/3 = 52.36 cm².
2. Triangle: ½ × 100 × sin(π/3) = 43.30 cm².
3. Segment: 9.06 cm² (3 s.f.).

#### Common mistakes

- Missing the second solution in the ambiguous case.
- Using the sector formulas with θ in degrees.
- Having the calculator in the wrong mode (degrees or radians).

### E2 Small angle approximations

*Refs: AQA E2 · Edexcel 5.2 · OCR 1.05e · MEI Mt12. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) For small θ in radians:

```latex
\sin\theta \approx \theta \qquad \cos\theta \approx 1 - \frac{\theta^2}{2} \qquad \tan\theta \approx \theta
```

#### Explained

On the graph of y = sin θ, the tangent at the origin is y = θ, so for angles close to zero the curve and the line are almost the same. The approximation for cos θ comes from the shape of the cosine curve near its maximum. These only work in radians, and get worse as θ grows: at θ = 0.1, sin θ = 0.0998; at θ = 0.5, sin θ = 0.479.

Replace each function by its approximation, simplify, and ignore powers of θ higher than those asked for.

The approximations are also the reason the derivative of sin x is cos x (file 12).

#### Worked example

(A-level only) For small θ, find an approximation to sin θ + cos 3θ.

1. sin θ ≈ θ. cos 3θ ≈ 1 − (3θ)²/2 = 1 − 9θ²/2.
2. sin θ + cos 3θ ≈ 1 + θ − 9θ²/2.
3. Check θ = 0.05: exact 1.038750, approximation 1.038750.

(A-level only) Find the limit of (1 − cos 2θ)/(θ tan θ) as θ → 0.

1. 1 − cos 2θ ≈ 1 − (1 − 2θ²) = 2θ².
2. θ tan θ ≈ θ².
3. The expression tends to 2.

(A-level only) A pendulum swings through a small angle θ. The horizontal displacement of the bob is L sin θ. Using a small angle approximation, give a simpler model and say when it is reasonable.

1. L sin θ ≈ Lθ.
2. Reasonable when θ is small, say less than about 0.2 radians (11°), where the error is under 1%.

#### Common mistakes

- Using degrees.
- Writing cos 3θ ≈ 1 − 3θ²/2. Square the whole angle: (3θ)² = 9θ².

### E3 Graphs, symmetries and exact values

*Refs: AQA E3 · Edexcel 5.3 · OCR 1.05f, 1.05g · MEI Mt2, Mt8. Level: AS & A (part A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- The graphs of sin, cos and tan, their symmetries and periods: sin and cos have period 360° (2π); tan has period 180° (π) with asymptotes at 90° + 180°k.
- Exact values of sin and cos for 0°, 30°, 45°, 60°, 90°, 180° and multiples, and of tan for 0°, 30°, 45°, 60°, 180° and multiples.
- (A-level only) The same exact values in radians: 0, π/6, π/4, π/3, π/2, π.

| θ | 0 | 30° (π/6) | 45° (π/4) | 60° (π/3) | 90° (π/2) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| sin θ | 0 | 1/2 | √2/2 | √3/2 | 1 |
| cos θ | 1 | √3/2 | √2/2 | 1/2 | 0 |
| tan θ | 0 | √3/3 | 1 | √3 | undefined |

#### Explained

These come from two triangles: half an equilateral triangle of side 2 (sides 1, √3, 2) and an isosceles right-angled triangle (sides 1, 1, √2).

Useful symmetries (in degrees; replace 180° by π in radians):

```latex
\sin(180^\circ - \theta) = \sin\theta \qquad \cos(360^\circ - \theta) = \cos\theta \qquad \tan(180^\circ + \theta) = \tan\theta
```

```latex
\sin(-\theta) = -\sin\theta \qquad \cos(-\theta) = \cos\theta \qquad \cos\theta = \sin(90^\circ - \theta)
```

Transformations from file 04 apply: y = sin 2x has period 180°; y = 3 cos x has amplitude 3; y = sin(x + 30°) is y = sin x moved 30° to the left.

#### Worked example

Find the exact value of sin 240°.

1. 240° is in the 3rd quadrant, where sin is negative.
2. The reference angle is 240° − 180° = 60°.
3. sin 240° = −sin 60° = −√3/2.

(A-level only) Find the exact values of cos(4π/3) and tan(−π/4).

1. 4π/3 = π + π/3, in the 3rd quadrant: cos(4π/3) = −cos(π/3) = −1/2.
2. tan(−π/4) = −tan(π/4) = −1.

Sketch y = cos(x − 60°) for 0° ≤ x ≤ 360° and give the points where it meets the axes.

1. y = cos x moved 60° to the right.
2. y-intercept: cos(−60°) = 1/2, so (0, 1/2).
3. x-intercepts: x − 60° = 90° or 270°, so x = 150° and 330°.
4. Maximum at (60°, 1); minimum at (240°, −1).

State the period of y = 3 tan 2x.

1. tan has period 180°; the 2 halves it: 90° (π/2).

#### Common mistakes

- Mixing up sin 60° and sin 30°. Picture the triangles.
- Giving the period of sin 2x as 720°. A horizontal factor of 2 squashes the graph, halving the period.

### Check questions

1. In triangle PQR, PQ = 12 cm, QR = 9 cm and angle PQR = 50°. Find PR and the area of the triangle. [4 marks]
2. In triangle ABC, AB = 10 cm, BC = 7 cm and angle A = 35°. Find the two possible values of angle C. [3 marks]
3. Find the exact values of: (a) sin 300°; (b) (A-level only) sin(2π/3); (c) (A-level only) cos(7π/6); (d) (A-level only) tan(5π/4). [4 marks]
4. Sketch y = cos(x − 60°) for 0° ≤ x ≤ 360°, showing where the curve meets the axes. [3 marks]
5. (A-level only) A sector has radius 5 cm and angle 0.8 radians. Find its arc length, area and perimeter. [3 marks]
6. (A-level only) A chord of a circle of radius 8 cm subtends an angle of 2π/3 at the centre. Find the area of the minor segment. [4 marks]
7. (A-level only) Show that, for small θ, (1 − cos 4θ)/(3θ sin θ) ≈ 8/3. [3 marks]
8. (A-level only) Convert 150° to radians and 1.2 radians to degrees. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. PR² = 144 + 81 − 216 cos 50° (1); PR = 9.28 cm (1); area = ½ × 12 × 9 × sin 50° (1) = 41.4 cm² (1).
2. sin C = 10 sin 35° / 7 = 0.819 (1); C = 55.0° (1); or C = 125.0° (1).
3. (a) −√3/2 (1). (b) √3/2 (1). (c) −√3/2 (1). (d) 1 (1).
4. Cosine shape moved 60° right (1); (0, 1/2) (1); (150°, 0) and (330°, 0) (1).
5. Arc 4 cm (1); area 10 cm² (1); perimeter 14 cm (1).
6. Sector ½ × 64 × 2π/3 = 67.02 (1); triangle ½ × 64 × sin(2π/3) = 27.71 (1); subtract (1); 39.3 cm² (1).
7. 1 − cos 4θ ≈ 1 − (1 − 16θ²/2) = 8θ² (1); 3θ sin θ ≈ 3θ² (1); ratio 8/3 (1).
8. 5π/6 (1); 68.8° (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/maths/10-trigonometry-identities-and-equations.md -->

## Trigonometry: identities, reciprocal and inverse functions, equations and proof

This file covers sec, cosec and cot, the inverse functions arcsin, arccos and arctan, the Pythagorean identities, compound and double angle formulae, writing a cos θ + b sin θ as a single function, solving equations, proving identities and using trigonometry in context.

### Overview

AS: tan θ = sin θ / cos θ, sin²θ + cos²θ = 1, and solving equations in a given interval (including quadratics in sin, cos or tan and multiples of the angle). A-level only: everything else in this file, and all work in radians.

#### Board differences

- AQA E4 to E9; Edexcel 5.4 to 5.9.
- OCR H240: 1.05j and 1.05o (AS); 1.05h, 1.05i, 1.05k, 1.05l, 1.05m, 1.05n, 1.05p, 1.05q (A-level only). OCR asks you to understand the geometrical proofs of the compound angle formulae (1.05m).
- OCR MEI H640: Mt5 to Mt7 (AS); Mt9 and Mt13 to Mt21 (A-level only).
- Edexcel's differentiation topic 7.4 also asks for the derivatives of sec x, cosec x and cot x; see file 13.

### E4 Reciprocal and inverse trigonometric functions

*Refs: AQA E4 · Edexcel 5.4 · OCR 1.05h, 1.05i · MEI Mt9, Mt13, Mt14. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) Definitions:

```latex
\sec\theta = \frac{1}{\cos\theta} \qquad \operatorname{cosec}\theta = \frac{1}{\sin\theta} \qquad \cot\theta = \frac{1}{\tan\theta} = \frac{\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}
```

- (A-level only) Their graphs, domains and ranges.
- (A-level only) arcsin, arccos and arctan as inverse functions on restricted domains:

| Function | Domain | Range |
| --- | --- | --- |
| arcsin x | −1 ≤ x ≤ 1 | −π/2 ≤ y ≤ π/2 |
| arccos x | −1 ≤ x ≤ 1 | 0 ≤ y ≤ π |
| arctan x | all real x | −π/2 < y < π/2 |

#### Explained

**Graphs.** sec θ has vertical asymptotes wherever cos θ = 0 (θ = 90°, 270°, ...), and |sec θ| ≥ 1, so it never takes values between −1 and 1. Its graph is U shapes above y = 1 where cos is positive and ∩ shapes below y = −1 where cos is negative. cosec θ is the same shape moved 90° along. cot θ has asymptotes where sin θ = 0 and is decreasing on each branch.

**Inverses.** sin is many-one, so it has no inverse on all real numbers. Restricting it to −π/2 ≤ x ≤ π/2 makes it one-one, and the inverse of that piece is arcsin. Each inverse graph is the reflection of the restricted graph in y = x. Your calculator's sin⁻¹ gives the arcsin value: the principal value only.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) Solve sec x = 2 for 0 ≤ x ≤ 2π.

1. cos x = 1/2.
2. x = π/3 or x = 2π − π/3 = 5π/3.

(A-level only) Find the exact values of cosec(π/6), cot(π/3) and arcsin(−1/2).

1. cosec(π/6) = 1/sin(π/6) = 2.
2. cot(π/3) = 1/√3 = √3/3.
3. arcsin(−1/2) = −π/6.

(A-level only) Find the exact value of cos(arcsin(3/5)).

1. Let θ = arcsin(3/5), so sin θ = 3/5 and θ is acute.
2. A 3-4-5 triangle gives cos θ = 4/5.

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) Writing sec θ as 1/sin θ.
- (A-level only) Writing sin⁻¹x as 1/sin x. sin⁻¹x is arcsin x; 1/sin x is cosec x.
- (A-level only) Giving an arccos value outside 0 to π.

### E5 Pythagorean and quotient identities

*Refs: AQA E5 · Edexcel 5.5 · OCR 1.05j, 1.05k · MEI Mt5, Mt6, Mt15. Level: AS & A (part A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

```latex
\tan\theta \equiv \frac{\sin\theta}{\cos\theta} \qquad \sin^2\theta + \cos^2\theta \equiv 1
```

- (A-level only)

```latex
\sec^2\theta \equiv 1 + \tan^2\theta \qquad \operatorname{cosec}^2\theta \equiv 1 + \cot^2\theta
```

#### Explained

sin²θ + cos²θ = 1 is Pythagoras on the unit circle. Dividing it by cos²θ gives the sec identity; dividing by sin²θ gives the cosec identity.

Use these identities to turn an equation with mixed functions into one function, usually a quadratic.

#### Worked example

Solve sin x = 3 cos x for 0° ≤ x ≤ 360°.

1. Divide by cos x: tan x = 3.
2. x = 71.6° or 71.6° + 180° = 251.6°.

Solve 2 sin²x + cos x = 1 for 0° ≤ x ≤ 360°.

1. 2(1 − cos²x) + cos x − 1 = 0, so 2cos²x − cos x − 1 = 0.
2. (2cos x + 1)(cos x − 1) = 0.
3. cos x = 1: x = 0°, 360°. cos x = −1/2: x = 120°, 240°.

(A-level only) Solve sec²x = 3 + tan x for 0° ≤ x ≤ 360°.

1. 1 + tan²x = 3 + tan x, so tan²x − tan x − 2 = 0, (tan x − 2)(tan x + 1) = 0.
2. tan x = 2: x = 63.4°, 243.4°. tan x = −1: x = 135°, 315°.

#### Common mistakes

- Dividing both sides by cos x and losing the solutions where cos x = 0. (In sin x = 3 cos x this is safe because cos x = 0 would force sin x = 0 too, which is impossible.)
- Writing sin²x as sin x².

### E6 Compound angle, double angle and R-form

*Refs: AQA E6 · Edexcel 5.6 · OCR 1.05l, 1.05m, 1.05n · MEI Mt16, Mt17, Mt18. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) Compound angle formulae:

```latex
\sin(A \pm B) \equiv \sin A\cos B \pm \cos A\sin B
```

```latex
\cos(A \pm B) \equiv \cos A\cos B \mp \sin A\sin B
```

```latex
\tan(A \pm B) \equiv \frac{\tan A \pm \tan B}{1 \mp \tan A\tan B}
```

- (A-level only) Double angle formulae:

```latex
\sin 2A \equiv 2\sin A\cos A \qquad \cos 2A \equiv \cos^2 A - \sin^2 A \equiv 2\cos^2 A - 1 \equiv 1 - 2\sin^2 A
```

```latex
\tan 2A \equiv \frac{2\tan A}{1 - \tan^2 A}
```

- (A-level only) Understand a geometrical proof of the compound angle formulae.
- (A-level only) Write a cos θ + b sin θ as R cos(θ ± α) or R sin(θ ± α), with R > 0.

#### Explained

**A geometrical proof of sin(A + B)** (for acute A and B with A + B < 90°).

1. Let O be the origin and P the point at distance 1 from O, on the line at angle A + B above the horizontal. The height of P is sin(A + B).
2. Draw the line from O at angle B above the horizontal, and let Q be the foot of the perpendicular from P to this line. Angle POQ is A, so OQ = cos A and PQ = sin A.
3. The height of Q is OQ sin B = cos A sin B.
4. PQ is perpendicular to OQ, so PQ makes angle B with the vertical. The rise from Q to P is PQ cos B = sin A cos B.
5. Adding the two heights: sin(A + B) = sin A cos B + cos A sin B.

The horizontal distances in the same diagram give cos(A + B) = cos A cos B − sin A sin B. Replacing B by −B gives the "minus" versions, and dividing sin by cos gives the tan formula.

**Double angles.** Put B = A in the compound angle formulae. The three forms of cos 2A come from sin²A + cos²A = 1. Rearranged, they let you write cos²A and sin²A in terms of cos 2A, which is used in integration (file 15):

```latex
\cos^2 A = \tfrac{1}{2}(1 + \cos 2A) \qquad \sin^2 A = \tfrac{1}{2}(1 - \cos 2A)
```

**R-form.** To write a sin θ + b cos θ = R sin(θ + α):

1. Expand: R sin θ cos α + R cos θ sin α.
2. Match: R cos α = a and R sin α = b.
3. R = √(a² + b²) and tan α = b/a.

The maximum value is R and the minimum is −R. This turns equations such as 3 sin x + 4 cos x = 2 into ones with a single trig function.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) Find the exact value of cos 75°.

1. cos(45° + 30°) = cos 45° cos 30° − sin 45° sin 30°.
2. = (√2/2)(√3/2) − (√2/2)(1/2) = (√6 − √2)/4.

(A-level only) A and B are acute with sin A = 3/5 and cos B = 5/13. Find the exact value of sin(A + B).

1. cos A = 4/5 and sin B = 12/13.
2. sin(A + B) = (3/5)(5/13) + (4/5)(12/13) = 15/65 + 48/65 = 63/65.

(A-level only) Write 3 sin x + 4 cos x in the form R sin(x + α), 0° < α < 90°. Hence find its maximum value, the value of x where it occurs, and solve 3 sin x + 4 cos x = 2 for 0° ≤ x ≤ 360°.

1. R cos α = 3, R sin α = 4. R = 5. tan α = 4/3, α = 53.13°.
2. 3 sin x + 4 cos x = 5 sin(x + 53.13°). Maximum 5 when x + 53.13° = 90°, x = 36.87°.
3. sin(x + 53.13°) = 0.4. The angle x + 53.13° lies between 53.13° and 413.13°.
4. sin⁻¹0.4 = 23.58°, so x + 53.13° = 156.42° or 383.58°.
5. x = 103.3° or 330.4° (1 d.p.).

(A-level only) Write cos x − √3 sin x in the form R cos(x + α), 0 < α < π/2.

1. R cos α = 1, R sin α = √3. R = 2, tan α = √3, α = π/3.
2. cos x − √3 sin x = 2cos(x + π/3).

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) Getting the sign in cos(A + B) wrong. It is minus.
- (A-level only) Using tan α = a/b instead of b/a. Write out the matching step every time.
- (A-level only) Forgetting to shift the interval when solving sin(x + α) = k.

### E7 Solving trigonometric equations

*Refs: AQA E7 · Edexcel 5.7 · OCR 1.05o · MEI Mt7, Mt19. Level: AS & A (part A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- Solve equations such as sin x = k, quadratic equations in sin, cos or tan, and equations with multiples of the angle, in a given interval.
- (A-level only) Use the identities in this file, and work in radians.

#### Explained

Method for sin(kx + c) = value in a given interval:

1. Work out the new interval for kx + c.
2. Find the principal value with the calculator.
3. Use the symmetry of the graph to find the other value in one period: for sin, 180° − θ; for cos, 360° − θ; for tan, θ + 180°.
4. Add or subtract the period to get every value in the new interval.
5. Undo: subtract c, divide by k.

Never divide both sides by a trig function that could be zero; factorise instead.

#### Worked example

Solve sin 2x = 0.5 for 0° ≤ x ≤ 360°.

1. 0° ≤ 2x ≤ 720°.
2. 2x = 30°, 150°, 390°, 510°.
3. x = 15°, 75°, 195°, 255°.

Solve tan(x − 20°) = √3 for 0° ≤ x ≤ 360°.

1. −20° ≤ x − 20° ≤ 340°.
2. x − 20° = 60° or 240°.
3. x = 80° or 260°.

(A-level only) Solve sin 2x = cos x for 0° ≤ x ≤ 360°.

1. 2 sin x cos x − cos x = 0, so cos x(2 sin x − 1) = 0.
2. cos x = 0: x = 90°, 270°. sin x = 1/2: x = 30°, 150°.

(A-level only) Solve cos 2θ + 3 cos θ + 2 = 0 for 0 ≤ θ ≤ 2π.

1. 2cos²θ − 1 + 3cos θ + 2 = 0, so 2cos²θ + 3cos θ + 1 = 0.
2. (2cos θ + 1)(cos θ + 1) = 0.
3. cos θ = −1/2: θ = 2π/3, 4π/3. cos θ = −1: θ = π.

#### Common mistakes

- Not extending the interval for multiple angles, so missing solutions.
- Dividing by cos x in sin 2x = cos x and losing x = 90°, 270°.

### E8 Proving trigonometric identities

*Refs: AQA E8 · Edexcel 5.8 · OCR 1.05p · MEI Mt20. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) Prove identities using the definitions and identities in this file.

#### Explained

Start from one side (usually the more complicated) and transform it into the other. Do not work on both sides at once, and do not start from the result you are trying to prove. Useful moves: write everything in sin and cos; use a common denominator; replace sin²θ + cos²θ by 1; expand double angles.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) Prove that (1 − cos 2x)/sin 2x ≡ tan x.

1. LHS = (1 − (1 − 2sin²x))/(2 sin x cos x).
2. = 2sin²x/(2 sin x cos x) = sin x/cos x = tan x = RHS.

(A-level only) Prove that cot x + tan x ≡ 2 cosec 2x.

1. LHS = cos x/sin x + sin x/cos x = (cos²x + sin²x)/(sin x cos x).
2. = 1/(sin x cos x) = 2/(2 sin x cos x) = 2/sin 2x = 2 cosec 2x = RHS.

(A-level only) Prove that sec x − cos x ≡ sin x tan x.

1. LHS = 1/cos x − cos x = (1 − cos²x)/cos x = sin²x/cos x.
2. = sin x × (sin x/cos x) = sin x tan x = RHS.

(A-level only) Prove that cos 3x ≡ 4cos³x − 3cos x.

1. cos 3x = cos(2x + x) = cos 2x cos x − sin 2x sin x.
2. = (2cos²x − 1)cos x − 2sin²x cos x.
3. = 2cos³x − cos x − 2(1 − cos²x)cos x = 4cos³x − 3cos x.

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) Writing "LHS = RHS" at the start and then manipulating both sides.
- (A-level only) Choosing the wrong form of cos 2x. Pick the form that cancels with the rest.

### E9 Trigonometry in context

*Refs: AQA E9 · Edexcel 5.9 · OCR 1.05q · MEI Mt21. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) Use trigonometric functions to model periodic situations (tides, temperatures, rotating wheels) and in vectors, kinematics and forces.

#### Explained

A model y = A + B sin(kt) oscillates about A with amplitude B and period 2π/k (or 360°/k in degrees). R-form helps find the greatest and least values and when they happen. In mechanics, resolving forces and velocities uses sin and cos (files 26 and 27).

#### Worked example

(A-level only) The depth of water in a harbour is d = 5 + 2.5cos(πt/6) metres, t hours after midnight. Find the least depth and the times in the first 12 hours when the depth is at least 6 m.

1. Least depth 5 − 2.5 = 2.5 m, when πt/6 = π, at t = 6 (6 a.m.).
2. cos(πt/6) ≥ 0.4, with 0 ≤ πt/6 ≤ 2π.
3. πt/6 ≤ 1.159 or πt/6 ≥ 2π − 1.159.
4. t ≤ 2.21 or t ≥ 9.79. So from midnight to about 2:13 a.m., and from about 9:47 a.m. to noon.

(A-level only) The temperature in a room is modelled by T = 15 + 3 sin(πt/12) + 4 cos(πt/12) °C, t hours after 6 a.m. Find the maximum temperature and when it first occurs.

1. 3 sin θ + 4 cos θ = 5 sin(θ + 0.9273).
2. Maximum T = 20 °C, when πt/12 + 0.9273 = π/2.
3. t = 2.46 hours, about 8:28 a.m.

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) Mixing degrees and radians in a model.
- (A-level only) Giving t as a decimal of hours when the question asks for a time of day.

### Check questions

1. Solve 2cos²x = 1 + sin x for 0° ≤ x ≤ 360°. [5 marks]
2. Solve tan 2x = 1 for 0° ≤ x ≤ 180°. [3 marks]
3. (A-level only) Find the exact value of sin 15°. [3 marks]
4. (A-level only) Prove that sin 2x/(1 + cos 2x) ≡ tan x. [3 marks]
5. (A-level only) (a) Write 5cos x − 12 sin x in the form R cos(x + α), 0° < α < 90°. (b) Solve 5cos x − 12 sin x = 6.5 for 0° ≤ x ≤ 360°. (c) Find the greatest value of 1/(20 − 5cos x + 12 sin x). [8 marks]
6. (A-level only) Solve 3sec²θ − 5 tan θ − 5 = 0 for 0 ≤ θ < 2π, giving answers to 3 decimal places. [5 marks]
7. (A-level only) (a) State the range of arccos x. (b) Find the exact value of cos(arcsin(5/13)). [3 marks]
8. (A-level only) The depth of water at a jetty is d = 6 + 3 sin(πt/6) metres, t hours after noon. Find the times in the first 12 hours when the depth is more than 7.5 m. [4 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. 2(1 − sin²x) = 1 + sin x (1); 2sin²x + sin x − 1 = 0 (1); (2 sin x − 1)(sin x + 1) = 0 (1); x = 30°, 150° (1); x = 270° (1).
2. 0° ≤ 2x ≤ 360° (1); 2x = 45°, 225° (1); x = 22.5°, 112.5° (1).
3. sin(45° − 30°) = sin 45° cos 30° − cos 45° sin 30° (1); (√2/2)(√3/2) − (√2/2)(1/2) (1); (√6 − √2)/4 (1).
4. sin 2x = 2 sin x cos x and 1 + cos 2x = 2cos²x (1); ratio = 2 sin x cos x / 2cos²x (1); = tan x (1).
5. (a) R = 13 (1); tan α = 12/5, α = 67.38° (1). (b) cos(x + 67.38°) = 0.5 (1); x + 67.38° = 300° or 420° (1); x = 232.6° or 352.6° (2). (c) Least denominator 20 − 13 = 7 (1); greatest value 1/7 (1).
6. 3(1 + tan²θ) − 5 tan θ − 5 = 0, so 3tan²θ − 5 tan θ − 2 = 0 (1); (3 tan θ + 1)(tan θ − 2) = 0 (1); tan θ = 2: θ = 1.107, 4.249 (1); tan θ = −1/3: θ = 2.820, 5.961 (2).
7. (a) 0 ≤ arccos x ≤ π (1). (b) sin θ = 5/13 with θ acute (1); cos θ = 12/13 (1).
8. sin(πt/6) > 0.5 (1); π/6 < πt/6 < 5π/6 (1); 1 < t < 5 (1); between 1 p.m. and 5 p.m. (1).

## Exponentials and logarithms

This file covers the functions aˣ and eˣ, why eˣ is special, logarithms and their laws, solving equations with the unknown in the power, using log graphs to find the constants in y = axⁿ and y = kbˣ, and exponential growth and decay models.

### Overview

All AS content. Differentiating eˣ and ln x in general (beyond the gradient of e^{kx}) is A-level and is in file 13.

#### Board differences

- AQA F1 to F7; Edexcel 6.1 to 6.7; OCR H240 1.06a to 1.06i.
- OCR MEI H640: ME1 to ME11. MEI lists the index-to-log conversion (ME2) and the values logₐa = 1, logₐ1 = 0 (ME5) separately.

### F1 Exponential functions

*Refs: AQA F1 · Edexcel 6.1 · OCR 1.06a · MEI ME1, ME8. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The graph of y = aˣ for a > 0: it passes through (0, 1), is always positive, and has the x-axis as an asymptote.
- For a > 1 the graph increases (growth); for 0 < a < 1 it decreases (decay).
- The function eˣ, where e = 2.71828..., and its graph.

#### Explained

y = aˣ is never zero or negative. y = a⁻ˣ = (1/a)ˣ is the reflection of y = aˣ in the y-axis. Transformations from file 04 apply: y = e^{x} + 2 has asymptote y = 2; y = 3e^{−x} passes through (0, 3).

#### Worked example

Sketch y = e^{−x} + 2 and state its asymptote and y-intercept.

1. y = e^{−x} is decreasing, through (0, 1), approaching 0.
2. Add 2: through (0, 3), approaching y = 2 from above as x → ∞.

#### Common mistakes

- Drawing the curve crossing the x-axis.
- Putting the y-intercept of y = 3e^{x} at (0, 1).

### F2 The gradient of e^{kx}

*Refs: AQA F2 · Edexcel 6.2 · OCR 1.06b · MEI ME9. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The gradient of y = e^{kx} is ke^{kx}.
- This means the rate of change is proportional to the value, which is why exponential models suit so many situations.

#### Explained

e is the one base for which the gradient of y = eˣ equals the value of y everywhere. For y = Ae^{kx}, dy/dx = kAe^{kx} = ky. "Rate of growth proportional to size" describes populations, money with continuous interest, and (with k negative) radioactive decay and cooling.

#### Worked example

Find the gradient of y = 3e^{2x} at x = 0 and at x = ln 2.

1. dy/dx = 6e^{2x}.
2. At x = 0: 6. At x = ln 2: 6e^{2 ln 2} = 6 × 4 = 24.

#### Common mistakes

- Writing the derivative of e^{2x} as e^{2x} or 2xe^{2x−1}.

### F3 Logarithms

*Refs: AQA F3 · Edexcel 6.3 · OCR 1.06c, 1.06d, 1.06e · MEI ME2, ME3, ME5, ME10. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- logₐx is the power you raise a to in order to get x:

```latex
y = a^x \iff x = \log_a y \qquad (a > 0,\ a \ne 1,\ y > 0)
```

- logₐa = 1 and logₐ1 = 0.
- ln x means logₑx. ln x is the inverse of eˣ: e^{ln x} = x and ln(eˣ) = x.
- The graph of y = ln x is the reflection of y = eˣ in the line y = x. It passes through (1, 0), and the y-axis is an asymptote.

#### Explained

A logarithm answers "what power?". log₂ 32 = 5 because 2⁵ = 32. Logs are only defined for positive numbers. Your calculator has ln and log₁₀ (often written log or lg).

#### Worked example

Write 3⁴ = 81 in log form, and find log₅ 125 and log₄ 2.

1. log₃ 81 = 4.
2. 5³ = 125, so log₅ 125 = 3.
3. 4^{1/2} = 2, so log₄ 2 = 1/2.

Solve ln(2x − 1) = 3.

1. 2x − 1 = e³.
2. x = (e³ + 1)/2 = 10.5 (3 s.f.).

#### Common mistakes

- Taking the log of a negative number or zero.
- Confusing ln and log₁₀.

### F4 Laws of logarithms

*Refs: AQA F4 · Edexcel 6.4 · OCR 1.06f · MEI ME4. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

```latex
\log_a x + \log_a y \equiv \log_a(xy) \qquad \log_a x - \log_a y \equiv \log_a\!\left(\frac{x}{y}\right) \qquad k\log_a x \equiv \log_a(x^k)
```

including k = −1 (so −logₐx = logₐ(1/x)) and k = 1/2.

#### Explained

These come from the index laws: adding powers when multiplying. Use them to combine several logs into one, then remove the log.

#### Worked example

Write 2 log a + 3 log b − log c as a single logarithm.

1. log(a²) + log(b³) − log c = log(a²b³/c).

Solve log₂x + log₂(x − 2) = 3.

1. log₂[x(x − 2)] = 3, so x(x − 2) = 8.
2. x² − 2x − 8 = 0, (x − 4)(x + 2) = 0.
3. x = −2 makes log₂x undefined, so x = 4.

Solve 2 ln x − ln(x + 2) = ln 3.

1. ln[x²/(x + 2)] = ln 3, so x² = 3(x + 2), x² − 3x − 6 = 0.
2. x = (3 + √33)/2 = 4.37. (The other root is negative and is rejected.)

#### Common mistakes

- Writing log(x + y) as log x + log y.
- Writing (log x)² as 2 log x. Only log(x²) = 2 log x.
- Keeping solutions that make a log of a negative number.

### F5 Solving aˣ = b

*Refs: AQA F5 · Edexcel 6.5 · OCR 1.06g · MEI ME6. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Solve equations of the form aˣ = b by taking logs of both sides.
- Solve equations that become quadratics in aˣ or eˣ.

#### Explained

```latex
a^x = b \implies x\ln a = \ln b \implies x = \frac{\ln b}{\ln a}
```

Any base of log works, as long as you use the same one on both sides. If the unknown appears in two powers with different bases, take logs, expand, and collect the x terms.

#### Worked example

Solve 5ˣ = 20.

1. x = ln 20 / ln 5 = 1.86 (3 s.f.).

Solve 3^{2x+1} = 7ˣ.

1. (2x + 1) ln 3 = x ln 7.
2. x(2 ln 3 − ln 7) = −ln 3.
3. x = −ln 3/(2 ln 3 − ln 7) = −4.37 (3 s.f.).

Solve e^{2x} − 5eˣ + 6 = 0.

1. (eˣ − 2)(eˣ − 3) = 0.
2. x = ln 2 or x = ln 3.

#### Common mistakes

- Writing log(3^{2x+1}) as 2x + 1 log 3 without the bracket.
- Dividing logs instead of subtracting: ln 20 / ln 5 is not ln 4.

### F6 Logarithmic graphs

*Refs: AQA F6 · Edexcel 6.6 · OCR 1.06h · MEI ME7. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- If y = axⁿ, plotting log y against log x gives a straight line with gradient n and intercept log a.
- If y = kbˣ, plotting log y against x gives a straight line with gradient log b and intercept log k.
- Use these lines to estimate the constants from data.

#### Explained

```latex
y = ax^n \implies \log y = n\log x + \log a
```

```latex
y = kb^x \implies \log y = (\log b)\,x + \log k
```

Compare each with Y = mX + c. Decide which model fits by checking which plot is straight. Then read the gradient and intercept, and undo the logs: a = 10^{intercept} (or e^{intercept} if you used ln).

#### Worked example

Data for x and y give a straight line when ln y is plotted against ln x, with gradient 1.5 and intercept 0.7. Find the relationship.

1. ln y = 1.5 ln x + 0.7.
2. n = 1.5 and a = e^{0.7} = 2.01.
3. y = 2.01x^{1.5}.

A plot of log₁₀ y against x is a straight line with intercept 0.48 and gradient 0.30. Find k and b in y = kbˣ.

1. log₁₀ k = 0.48, so k = 10^{0.48} = 3.02.
2. log₁₀ b = 0.30, so b = 10^{0.30} = 2.00 (3 s.f.).
3. y ≈ 3.02 × 2ˣ.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the intercept as a instead of e^{intercept} or 10^{intercept}.
- Plotting log y against log x for an exponential model.

### F7 Exponential growth and decay

*Refs: AQA F7 · Edexcel 6.7 · OCR 1.06i · MEI ME11. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Use models of the form y = Ae^{kt} (growth if k > 0, decay if k < 0), for example continuous compound interest, radioactive decay, drug concentration and population growth.
- Find the constants from given information, interpret them, and comment on the limitations of the model.

#### Explained

A is the initial value (t = 0). k controls the rate: the rate of change is k times the current value. Time to double: ln 2 / k. Half-life (for k < 0): ln 2 / |k|.

Limitations: real populations run out of food or space, so growth slows; a drug may be absorbed before it starts decaying; a model fitted to a short time may not hold for long times.

#### Worked example

A population is modelled by N = 500e^{0.04t}, t in years. Find the time to double, and the rate of growth when t = 10.

1. 1000 = 500e^{0.04t}, so e^{0.04t} = 2, t = ln 2/0.04 = 17.3 years.
2. dN/dt = 0.04N = 0.04 × 500e^{0.4} = 29.8 per year.

Carbon-14 has a half-life of 5730 years. The mass of carbon-14 in a sample is modelled by m = 80e^{−kt}. Find k and the mass after 2000 years.

1. 40 = 80e^{−5730k}, so k = ln 2/5730 = 1.21 × 10⁻⁴.
2. m = 80e^{−2000k} = 62.8.

£1000 is invested at 4% a year compounded continuously. Find its value after 10 years.

1. V = 1000e^{0.04 × 10} = £1491.82.

A drug's concentration is C = 12e^{−0.3t} mg/l, t hours after a dose. When does it fall below 2 mg/l?

1. 12e^{−0.3t} < 2, so e^{−0.3t} < 1/6.
2. −0.3t < ln(1/6), so t > ln 6/0.3 = 5.97 hours.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting to reverse the inequality when dividing by a negative number.
- Using percentage growth (1.04ᵗ) when the question says continuous (e^{0.04t}).

### Check questions

1. Solve 4ˣ = 30, giving x to 3 significant figures. [2 marks]
2. Solve 2^{x+1} = 3^{x−1}, giving x to 3 significant figures. [4 marks]
3. Solve log₃(x + 4) − log₃(x − 2) = 2. [4 marks]
4. Solve e^{2x} − 7eˣ + 12 = 0, giving exact answers. [3 marks]
5. Solve ln(3x + 2) = 2 + ln x, giving x to 3 significant figures. [4 marks]
6. Sketch y = e^{−x} + 2, showing its asymptote and y-intercept. [2 marks]
7. The population P of a type of bacteria is thought to follow P = abᵗ. A graph of log₁₀P against t is a straight line with intercept 2.1 and gradient 0.045. Find a and b, and interpret b. [4 marks]
8. A town's population is modelled by P = 2000e^{0.03t}, t years after 2020. (a) Find P when t = 10. (b) Find when the population reaches 5000. (c) Find the rate of increase when t = 10. (d) Give one reason why the model may not be suitable in the long term. [7 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. x = ln 30/ln 4 (1) = 2.45 (1).
2. (x + 1) ln 2 = (x − 1) ln 3 (1); x(ln 3 − ln 2) = ln 3 + ln 2 (1); x = ln 6/ln 1.5 (1) = 4.42 (1).
3. log₃[(x + 4)/(x − 2)] = 2 (1); (x + 4)/(x − 2) = 9 (1); x + 4 = 9x − 18 (1); x = 11/4 (1).
4. (eˣ − 3)(eˣ − 4) = 0 (1); x = ln 3 (1); x = ln 4 (1).
5. ln[(3x + 2)/x] = 2 (1); 3x + 2 = e²x (1); x = 2/(e² − 3) (1) = 0.456 (1).
6. Decreasing curve through (0, 3) (1); asymptote y = 2 (1).
7. log₁₀a = 2.1, a = 126 (1); log₁₀b = 0.045 (1); b = 1.11 (1); the population grows by about 11% each unit of time (1).
8. (a) 2000e^{0.3} = 2700 (1). (b) e^{0.03t} = 2.5 (1); t = ln 2.5/0.03 = 30.5 years, so during 2050 (1). (c) dP/dt = 60e^{0.03t} (1); 81.0 people per year (1). (d) e.g. it grows without limit, but housing and space are limited (2).

## Integration: the fundamental theorem, powers of x and areas

This file covers integration as the reverse of differentiation, integrating powers of x, finding the constant of integration, evaluating definite integrals, and using them to find areas. Integrating other functions, and the techniques of substitution, parts and partial fractions, are in file 15.

### Overview

AS: the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus, indefinite and definite integrals of xⁿ (n ≠ −1), and the area between a curve and the x-axis. A-level only: the area between two curves (and between a curve and a line).

#### Board differences

- AQA H1, H3 and the xⁿ part of H2; Edexcel 8.1 to 8.3.
- OCR H240: 1.08a, 1.08b, 1.08d, 1.08e (AS); 1.08f (A-level only).
- OCR MEI H640: Mc19 to Mc23 (AS) and Mc26 (A-level only). MEI lists finding the constant of integration from a given point (Mc21) as its own outcome.

### H1 The Fundamental Theorem of Calculus

*Refs: AQA H1 · Edexcel 8.1 · OCR 1.08a · MEI Mc19, Mc22. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Integration is the reverse of differentiation.
- If F′(x) = f(x), then

```latex
\int f(x)\,dx = F(x) + c \qquad \int_a^b f(x)\,dx = F(b) - F(a)
```

- An indefinite integral has a constant of integration; a definite integral has limits and gives a number.

#### Explained

The Fundamental Theorem of Calculus links two ideas: the area under a curve and the reverse of differentiation. If A(x) is the area under y = f(x) from a fixed point up to x, then increasing x by a small amount h adds a thin strip of area about f(x) × h. So [A(x + h) − A(x)]/h ≈ f(x), and in the limit A′(x) = f(x). The area function is an antiderivative of f.

The constant c appears because every constant differentiates to zero, so there is a family of antiderivatives. In a definite integral the constant cancels.

#### Worked example

Explain why ∫ 2x dx = x² + c.

1. d/dx(x² + c) = 2x for any constant c.
2. So every function x² + c is an antiderivative of 2x.

#### Common mistakes

- Leaving out + c in an indefinite integral.

### H2 Integrating xⁿ

*Refs: AQA H2 · Edexcel 8.2 · OCR 1.08b · MEI Mc20, Mc21. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

```latex
\int x^n\,dx = \frac{x^{n+1}}{n+1} + c \quad (n \ne -1)
```

- Integrate sums, differences and constant multiples term by term.
- Find c from a point on the curve.

#### Explained

Add one to the power, then divide by the new power. As with differentiation, first write every term as a power of x: expand brackets, split fractions with one term on the bottom, and turn roots into fractional powers.

If you know dy/dx and one point on the curve, integrate to find y with + c, then substitute the point to find c.

#### Worked example

Find ∫(6x² − 4x + 3) dx.

1. 2x³ − 2x² + 3x + c.

Find ∫(3/x² + 2√x) dx.

1. ∫(3x⁻² + 2x^{1/2}) dx.
2. = −3x⁻¹ + (4/3)x^{3/2} + c.

Find ∫(x² − 2)/√x dx.

1. Split: x^{3/2} − 2x^{−1/2}.
2. (2/5)x^{5/2} − 4x^{1/2} + c.

A curve has gradient f′(x) = 3x² − 2 and passes through (2, 5). Find f(x).

1. f(x) = x³ − 2x + c.
2. 8 − 4 + c = 5, so c = 1.
3. f(x) = x³ − 2x + 1.

#### Common mistakes

- Dividing by the old power instead of the new one.
- Integrating a product or quotient term by term without expanding or splitting first.

### H3 Definite integrals and areas

*Refs: AQA H3 · Edexcel 8.3 · OCR 1.08d, 1.08e, 1.08f · MEI Mc22, Mc23, Mc26. Level: AS & A (part A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- Evaluate definite integrals.
- The area between y = f(x), the x-axis and the lines x = a and x = b is ∫ₐᵇ f(x) dx when the curve is above the axis.
- A definite integral of a curve below the x-axis is negative. For an area that crosses the axis, split it at the roots and add the sizes.
- (A-level only) The area between two curves y = f(x) (upper) and y = g(x) (lower) is

```latex
\int_a^b \left[f(x) - g(x)\right] dx
```

#### Explained

Write the integral in square brackets with the limits, substitute the top limit, substitute the bottom limit, subtract. Show this step: many marks depend on it.

For areas, always sketch first. Find where the curve crosses the axis. Integrate each section separately and take the size (modulus) of any negative result.

(A-level only) For two curves, find the x-coordinates where they meet, and integrate "top minus bottom". This works even if part of the region is below the x-axis. A region bounded by a curve and a straight line can also be done as "area under the curve" plus or minus a triangle or trapezium.

#### Worked example

Evaluate ∫₁³(x² + 1) dx.

1. [x³/3 + x]₁³ = (9 + 3) − (1/3 + 1).
2. = 12 − 4/3 = 32/3.

Evaluate ∫₁⁴ 3√x dx.

1. [2x^{3/2}]₁⁴ = 2(8) − 2(1) = 14.

Find the total area between y = x³ − x² − 2x and the x-axis.

1. y = x(x − 2)(x + 1), roots −1, 0, 2.
2. From −1 to 0 (above the axis): [x⁴/4 − x³/3 − x²] gives 0 − (1/4 + 1/3 − 1) = 5/12.
3. From 0 to 2 (below): (4 − 8/3 − 4) − 0 = −8/3.
4. Total area = 5/12 + 8/3 = 37/12.
5. Note: ∫ from −1 to 2 in one go would give 5/12 − 8/3 = −9/4, which is not the area.

(A-level only) Find the area enclosed by y = x² and y = x + 2.

1. x² = x + 2, so (x − 2)(x + 1) = 0: x = −1, 2.
2. The line is above the curve between them.
3. ∫ from −1 to 2 of (x + 2 − x²) dx = [x²/2 + 2x − x³/3] from −1 to 2.
4. = (2 + 4 − 8/3) − (1/2 − 2 + 1/3) = 10/3 + 7/6 = 9/2.

(A-level only) Find the area between y = 4x − x² and y = x.

1. 4x − x² = x gives x = 0, 3.
2. ∫₀³(3x − x²) dx = [3x²/2 − x³/3]₀³ = 27/2 − 9 = 9/2.

#### Common mistakes

- Integrating straight across a root and getting the wrong area.
- Substituting the limits the wrong way round.
- (A-level only) Subtracting "bottom minus top", giving a negative area.

### Check questions

1. Find ∫(4x³ − 6x + 5) dx. [2 marks]
2. Find ∫(x + 3)(x − 1)/√x dx. [4 marks]
3. A curve has dy/dx = 6x² − 2/x² and passes through (1, 4). Find its equation. [4 marks]
4. Evaluate ∫₁² (3x² − 2/x³) dx. [3 marks]
5. Evaluate ∫₁⁹ (1/√x) dx. [3 marks]
6. Find the area enclosed by y = x² − 4x and the x-axis. [4 marks]
7. Find the total area between y = x³ − 4x and the x-axis for −2 ≤ x ≤ 2. [4 marks]
8. Given that ∫₀ᵏ (2x + 3) dx = 10 and k > 0, find k. [3 marks]
9. (A-level only) Find the area of the region enclosed by y = 6 − x² and y = x. [5 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. x⁴ − 3x² + 5x + c (2: 1 for two correct terms, 1 for all with + c).
2. Expand to x² + 2x − 3 (1); divide by x^{1/2}: x^{3/2} + 2x^{1/2} − 3x^{−1/2} (1); integrate (1); (2/5)x^{5/2} + (4/3)x^{3/2} − 6x^{1/2} + c (1).
3. y = 2x³ + 2x⁻¹ + c (2); 2 + 2 + c = 4, so c = 0 (1); y = 2x³ + 2/x (1).
4. [x³ + x⁻²]₁² (1); (8 + 1/4) − (1 + 1) (1); 25/4 (1).
5. [2x^{1/2}] (1); 6 − 2 (1); 4 (1).
6. Roots x = 0 and x = 4 (1); ∫₀⁴(x² − 4x) dx = [x³/3 − 2x²]₀⁴ (1); = −32/3 (1); area 32/3 (1).
7. Roots −2, 0, 2 (1); ∫ from −2 to 0 = 4 (1); ∫ from 0 to 2 = −4 (1); total 8 (1).
8. [x² + 3x]₀ᵏ = k² + 3k (1); k² + 3k − 10 = 0, (k + 5)(k − 2) = 0 (1); k = 2 (1).
9. 6 − x² = x gives x = −3, 2 (1); ∫ from −3 to 2 of (6 − x − x²) dx (1); [6x − x²/2 − x³/3] (1); (22/3) − (−27/2) (1); 125/6 (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/maths/15-integration-techniques.md -->

## Integration: standard functions, substitution, parts, partial fractions and limit of a sum

This file covers integrating e^{kx}, 1/x, sin kx and cos kx, using identities and "reverse chain rule" patterns, integration by substitution, integration by parts, integrating with partial fractions, and integration as the limit of a sum.

### Overview

All A-level only. Separable differential equations, which use all of these techniques, are in file 16. The trapezium rule is in file 17.

#### Board differences

- AQA H2 (non-power part), H4, H5, H6; Edexcel 8.2, 8.4, 8.5, 8.6.
- OCR H240: 1.08c, 1.08g to 1.08j.
- OCR MEI H640: Mc24, Mc25, Mc27 to Mc30. MEI separates substitution where the substitution is given or easy to spot (Mc27) from other cases (Mc28).
- AQA's guidance: one substitution that leads to a function you can integrate; parts may be used more than once but reduction formulae are excluded. Partial fractions in integration are limited to linear denominators (AQA H6, OCR 1.08j); Edexcel's 8.6 also covers a repeated linear factor.

### H2 Integrating standard functions

*Refs: AQA H2 · Edexcel 8.2 · OCR 1.08c · MEI Mc24. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) The standard integrals (x in radians):

| f(x) | ∫ f(x) dx |
| --- | --- |
| e^{kx} | (1/k)e^{kx} + c |
| 1/x | ln\|x\| + c |
| sin kx | −(1/k) cos kx + c |
| cos kx | (1/k) sin kx + c |
| sec² kx | (1/k) tan kx + c |

- (A-level only) Related sums, differences and constant multiples, and functions of (ax + b).

#### Explained

**Linear inside.** For a function of ax + b, integrate as normal and divide by a:

```latex
\int (ax+b)^n\,dx = \frac{(ax+b)^{n+1}}{a(n+1)} + c \qquad \int \frac{1}{ax+b}\,dx = \frac{1}{a}\ln|ax+b| + c
```

**The modulus.** ln x only exists for x > 0, but 1/x exists for negative x too, so ∫ 1/x dx = ln|x| + c.

**Using identities.** sin²x and cos²x cannot be integrated directly; use the double angle formula first (file 10). tan²x = sec²x − 1.

```latex
\int \cos^2 x\,dx = \int \tfrac{1}{2}(1 + \cos 2x)\,dx = \tfrac{x}{2} + \tfrac{\sin 2x}{4} + c
```

**Recognition (reverse chain rule).** If the integrand is a function multiplied by the derivative of its inside, you can write the answer down. Two very common cases:

```latex
\int \frac{f'(x)}{f(x)}\,dx = \ln|f(x)| + c \qquad \int f'(x)\left[f(x)\right]^n dx = \frac{\left[f(x)\right]^{n+1}}{n+1} + c
```

Adjust constants: if the derivative is there apart from a number, divide by that number. Always check by differentiating your answer.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) Find ∫(e^{3x} + 4/x − sin 2x + cos(x/3)) dx.

1. (1/3)e^{3x} + 4 ln|x| + (1/2) cos 2x + 3 sin(x/3) + c.

(A-level only) Find ∫ 1/(2x + 1) dx and ∫₀^{π/4} sec²x dx.

1. (1/2) ln|2x + 1| + c.
2. [tan x]₀^{π/4} = 1 − 0 = 1.

(A-level only) Find ∫ cos²x dx and ∫ tan²x dx.

1. x/2 + (sin 2x)/4 + c.
2. ∫(sec²x − 1) dx = tan x − x + c.

(A-level only) Find ∫ 2x/(x² + 5) dx, ∫ tan x dx and ∫ x(x² + 1)⁴ dx.

1. The numerator is the derivative of the denominator: ln(x² + 5) + c.
2. tan x = sin x/cos x = −(−sin x)/cos x, so ∫ tan x dx = −ln|cos x| + c = ln|sec x| + c.
3. The derivative of x² + 1 is 2x, so ∫ x(x² + 1)⁴ dx = (x² + 1)⁵/10 + c.

(A-level only) Find ∫ xe^{x²} dx and ∫ sin x cos³x dx.

1. (1/2)e^{x²} + c.
2. −(1/4)cos⁴x + c. (Check: differentiating gives −(1/4) × 4cos³x × (−sin x) = sin x cos³x ✓.)

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) ∫ sin x dx = −cos x, not cos x.
- (A-level only) Dividing by k when you should multiply, or the reverse: ∫ cos(x/3) dx = 3 sin(x/3).
- (A-level only) Writing ∫ 1/(x² + 5) dx = ln(x² + 5). The f′/f pattern needs the derivative on top.

### H4 Integration as the limit of a sum

*Refs: AQA H4 · Edexcel 8.4 · OCR 1.08g · MEI Mc25. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) The area under a curve is the limit of the sum of the areas of thin rectangles as their width tends to zero:

```latex
\int_a^b f(x)\,dx = \lim_{\delta x \to 0} \sum_{x=a}^{b} f(x)\,\delta x
```

#### Explained

Split the interval from a to b into strips of width δx. Each strip is roughly a rectangle of height f(x) and area f(x)δx. Adding them gives an estimate of the area; the thinner the strips, the better the estimate. In the limit the sum becomes the integral. The ∫ sign is a stretched S for "sum", and dx comes from δx.

This also explains why integrals give other totals, such as distance from velocity (file 25) or volume of liquid from a flow rate.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) Write lim (δx → 0) of Σ (from x = 0 to 2) of x² δx as an integral and evaluate it.

1. ∫₀² x² dx = [x³/3]₀² = 8/3.

(A-level only) Water flows into a tank at a rate of r(t) = 3t² + 2 litres per minute. Explain why the volume added in the first 4 minutes is ∫₀⁴ r(t) dt, and find it.

1. In a short time δt the volume added is about r(t)δt. Adding these and letting δt → 0 gives the integral.
2. [t³ + 2t]₀⁴ = 64 + 8 = 72 litres.

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) Leaving the δx out of the sum, or writing dx outside the limit.

### H5 Integration by substitution and by parts

*Refs: AQA H5 · Edexcel 8.5 · OCR 1.08h, 1.08i · MEI Mc27, Mc28, Mc29. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) Integration by substitution: the reverse of the chain rule. Choose u, replace dx using du/dx, change the limits for a definite integral, integrate in u.
- (A-level only) Integration by parts: the reverse of the product rule.

```latex
\int u\frac{dv}{dx}\,dx = uv - \int v\frac{du}{dx}\,dx
```

#### Explained

**Substitution.**

1. Choose u (often the inside of a bracket or root, or the one given in the question).
2. Find du/dx and write dx in terms of du.
3. Replace every x, including any left over, using the substitution.
4. For a definite integral, change the limits to u values. Then you never go back to x.
5. Integrate and, for an indefinite integral, substitute back.

**Parts.** Choose u to be the function that gets simpler when differentiated, and dv/dx to be easy to integrate. A rule of thumb: logs first, then powers of x, then trig and exponentials. For ln x on its own, write it as 1 × ln x with u = ln x and dv/dx = 1. Some integrals, such as x²eˣ, need parts twice.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) Use u = x − 1 to find ∫₁⁵ x√(x − 1) dx.

1. du = dx, and x = u + 1. Limits: x = 1 → u = 0; x = 5 → u = 4.
2. ∫₀⁴ (u + 1)u^{1/2} du = ∫₀⁴ (u^{3/2} + u^{1/2}) du.
3. = [(2/5)u^{5/2} + (2/3)u^{3/2}]₀⁴ = (2/5)(32) + (2/3)(8) = 64/5 + 16/3 = 272/15.

(A-level only) Use u = 2x − 1 to find ∫₁⁵ x/√(2x − 1) dx.

1. du = 2 dx, x = (u + 1)/2. Limits: u = 1 to u = 9.
2. ∫₁⁹ [(u + 1)/2] u^{−1/2} (1/2) du = (1/4)∫₁⁹ (u^{1/2} + u^{−1/2}) du.
3. = (1/4)[(2/3)u^{3/2} + 2u^{1/2}]₁⁹ = (1/4)[(18 + 6) − (2/3 + 2)] = (1/4)(64/3) = 16/3.

(A-level only) Find ∫ xe^{2x} dx.

1. u = x, dv/dx = e^{2x}; du/dx = 1, v = (1/2)e^{2x}.
2. = (1/2)xe^{2x} − ∫(1/2)e^{2x} dx = (1/2)xe^{2x} − (1/4)e^{2x} + c.

(A-level only) Find ∫ x cos x dx and ∫ ln x dx.

1. u = x, v = sin x: x sin x − ∫ sin x dx = x sin x + cos x + c.
2. u = ln x, dv/dx = 1, v = x: x ln x − ∫ x(1/x) dx = x ln x − x + c.

(A-level only) Evaluate ∫₁ᵉ x ln x dx.

1. u = ln x, dv/dx = x, v = x²/2.
2. [(x²/2) ln x]₁ᵉ − ∫₁ᵉ x/2 dx = e²/2 − [x²/4]₁ᵉ = e²/2 − e²/4 + 1/4.
3. = (e² + 1)/4.

(A-level only) Find ∫ x²eˣ dx.

1. Parts once: x²eˣ − ∫ 2xeˣ dx.
2. Parts again: ∫ 2xeˣ dx = 2xeˣ − 2eˣ.
3. Answer: eˣ(x² − 2x + 2) + c.

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) Forgetting to change the limits, or changing them and then substituting x back as well.
- (A-level only) Leaving a dx in the u-integral.
- (A-level only) Choosing u = eˣ in ∫ xeˣ dx, which makes the integral harder.
- (A-level only) Sign errors in the second application of parts.

### H6 Integrating with partial fractions

*Refs: AQA H6 · Edexcel 8.6 · OCR 1.08j · MEI Mc30. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) Split a rational function into partial fractions (file 03), then integrate each term, which gives logarithms (and, for a squared factor, a power).

#### Explained

Each A/(ax + b) integrates to (A/a) ln|ax + b|. A term B/(ax + b)² integrates to −B/[a(ax + b)]. Combine the logs at the end with the laws of logarithms if the question asks for a single log or the form ln k.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) Show that ∫₂³ (5x + 1)/[(x − 1)(x + 2)] dx = ln(125/16).

1. From file 03: 2/(x − 1) + 3/(x + 2).
2. [2 ln|x − 1| + 3 ln|x + 2|]₂³ = (2 ln 2 + 3 ln 5) − (0 + 3 ln 4).
3. = ln 4 + ln 125 − ln 64 = ln(4 × 125/64) = ln(125/16).

(A-level only) Find ∫ (2x² + 2)/[(x + 1)(x − 1)²] dx.

1. From file 03: 1/(x + 1) + 1/(x − 1) + 2/(x − 1)².
2. ln|x + 1| + ln|x − 1| − 2/(x − 1) + c.

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) Writing ∫ 1/(2x + 1) dx = ln|2x + 1|, missing the 1/2.
- (A-level only) Integrating 2/(x − 1)² as 2 ln(x − 1)².

### Check questions

1. (A-level only) Find ∫(e^{−2x} + 3/x − sin 4x) dx. [3 marks]
2. (A-level only) Evaluate ∫₀^{π/6} cos 3x dx. [2 marks]
3. (A-level only) Show that ∫₀^{π/4} sin²x dx = π/8 − 1/4. [4 marks]
4. (A-level only) Find ∫ x/(x² + 4) dx. [2 marks]
5. (A-level only) Find ∫ tan²x dx. [2 marks]
6. (A-level only) Show that ∫₀¹ xe^{−x} dx = 1 − 2/e. [4 marks]
7. (A-level only) Evaluate ∫₁² x² ln x dx exactly. [5 marks]
8. (A-level only) Use the substitution u = 2x − 1 to find the exact value of ∫₁⁵ x/√(2x − 1) dx. [6 marks]
9. (A-level only) Using partial fractions, show that ∫₀¹ (4x + 5)/[(x + 1)(x + 2)] dx = ln(27/4). [6 marks]
10. (A-level only) Explain what is meant by integration as the limit of a sum, using ∫₀² x² dx as an example. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. −(1/2)e^{−2x} (1); 3 ln|x| (1); + (1/4)cos 4x + c (1).
2. [(1/3)sin 3x]₀^{π/6} (1) = 1/3 (1).
3. sin²x = (1/2)(1 − cos 2x) (1); integral x/2 − (sin 2x)/4 (1); limits substituted (1); π/8 − 1/4 (1).
4. (1/2)ln(x² + 4) + c (2).
5. sec²x − 1 (1); tan x − x + c (1).
6. u = x, v = −e^{−x} (1); −xe^{−x} + ∫e^{−x} dx = −xe^{−x} − e^{−x} (1); limits (1); (−2/e) − (−1) = 1 − 2/e (1).
7. u = ln x, v = x³/3 (1); (x³/3)ln x − ∫x²/3 dx (1); = (x³/3)ln x − x³/9 (1); limits (1); (8/3)ln 2 − 7/9 (1).
8. du = 2 dx and x = (u + 1)/2 (1); limits 1 and 9 (1); (1/4)∫(u^{1/2} + u^{−1/2}) du (1); (1/4)[(2/3)u^{3/2} + 2u^{1/2}] (1); limits substituted (1); 16/3 (1).
9. 1/(x + 1) + 3/(x + 2) (2); [ln(x + 1) + 3 ln(x + 2)]₀¹ (1); ln 2 + 3 ln 3 − 3 ln 2 (1); = 3 ln 3 − 2 ln 2 (1); = ln(27/4) (1).
10. Split 0 to 2 into strips of width δx with area about x²δx; add them (1); as δx → 0 the sum tends to the integral, 8/3 (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/maths/16-differential-equations.md -->

## Differential equations

This file covers writing a differential equation from a description, solving first order differential equations with separable variables, finding particular solutions, and interpreting solutions in context.

### Overview

All A-level only. It draws on differentiation (files 12 and 13) and integration (files 14 and 15), including partial fractions.

#### Board differences

- AQA G6, H7, H8; Edexcel 7.6, 8.7, 8.8; OCR H240 1.07t, 1.08k, 1.08l; OCR MEI H640 Mc31 to Mc33.
- AQA's guidance notes that separating the variables may need factorising out a common factor, and that H8 includes links to kinematics.

### G6 Constructing differential equations

*Refs: AQA G6 · Edexcel 7.6 · OCR 1.07t · MEI Mc31. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) Turn a description of a rate of change into a differential equation, in pure mathematics and in contexts such as kinematics, population growth, and price and demand.

#### Explained

"Rate of change of y" means dy/dt (or dy/dx). "Proportional to" introduces a constant k. "Decreasing" usually gives a minus sign with k > 0. Read each phrase in turn:

| Description | Equation |
| --- | --- |
| P grows at a rate proportional to P | dP/dt = kP |
| T decreases at a rate proportional to the difference between T and 20 | dT/dt = −k(T − 20) |
| V increases at 20 per second but leaks at a rate proportional to V | dV/dt = 20 − kV |
| the gradient of a curve at (x, y) is the product of x and y | dy/dx = xy |

When the rate is given in terms of a different variable, use the chain rule (file 13). For example, if the volume of a sphere decreases at a rate proportional to its surface area, dV/dt = −k(4πr²), and since dV/dt = 4πr²(dr/dt), this gives dr/dt = −k: the radius shrinks at a constant rate.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) Demand D for a product falls as the price p rises. The rate of change of D with respect to p is proportional to D and inversely proportional to p. Write a differential equation.

1. dD/dp = −kD/p, where k > 0 (minus because demand falls).

(A-level only) A raindrop's mass m grows at a rate proportional to its surface area. Taking the drop as a sphere of constant density, show that dr/dt is constant.

1. m = ρ(4/3)πr³, so dm/dt = 4πρr²(dr/dt).
2. dm/dt = k × 4πr², so 4πρr²(dr/dt) = 4πkr².
3. dr/dt = k/ρ, a constant.

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) Missing the minus sign for a decreasing quantity.
- (A-level only) Writing dy/dx when the rate is with respect to time.

### H7 Solving separable differential equations

*Refs: AQA H7 · Edexcel 8.7 · OCR 1.08k · MEI Mc32. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) Solve first order equations of the form dy/dx = f(x)g(y) by separating the variables:

```latex
\int \frac{1}{g(y)}\,dy = \int f(x)\,dx
```

- (A-level only) The general solution has one arbitrary constant. A particular solution uses a given condition to find it.

#### Explained

1. Move every y term (with dy) to one side and every x term (with dx) to the other. Factorise first if needed, for example dy/dx = xy + x = x(y + 1).
2. Integrate both sides. One constant is enough; put it on the x side.
3. Use the initial condition to find the constant.
4. If asked, rearrange to y = .... When you have ln|y| = f(x) + c, write y = Ae^{f(x)}, where A = ±eᶜ.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) Solve dy/dx = 3x²y, given y = 2 when x = 0.

1. ∫(1/y) dy = ∫3x² dx, so ln|y| = x³ + c.
2. y = Ae^{x³}. x = 0, y = 2: A = 2.
3. y = 2e^{x³}.

(A-level only) Solve dy/dx = cos x / y, given y = 2 when x = 0.

1. ∫y dy = ∫cos x dx, so y²/2 = sin x + c.
2. x = 0, y = 2: 2 = c.
3. y² = 2 sin x + 4. Since y > 0 at the start, y = √(2 sin x + 4).

(A-level only) Find the general solution of dy/dx = e^{x−y}.

1. e^{x−y} = eˣe^{−y}, so ∫eʸ dy = ∫eˣ dx.
2. eʸ = eˣ + c, so y = ln(eˣ + c).

(A-level only) Solve dy/dx = y/[x(x + 1)] for x > 0, given y = 1 when x = 1.

1. ∫(1/y) dy = ∫ 1/[x(x + 1)] dx.
2. Partial fractions: 1/[x(x + 1)] = 1/x − 1/(x + 1).
3. ln|y| = ln x − ln(x + 1) + c, so y = Ax/(x + 1).
4. x = 1, y = 1: A = 2. y = 2x/(x + 1).

(A-level only) A population is modelled by dP/dt = 0.2P(1 − P/500), with P = 50 when t = 0. Find P in terms of t.

1. Separate: ∫ 500/[P(500 − P)] dP = ∫ 0.2 dt.
2. Partial fractions: 500/[P(500 − P)] = 1/P + 1/(500 − P).
3. ln P − ln(500 − P) = 0.2t + c, so ln[P/(500 − P)] = 0.2t + c.
4. t = 0: ln(50/450) = c = ln(1/9).
5. P/(500 − P) = (1/9)e^{0.2t}. Rearranging: P = 500/(1 + 9e^{−0.2t}).

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) Integrating dy/dx = 3x²y as y = x³y + c, leaving y on both sides.
- (A-level only) Writing ln y = x³ + c as y = e^{x³} + c.
- (A-level only) Forgetting the sign when ∫1/(500 − P) dP = −ln|500 − P|.

### H8 Interpreting solutions

*Refs: AQA H8 · Edexcel 8.8 · OCR 1.08l · MEI Mc33. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) Interpret a solution in context: long-term behaviour, times to reach given values, and the meaning of constants.
- (A-level only) Identify limitations of the model, including in kinematics.

#### Explained

Look at what happens as t → ∞: an exponential decay term tends to 0, so the solution approaches a limit. Check the model against any extra data and comment on the fit. Typical limitations: constants that are really variable (room temperature changes), quantities that must be whole numbers (populations), or effects left out (food supply, air resistance).

#### Worked example

(A-level only) Tea at 90 °C cools in a room at 20 °C according to dT/dt = −k(T − 20). After 10 minutes it is 60 °C. Find T in terms of t, and the time for the tea to reach 30 °C.

1. ∫ 1/(T − 20) dT = ∫ −k dt, so ln(T − 20) = −kt + c.
2. T − 20 = Ae^{−kt}. t = 0: A = 70. T = 20 + 70e^{−kt}.
3. t = 10: 40 = 70e^{−10k}, so k = ln(7/4)/10 = 0.0560.
4. 30 = 20 + 70e^{−kt}: e^{−kt} = 1/7, t = ln 7/k = 34.8 minutes.
5. As t → ∞, T → 20 °C: the tea approaches room temperature.

(A-level only) For the population P = 500/(1 + 9e^{−0.2t}), describe the long-term behaviour and find when P = 250.

1. As t → ∞, e^{−0.2t} → 0, so P → 500. The population levels off at 500.
2. 250 = 500/(1 + 9e^{−0.2t}) gives 9e^{−0.2t} = 1, t = 5 ln 9 = 11.0.

(A-level only) A particle moves so that dv/dt = −0.5v (m s⁻²), starting with v = 8 m s⁻¹. Find v in terms of t, and comment on the model.

1. v = 8e^{−0.5t}.
2. The model says the particle never quite stops, which is unrealistic; friction would bring it to rest in a finite time.

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) Giving a time without units, or to too many significant figures.
- (A-level only) Vague limitations. Say what the model ignores and how that affects the prediction.

### Check questions

1. (A-level only) Water flows into a tank at 20 cm³ s⁻¹ and leaks out at a rate proportional to the volume V in the tank. Write a differential equation for V. [2 marks]
2. (A-level only) Solve dy/dx = 6x√y, given y = 4 when x = 0. Give y in terms of x. [5 marks]
3. (A-level only) Find the general solution of (x + 1) dy/dx = y for x > −1, and the particular solution with y = 6 when x = 2. [5 marks]
4. (A-level only) Solve dy/dx = y tan x for −π/2 < x < π/2, given y = 3 when x = 0. [4 marks]
5. (A-level only) Solve dy/dx = y/[x(x + 1)] for x > 0, given y = 1 when x = 1. [6 marks]
6. (A-level only) The temperature θ °C of a liquid satisfies dθ/dt = −k(θ − 15), t in minutes. Initially θ = 85, and θ = 55 when t = 5. (a) Show that θ = 15 + 70e^{−kt} and find k. (b) Find when θ = 25. (c) State the long-term temperature and one limitation of the model. [9 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. dV/dt = 20 − kV (2: 1 for each term, with k > 0 stated).
2. ∫y^{−1/2} dy = ∫6x dx (1); 2y^{1/2} = 3x² + c (2); c = 4 (1); y = (3x² + 4)²/4 (1).
3. ∫(1/y) dy = ∫ 1/(x + 1) dx (1); ln|y| = ln(x + 1) + c (1); y = A(x + 1) (1); 6 = 3A (1); y = 2(x + 1) (1).
4. ∫(1/y) dy = ∫ tan x dx (1); ln|y| = ln|sec x| + c (1); y = A sec x (1); y = 3 sec x (1).
5. Separate (1); 1/x − 1/(x + 1) (1); ln|y| = ln x − ln(x + 1) + c (2); y = Ax/(x + 1) (1); y = 2x/(x + 1) (1).
6. (a) ∫1/(θ − 15) dθ = −∫k dt (1); ln(θ − 15) = −kt + c (1); θ − 15 = Ae^{−kt}, A = 70 (1); 40 = 70e^{−5k} (1); k = ln(1.75)/5 = 0.112 (1). (b) e^{−kt} = 1/7 (1); t = 17.4 minutes (1). (c) 15 °C (1); limitation, e.g. the surrounding temperature may not stay at 15 °C (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/maths/17-numerical-methods.md -->

## Numerical methods

This file covers locating roots by a change of sign, solving equations by fixed-point iteration (with cobweb and staircase diagrams) and by the Newton-Raphson method, how these methods can fail, the trapezium rule, and using numerical methods in context.

### Overview

All A-level only. These methods are for equations and integrals that cannot be solved exactly. The overarching theme OT2.4 (many problems cannot be solved analytically) is met here.

#### Board differences

- AQA I1 to I4; Edexcel 9.1 to 9.5 (Edexcel numbers iteration, 9.2, and Newton-Raphson, 9.3, separately).
- OCR H240: 1.09a to 1.09g.
- OCR MEI H640: Me1 to Me6, plus Mc34 (trapezium rule) and Mc35, which asks you to use rectangles to find upper and lower bounds for an integral.

### I1 Locating roots by change of sign

*Refs: AQA I1, OT2.4 · Edexcel 9.1 · OCR 1.09a, 1.09b · MEI Me1, Me2. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) If f is continuous on [a, b] and f(a) and f(b) have opposite signs, then f(x) = 0 has at least one root between a and b.
- (A-level only) How the method fails: a discontinuity (such as an asymptote) can cause a sign change with no root; a repeated root, or two roots close together, can give no sign change even though roots exist.

#### Explained

To show a root lies between a and b: work out f(a) and f(b), state their signs, and write a conclusion that mentions continuity, such as "f is continuous and there is a change of sign, so there is a root in [a, b]".

To show a root is α correct to 2 decimal places, check the sign of f at the bounds α − 0.005 and α + 0.005.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) Show that x³ − 2x − 5 = 0 has a root between 2 and 3, and that the root is 2.09 to 2 decimal places.

1. f(2) = −1 < 0 and f(3) = 16 > 0. f is a polynomial, so continuous; the sign changes, so there is a root in (2, 3).
2. f(2.085) = −0.106 < 0 and f(2.095) = 0.005 > 0.
3. The sign changes, so the root lies in (2.085, 2.095) and is 2.09 to 2 d.p.

(A-level only) f(x) = tan x. f(1) = 1.557 and f(2) = −2.185. Explain why this does not show a root between 1 and 2.

1. tan x has an asymptote at x = π/2 ≈ 1.571, which lies between 1 and 2.
2. f is not continuous on [1, 2], so the change of sign is caused by the asymptote, not a root.

(A-level only) Explain why a change of sign search would miss the root of (x − 1)² = 0.

1. (x − 1)² ≥ 0 everywhere, so f never becomes negative. The graph touches the axis at x = 1 without crossing it.

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) Giving values without stating the signs and the conclusion.
- (A-level only) Leaving out "continuous" in the conclusion.

### I2 Iteration and the Newton-Raphson method

*Refs: AQA I2 · Edexcel 9.2, 9.3 · OCR 1.09c, 1.09d, 1.09e · MEI Me3, Me4, Me5. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) Rearrange f(x) = 0 into x = g(x) and iterate xₙ₊₁ = g(xₙ). Draw cobweb and staircase diagrams.
- (A-level only) The Newton-Raphson method:

```latex
x_{n+1} = x_n - \frac{f(x_n)}{f'(x_n)}
```

- (A-level only) How these methods can fail.

#### Explained

**Fixed-point iteration.** If the sequence xₙ₊₁ = g(xₙ) converges to α, then α = g(α), which is a root of f(x) = 0. Different rearrangements behave differently: some converge, some diverge.

On a graph, draw y = x and y = g(x). From x₀, go vertically to the curve, then horizontally to the line y = x, then vertically to the curve again, and so on.

- A staircase diagram (steps approaching the root from one side) happens when g′(x) is between 0 and 1 near the root.
- A cobweb diagram (spiralling in) happens when g′(x) is between −1 and 0.
- If |g′(x)| > 1 near the root, the iteration moves away from it.

**Newton-Raphson.** From xₙ, follow the tangent to the curve until it meets the x-axis. That point is xₙ₊₁. It usually converges very quickly.

It fails or behaves badly if f′(xₙ) = 0 (the tangent is horizontal, so division by zero), if f′(xₙ) is close to zero (the next value is thrown far away), or if x₀ is far from the root (it may converge to a different root).

#### Worked example

(A-level only) Show that x³ − 2x − 5 = 0 can be written as x = (2x + 5)^{1/3}, and use xₙ₊₁ = (2xₙ + 5)^{1/3} with x₀ = 2 to find the root to 3 decimal places.

1. x³ = 2x + 5, so x = (2x + 5)^{1/3}.
2. x₁ = 2.08008, x₂ = 2.09235, x₃ = 2.09422, x₄ = 2.09450, x₅ = 2.09454.
3. The root is 2.095 to 3 d.p. (check: f(2.0945) < 0 and f(2.0955) > 0).
4. This is a staircase: the values increase towards the root from one side.

(A-level only) Show that the rearrangement x = (x³ − 5)/2 with x₀ = 2 fails.

1. x₁ = 1.5, x₂ = −0.8125, x₃ = −2.768, ...
2. The values move away from the root. Here g′(x) = 3x²/2, which is about 6.6 near the root, so |g′| > 1.

(A-level only) Use Newton-Raphson with x₀ = 2 on f(x) = x³ − 2x − 5.

1. f′(x) = 3x² − 2.
2. x₁ = 2 − (−1)/10 = 2.1.
3. x₂ = 2.1 − f(2.1)/f′(2.1) = 2.1 − 0.061/11.23 = 2.094568.
4. x₃ = 2.094551. Two steps already give 4 decimal places.

(A-level only) Explain why Newton-Raphson fails for f(x) = x³ − 3x + 1 with x₀ = 1.

1. f′(x) = 3x² − 3, so f′(1) = 0.
2. The formula divides by zero: the tangent at x = 1 is horizontal and never meets the x-axis.

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) Rounding each iterate heavily, so errors build up. Keep full calculator values.
- (A-level only) Drawing a cobweb from the line instead of the curve first.
- (A-level only) Writing xₙ₊₁ = xₙ − f′(xₙ)/f(xₙ).

### I3 Numerical integration: the trapezium rule

*Refs: AQA I3 · Edexcel 9.4 · OCR 1.09f · MEI Mc34, Mc35. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) The trapezium rule with n strips of width h = (b − a)/n:

```latex
\int_a^b y\,dx \approx \frac{h}{2}\left[y_0 + y_n + 2(y_1 + y_2 + \dots + y_{n-1})\right]
```

- (A-level only) Whether the estimate is too big or too small, and limits the true area must lie between.
- (A-level only) (MEI) Upper and lower bounds from rectangles.

#### Explained

The rule replaces the curve by straight chords. If the curve is convex (bends upwards), each chord lies above the curve, so the trapezium rule overestimates. If concave, it underestimates. More strips give a better estimate.

For an increasing function, rectangles using the left-hand heights give a lower bound and rectangles using the right-hand heights give an upper bound (the other way round for a decreasing function). The true area lies between them.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) Use the trapezium rule with 4 strips to estimate ∫₀² √(1 + x³) dx, and state whether it is an over- or underestimate.

1. h = 0.5. y-values at x = 0, 0.5, 1, 1.5, 2: 1, 1.06066, 1.41421, 2.09165, 3.
2. Estimate = 0.25[1 + 3 + 2(1.06066 + 1.41421 + 2.09165)] = 0.25 × 13.13304 = 3.283.
3. The curve is convex on [0, 2], so this is an overestimate. (The true value is 3.241.)

(A-level only) For ∫₀² √(1 + x³) dx, find lower and upper bounds using 4 rectangles.

1. The function is increasing.
2. Lower: 0.5(1 + 1.06066 + 1.41421 + 2.09165) = 2.783.
3. Upper: 0.5(1.06066 + 1.41421 + 2.09165 + 3) = 3.783.
4. So 2.783 < true value < 3.783. The trapezium estimate is the mean of these two.

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) Using the number of ordinates (y-values) as n. Four strips need five y-values.
- (A-level only) Forgetting to double the middle values.

### I4 Numerical methods in context

*Refs: AQA I4 · Edexcel 9.5 · OCR 1.09g · MEI Me6. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) Choose and use a numerical method to solve a problem in context, and interpret the answer.

#### Explained

Context problems give an equation that cannot be rearranged, such as one mixing exponentials and polynomials, or an integral with no exact form. State the method, show the iterations or table of values, and give the answer to a sensible accuracy with units.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) The number of bacteria in two colonies is modelled by A = e^{t} and B = 3t (in thousands), t hours after midnight. Find when they are first equal after t = 1.

1. Solve f(t) = eᵗ − 3t = 0. f(1.5) = −0.018 and f(1.6) = 0.153: a root in (1.5, 1.6).
2. Newton-Raphson with f′(t) = eᵗ − 3, t₀ = 1.5: t₁ = 1.512358, t₂ = 1.512135.
3. The colonies are equal after about 1.512 hours, about 1:31 a.m.

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) Giving an answer to more accuracy than the model supports.

### Check questions

1. (A-level only) Show that eˣ − 3x = 0 has a root α between 1.5 and 1.6. [3 marks]
2. (A-level only) (a) Show that eˣ = 3x can be written as x = ln(3x). (b) Use xₙ₊₁ = ln(3xₙ) with x₀ = 1.5 to find x₁, x₂, x₃ to 4 decimal places. (c) Comment on the speed of convergence. [5 marks]
3. (A-level only) Use the Newton-Raphson method on f(x) = x² − 7 with x₀ = 3 to find x₂, and compare with √7. [4 marks]
4. (A-level only) Use the trapezium rule with 4 strips to estimate ∫₁³ ln x dx. State, with a reason, whether it is an overestimate or an underestimate. [5 marks]
5. (A-level only) f(x) = x³ − 3x + 1. Explain why x₀ = 1 is not a suitable starting value for Newton-Raphson. [2 marks]
6. (A-level only) Explain why f(1) and f(2) having opposite signs for f(x) = tan x does not show there is a root between 1 and 2. [2 marks]
7. (A-level only) Sketch a staircase diagram for an iteration converging to a root, and describe a cobweb diagram. [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. f(1.5) = −0.0183 (1); f(1.6) = 0.153 (1); change of sign and f continuous, so root in (1.5, 1.6) (1).
2. (a) ln(eˣ) = ln(3x), so x = ln(3x) (1). (b) x₁ = 1.5041 (1); x₂ = 1.5068 (1); x₃ = 1.5086 (1). (c) Converges slowly: g′(x) = 1/x ≈ 0.66 near the root, so each step only reduces the error by about a third (1).
3. x₁ = 3 − 2/6 = 2.6667 (1); x₂ = 2.6667 − (0.1111)/(5.3333) = 2.6458 (2); √7 = 2.6458 to 4 d.p., so very close (1).
4. h = 0.5 and y-values 0, 0.4055, 0.6931, 0.9163, 1.0986 (1); 0.25[0 + 1.0986 + 2(2.0149)] (1); = 1.282 (1); underestimate (1); ln x is concave, so chords lie below the curve (1).
5. f′(1) = 0 (1); the formula would divide by zero / the tangent is horizontal (1).
6. tan x is not continuous on [1, 2] (1); it has an asymptote at π/2 (1).
7. Staircase: steps from the curve to y = x approaching the root from one side (1); cobweb: path spirals around the root, alternating sides (1); occurs when −1 < g′(x) < 0 (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/maths/18-vectors.md -->

## Vectors

This file covers vector notation in two and three dimensions, magnitude and direction, adding vectors and multiplying by scalars, position vectors and distances, and using vectors to solve geometric and mechanics problems.

### Overview

AS: everything in two dimensions. A-level only: three dimensions, and applying vectors to forces and kinematics (which links to files 25 to 27). The scalar (dot) product and vector equations of lines are not in A-level Mathematics; they are in Further Mathematics.

#### Board differences

- AQA J1 to J5; Edexcel 10.1 to 10.5.
- OCR H240: 1.10a and 1.10c to 1.10g (AS); 1.10b and 1.10h (A-level only).
- OCR MEI H640: Mv1 to Mv6 (AS) and Mv7 (three dimensions, A-level only).

### J1 Vectors in two and three dimensions

*Refs: AQA J1 · Edexcel 10.1 · OCR 1.10a, 1.10b · MEI Mv1, Mv7. Level: AS & A (part A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- A vector has size (magnitude) and direction. A scalar has size only.
- Notation: a (bold) or a with a line under it; AB with an arrow for the vector from A to B; column vectors; i, j unit vectors in the x and y directions.
- (A-level only) In three dimensions, k is the unit vector in the z direction, and column vectors have three components.

```latex
\mathbf{a} = 3\mathbf{i} - 2\mathbf{j} + 5\mathbf{k} = \begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ -2 \\ 5 \end{pmatrix}
```

#### Explained

Two vectors are equal if they have the same magnitude and direction, wherever they are drawn. A vector −a has the same magnitude as a and the opposite direction. The zero vector has magnitude 0.

(A-level only) In 3D, use right-handed axes: x and y as usual on the floor, z upwards. A point (x, y, z) has position vector xi + yj + zk.

#### Worked example

Write the vector from A(1, 4) to B(6, −2) in i, j form and as a column vector.

1. AB = (6 − 1)i + (−2 − 4)j = 5i − 6j, or the column vector (5, −6).

(A-level only) Write the position vector of the point (2, −1, 4) in i, j, k form.

1. 2i − j + 4k.

#### Common mistakes

- Writing coordinates instead of a vector, or the vector from B to A instead of A to B.

### J2 Magnitude and direction

*Refs: AQA J2 · Edexcel 10.2 · OCR 1.10c · MEI Mv3. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The magnitude of a vector is its length:

```latex
|x\mathbf{i} + y\mathbf{j}| = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2} \qquad |x\mathbf{i} + y\mathbf{j} + z\mathbf{k}| = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2 + z^2}
```

- The direction in 2D is usually given as an angle with i (or a bearing).
- Convert between component form and magnitude-direction form.
- A unit vector has magnitude 1. The unit vector in the direction of a is a/|a|.

#### Explained

For a vector of magnitude r at angle θ to i (anticlockwise), the components are r cos θ and r sin θ. Going the other way, r = √(x² + y²) and tan θ = y/x; draw a sketch to put the angle in the right quadrant.

#### Worked example

Find the magnitude and direction of a = 3i − 4j, and the unit vector in its direction.

1. |a| = √(9 + 16) = 5.
2. The angle with i is tan⁻¹(−4/3) = −53.1°, that is 53.1° below i.
3. Unit vector: (3i − 4j)/5 = 0.6i − 0.8j.

A force of 10 N acts at 30° above the positive x-direction. Write it in component form.

1. 10 cos 30° i + 10 sin 30° j = 8.66i + 5j N.

A velocity of 20 m s⁻¹ is on a bearing of 060°. Write it in terms of i (east) and j (north).

1. East: 20 sin 60° = 17.3. North: 20 cos 60° = 10.
2. v = 17.3i + 10j m s⁻¹.

(A-level only) Find |2i − j + 2k|.

1. √(4 + 1 + 4) = 3.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the wrong trig function for bearings: bearings are measured from north, so the east component uses sin.
- Giving the angle without saying what it is measured from.

### J3 Adding vectors and scalar multiples

*Refs: AQA J3 · Edexcel 10.3 · OCR 1.10d · MEI Mv2. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Add vectors with the triangle (nose to tail) or parallelogram law; add their components.
- Multiply a vector by a scalar: ka has magnitude |k||a| and the same direction as a if k > 0 (opposite if k < 0).
- Two vectors are parallel if one is a scalar multiple of the other.

#### Explained

AB + BC = AC: follow one route from A to C. Subtracting: a − b = a + (−b). In components, add or subtract each component separately.

If λa + μb = c, where a and b are not parallel, compare components to get simultaneous equations in λ and μ.

#### Worked example

Show that 2i − 3j and −6i + 9j are parallel.

1. −6i + 9j = −3(2i − 3j). A scalar multiple, so parallel (opposite directions).

Find λ and μ such that λ(2i + j) + μ(i − 3j) = 7i − 7j.

1. i: 2λ + μ = 7. j: λ − 3μ = −7.
2. From the first, μ = 7 − 2λ. Then λ − 21 + 6λ = −7, so λ = 2, μ = 3.

#### Common mistakes

- Adding magnitudes instead of vectors: |a + b| is not usually |a| + |b|.

### J4 Position vectors and distance

*Refs: AQA J4 · Edexcel 10.4 · OCR 1.10e, 1.10f · MEI Mv4, Mv5. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The position vector of A is OA = a, from the origin.
- AB = b − a.
- The distance between A and B is |b − a|.
- The midpoint of AB has position vector ½(a + b).

#### Explained

To find the point dividing AB in the ratio m : n, go from A a fraction m/(m + n) of the way along AB: a + [m/(m + n)](b − a).

Three points A, B, C are collinear if AB is a multiple of BC (they are parallel and share the point B).

#### Worked example

A is (1, 2) and B is (7, −6). Find AB, the distance AB, and the position vector of the point P that divides AB in the ratio 1 : 3.

1. AB = (7 − 1)i + (−6 − 2)j = 6i − 8j.
2. |AB| = √(36 + 64) = 10.
3. OP = a + (1/4)(6i − 8j) = (1 + 1.5)i + (2 − 2)j = 2.5i.

In triangle OAB, OA = a and OB = b. M is the midpoint of AB. Express OM in terms of a and b.

1. AB = b − a, so AM = ½(b − a).
2. OM = a + ½(b − a) = ½(a + b).

(A-level only) A(1, 2, −1) and B(4, −2, 11). Find the distance AB.

1. AB = 3i − 4j + 12k.
2. |AB| = √(9 + 16 + 144) = 13.

#### Common mistakes

- Writing AB as a − b.

### J5 Using vectors in problems

*Refs: AQA J5 · Edexcel 10.5 · OCR 1.10g, 1.10h · MEI Mv6. Level: AS & A (part A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- Use vectors to solve geometric problems (parallel lines, midpoints, ratios, collinear points, shapes).
- (A-level only) Use vectors in mechanics: resultant forces, velocities and positions changing with time.

#### Explained

In geometry, write each vector in terms of a small set of basic vectors (such as a and b), then compare. If xa + yb = pa + qb and a, b are not parallel, then x = p and y = q.

(A-level only) In mechanics: a resultant force is the vector sum of the forces. A particle moving with constant velocity v from position r₀ has position r = r₀ + vt after time t.

#### Worked example

OABC is a parallelogram with OA = a and OC = c. Show that its diagonals bisect each other.

1. OB = a + c. The midpoint of OB has position vector ½(a + c).
2. AC = c − a. The midpoint of AC has position vector a + ½(c − a) = ½(a + c).
3. Same point, so the diagonals bisect each other.

(A-level only) Forces F₁ = (3i + 2j) N and F₂ = (−5i + 4j) N act on a particle. Find the magnitude and direction of the resultant.

1. R = −2i + 6j.
2. |R| = √40 = 6.32 N.
3. Direction: it points up and to the left, at 180° − tan⁻¹(3) = 108.4° to i.

(A-level only) A ship starts at (−6i + 2j) km and moves with velocity (4i + 3j) km h⁻¹. Find its position after t hours and when it is due north of the origin.

1. r = (−6 + 4t)i + (2 + 3t)j.
2. Due north of the origin when the i component is 0: t = 1.5 h.
3. Position then: 6.5j km.

(A-level only) A(1, 0, 2), B(3, 4, −2) and C(4, 6, −4). Show that A, B and C are collinear.

1. AB = 2i + 4j − 4k. BC = i + 2j − 2k.
2. AB = 2BC, so they are parallel, and they share point B. So A, B, C are collinear.

#### Common mistakes

- Concluding "collinear" from parallel vectors without a shared point.
- (A-level only) Giving the magnitude of a resultant but not its direction when both are asked for.

### Check questions

1. p = 5i − 12j. Find |p|, the unit vector in the direction of p, and the angle p makes with i. [4 marks]
2. A velocity of 20 m s⁻¹ is on a bearing of 060°. Write it in the form ai + bj, with i east and j north. [2 marks]
3. a = ki + (k + 1)j and |a| = 5. Find the possible values of k. [3 marks]
4. Find λ and μ such that λ(3i + j) + μ(i − 2j) = 5i + 4j. [3 marks]
5. In triangle OAB, OA = a and OB = b. P lies on AB with AP : PB = 2 : 1. Show that OP = (a + 2b)/3. [3 marks]
6. (A-level only) A is (2, −1, 3) and B is (5, 3, 3). Find the distance AB and the coordinates of its midpoint. [3 marks]
7. (A-level only) Show that A(1, 0, 2), B(3, 4, −2) and C(4, 6, −4) are collinear. [3 marks]
8. (A-level only) Forces (2i + 5j) N and (pi + qj) N have resultant (6i + 2j) N. Find p and q, and the magnitude of the resultant. [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. |p| = 13 (1); (5i − 12j)/13 (1); tan⁻¹(−12/5) (1); 67.4° below i (1).
2. 20 sin 60° = 17.3 (east), 20 cos 60° = 10 (north) (1); 17.3i + 10j (1).
