3. k² + (k + 1)² = 25 (1); k² + k − 12 = 0 (1); k = 3 or −4 (1).
4. 3λ + μ = 5 and λ − 2μ = 4 (1); λ = 2 (1); μ = −1 (1).
5. AB = b − a (1); OP = a + (2/3)(b − a) (1); = (1/3)a + (2/3)b (1).
6. AB = 3i + 4j + 0k (1); distance 5 (1); midpoint (3.5, 1, 3) (1).
7. AB = 2i + 4j − 4k and BC = i + 2j − 2k (1); AB = 2BC, so parallel (1); common point B, so collinear (1).
8. p = 4 (1); q = −3 (1); √40 = 6.32 N (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/maths/19-statistical-sampling.md -->

## Statistical sampling

This file covers populations and samples, censuses, sampling frames, the main sampling techniques, how to choose between them, and why different samples can give different conclusions.

### Overview

All AS content. Sampling ideas come back in hypothesis testing (file 23), where a sample is used to make an inference about a population. Large data set questions often ask you to criticise a sample drawn from the data set (file 20).

#### Board differences

The four boards name different lists of techniques:

| Board | Techniques named |
| --- | --- |
| AQA 7357 (K1) | simple random sampling and opportunity sampling are named; others may be asked about in context |
| Edexcel 9MA0 (Statistics 1.1) | simple random, stratified, systematic, quota and opportunity (convenience) sampling |
| OCR H240 (2.01a to 2.01d) | carry out simple random and opportunity sampling; know and critique, but not carry out, systematic, stratified, cluster and quota sampling. Random samples may be assumed to be taken without replacement from a large population unless stated. |
| OCR MEI H640 (Mp21 to Mp25) | simple random, opportunity, systematic, stratified, quota, cluster and self-selected samples; any other method will be explained in the question |

### K1 Populations, samples and sampling techniques

*Refs: AQA K1 · Edexcel Statistics 1.1 · OCR 2.01a, 2.01b, 2.01c, 2.01d · MEI Mp21, Mp22, Mp23, Mp24, Mp25. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- A population is the whole group you want to know about. A sample is part of it.
- A census collects data from every member of the population.
- A sampling frame is a list of the members of the population that the sample is chosen from. A sampling unit is one member that can be sampled.
- Use a sample to make informal inferences about the population (for example, using a sample mean to estimate the population mean).
- Different samples can lead to different conclusions; larger samples tend to vary less.
- Use, select and criticise sampling techniques in context, including recognising possible bias.

#### Explained

**Census or sample?**

| | Census | Sample |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Advantages | complete and accurate picture | cheaper, quicker, less data to process; the only option if testing destroys the item |
| Disadvantages | expensive and slow; hard to reach everyone | may not represent the population; results vary between samples |

**Random methods** (need a sampling frame):

- **Simple random sampling.** Every sample of the required size is equally likely. Number the population 1 to N, then use a random number generator to pick n different numbers. Free from selection bias, but needs a full list and may, by chance, under-represent a group.
- **Systematic sampling.** Choose every kth member, where k = N/n, starting from a random member among the first k. Simple to do, but can be biased if the list has a repeating pattern.
- **Stratified sampling.** Split the population into groups (strata), such as year groups, and take a simple random sample from each, in proportion to its size. Represents each group fairly, but needs to know the size of each stratum and the strata must not overlap.
- **Cluster sampling** (MEI, OCR). Split the population into clusters (such as schools or streets), randomly choose some clusters, then sample within them. Cheaper when the population is spread out, but clusters may not be typical.

**Non-random methods** (no sampling frame needed):

- **Quota sampling.** An interviewer fills set numbers (quotas) of people from each group, choosing who to ask. Quick and needs no list, but the interviewer's choices can introduce bias.
- **Opportunity (convenience) sampling.** Use whoever is available at the time, such as the first 30 people leaving a shop. Easy and cheap, but unlikely to be representative.
- **Self-selected samples** (MEI). People volunteer, such as by replying to an online poll. Those with strong opinions are more likely to respond.

**Bias.** A sample is biased if the method makes some members more likely to be chosen in a way that affects the result. Examples: surveying a train station at 8 a.m. about commuting habits, or asking only one class about school dinners.

**Informal inference.** A sample statistic (mean, proportion) estimates the population value. It will not be exact, and another sample would give a different estimate. Bigger random samples give more reliable estimates.

#### Worked example

A sixth form has 500 students in Year 12 and 700 in Year 13. A stratified sample of 60 is wanted. How many from each year?

1. Total 1200. Year 12: 60 × 500/1200 = 25. Year 13: 60 × 700/1200 = 35.
2. Then take a simple random sample of 25 from the Year 12 list and 35 from the Year 13 list.

Describe how to take a systematic sample of 30 from a numbered list of 600 customers.

1. k = 600/30 = 20.
2. Choose a random start between 1 and 20, say 7, using a random number generator.
3. Take customers 7, 27, 47, ..., 587.

Describe how to take a simple random sample of 10 days from the 184 days in a data set.

1. Number the days 1 to 184.
2. Use a calculator's random integer function to generate numbers from 1 to 184.
3. Ignore repeats until there are 10 different numbers; select those days.

A researcher wants to estimate the mean time teenagers in a town spend on homework. She asks the first 40 students who leave the library on a Saturday. Name the method and give two criticisms.

1. Opportunity sampling.
2. Students at the library on a Saturday are likely to study more than average, so the estimate is likely to be too high.
3. Only one place and time is used, so students with jobs or other weekend commitments are missed. A random sample from a list of all teenagers in the town would be better.

Two students each take a random sample of 20 days from a data set and work out the mean daily rainfall. They get 1.8 mm and 2.6 mm. Explain why, and say how to get a more reliable estimate.

1. Random samples vary: each contains different days, so the sample means differ.
2. A larger sample (or combining the two samples into one of 40) gives a more reliable estimate of the population mean.

#### Common mistakes

- Calling any sample "random" just because it was not planned. Random sampling needs a list and a random process.
- Forgetting to say how a random number generator is used and what happens to repeats.
- In stratified sampling, taking equal numbers from each stratum instead of proportional numbers.
- Criticising a method without linking the criticism to the context.

### Check questions

1. Explain the difference between a population and a sample, and give one reason for using a sample rather than a census. [2 marks]
2. Define a sampling frame and give an example of one for a survey of members of a gym. [2 marks]
3. A company has 240 office staff, 360 factory staff and 120 drivers. A stratified sample of 60 is required. How many from each group? [3 marks]
4. Describe how to select a systematic sample of 25 from a list of 500 names. [3 marks]
5. Give one advantage and one disadvantage of quota sampling compared with stratified sampling. [2 marks]
6. A council wants to know residents' views on a new bus route. It posts a survey on its website and analyses all replies. (a) Name this type of sample. (b) Explain why the results might be biased. [3 marks]
7. Explain why two random samples from the same population may lead to different conclusions, and how this effect can be reduced. [2 marks]
8. A student wants to estimate the mean daily maximum temperature at one weather station using 15 days from a data set of 184 days. She uses the first 15 days. Criticise this method and suggest a better one. [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. The population is the whole group of interest; a sample is a subset of it (1); one reason, e.g. cheaper, quicker, or testing would destroy every item (1).
2. A list of all members of the population from which the sample is drawn (1); e.g. the gym's membership list (1).
3. Total 720 (1); office 20, factory 30 (1); drivers 10 (1).
4. k = 500/25 = 20 (1); random start between 1 and 20 using random numbers (1); then every 20th name after it (1).
5. Advantage: no sampling frame needed / quick / cheap (1). Disadvantage: not random, so interviewer bias is possible (1).
6. (a) Self-selected (volunteer) sample (1). (b) Only people who visit the website and choose to reply are included (1); people with strong views, or regular bus users, are more likely to respond, so the sample may not represent all residents (1).
7. Each sample contains different members, so sample statistics vary (1); use larger samples (1).
8. The first 15 days all come from the start of the period, so they may be warmer or cooler than the rest (1); a simple random sample of 15 days, numbering days 1 to 184 and using random numbers (1); or a systematic sample with a random start (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/maths/20-data-presentation-and-interpretation.md -->

## Data presentation and interpretation, and the large data set

This file covers diagrams for single-variable data (especially histograms), scatter diagrams, regression lines and correlation, measures of location and spread including standard deviation, outliers, choosing a presentation, cleaning data, and each board's large data set.

### Overview

All AS content. Calculator skills are expected throughout: you should be able to get the mean and standard deviation from raw data or a frequency table using the statistics mode. Correlation coefficients and hypothesis tests on them are in file 23 (A-level only).

#### Board differences

- AQA L1 to L4; Edexcel Statistics 2.1 to 2.4; OCR H240 2.02a to 2.02j; OCR MEI H640 MD1 to MD14.
- **Standard deviation divisor.** OCR H240 uses divisor n. Edexcel expects divisor n but accepts n − 1. OCR MEI defines the sample variance s² and sample standard deviation s with divisor n − 1. AQA's subject content does not fix the divisor (check its formula booklet and mark schemes).
- **Outlier rules.** OCR and MEI expect you to know two rules: more than 1.5 × IQR beyond the nearer quartile, and more than 2 standard deviations from the mean. Edexcel says any rule needed will be given in the question.
- **Edexcel extras.** Coding; linear interpolation for medians and percentiles from grouped data; using logarithms to change variables so that y = axⁿ or y = kbˣ gives a straight line (as in file 11); the terms explanatory (independent) and response (dependent) variable.
- **MEI extras.** Types of data (categorical, discrete, continuous, ranked); cumulative frequency diagrams (MD3); describing distributions, including skew (MD4); and diagrams that mislead (MD5).

### L1 Diagrams for single-variable data

*Refs: AQA L1 · Edexcel Statistics 2.1 · OCR 2.02a, 2.02b · MEI MD1, MD2, MD3, MD4, MD5. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Interpret histograms, box plots, cumulative frequency diagrams, frequency polygons, stem-and-leaf diagrams, bar charts and pie charts.
- In a histogram, the area of each bar represents frequency. The height is the frequency density.

```latex
\text{frequency density} = \frac{\text{frequency}}{\text{class width}}
```

- Describe the shape of a distribution: symmetrical, positively skewed (long tail to the right), negatively skewed (long tail to the left).
- Link histograms to probability distributions: the shape of a histogram of a large sample suggests a model, such as the Normal distribution (file 22).

#### Explained

**Histograms.** Used for continuous grouped data with unequal class widths. Frequency = frequency density × class width. If the vertical axis is not labelled, find the scale from one bar whose frequency you know: area (in cm²) is proportional to frequency.

To estimate how many values lie in part of a class, assume the values are spread evenly across the class and take the fraction of the area.

**Box plots** show minimum, lower quartile, median, upper quartile and maximum, with outliers marked separately. They make it easy to compare two data sets.

**Data types.** Categorical (colours, makes of car), discrete numerical (number of goals), continuous (time, mass), ranked (positions in a race). Histograms are for continuous data; bar charts for categorical or discrete.

**Skew.** For positive skew, typically mean > median, and the upper whisker of a box plot is longer. For negative skew, the reverse.

#### Worked example

A histogram has classes 0–10 (frequency 12), 10–15 (18) and 15–30 (24). Find the frequency densities and estimate the number of values between 12 and 20.

1. Frequency densities: 12/10 = 1.2, 18/5 = 3.6, 24/15 = 1.6.
2. From 12 to 15: 3 × 3.6 = 10.8. From 15 to 20: 5 × 1.6 = 8.
3. Estimate: 18.8, about 19.

On a histogram, a bar for the class 20–30 with frequency 15 is 3 cm tall. The bar for 30–40 is 5 cm tall. Find its frequency.

1. The widths are equal, so frequency is proportional to height.
2. 15 × 5/3 = 25.

#### Common mistakes

- Reading the height of a histogram bar as the frequency.
- Drawing gaps between bars of a histogram for continuous data.

### L2 Scatter diagrams, regression lines and correlation

*Refs: AQA L2 · Edexcel Statistics 2.2 · OCR 2.02c, 2.02d, 2.02e · MEI MD6, MD7, MD8. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Interpret scatter diagrams and regression lines for bivariate (two-variable) data. You do not calculate regression lines.
- Recognise when a scatter diagram contains distinct sections of the population.
- Describe correlation informally: positive or negative, strong or weak, or no correlation.
- Correlation does not imply causation.

#### Explained

**Regression line y = a + bx.** b is the change in y for each increase of 1 in x. a is the value of y when x = 0, which may have no sensible meaning in context. Use the line to predict y from x within the range of the data (interpolation). Predictions outside the range (extrapolation) are unreliable because the relationship may change.

The explanatory (independent) variable goes on the x-axis; the response (dependent) variable on the y-axis.

**Distinct sections.** A scatter diagram may contain two groups that each show a pattern, such as heights and masses for adults and children. A single line through both groups can be misleading; analyse each group separately.

**Causation.** Two variables can be correlated because both depend on a third variable (ice cream sales and sunburn both rise in hot weather), or by coincidence.

#### Worked example

For days at a UK weather station, daily mean temperature y (°C) and daily hours of sunshine x satisfy the regression line y = 11.2 + 0.6x, for data with 0 ≤ x ≤ 14. Interpret 0.6, estimate y when x = 8, and comment on using the line when x = 18.

1. Each extra hour of sunshine is linked to a mean temperature about 0.6 °C higher.
2. y = 11.2 + 4.8 = 16.0 °C. This is interpolation, so it is reasonably reliable.
3. x = 18 is outside the data (extrapolation), so the prediction is unreliable; days with that much sunshine may not exist at that station.

A scatter diagram of car engine size against fuel economy shows two clusters: one for petrol cars and one for diesel cars. Why might one line for all cars be misleading?

1. The two fuel types follow different relationships. A single line mixes them and could suggest a pattern that neither group follows. Separate lines for each group are better.

#### Common mistakes

- Claiming one variable causes the other.
- Interpreting the intercept when x = 0 is far outside the data.

### L3 Measures of location and spread

*Refs: AQA L3 · Edexcel Statistics 2.3 · OCR 2.02f, 2.02g · MEI MD10, MD11, MD12. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Measures of location: mean, median, mode. Measures of spread: range, interquartile range, interpercentile ranges, variance, standard deviation.
- Calculate the standard deviation from raw data, from a frequency table and from summary statistics.

```latex
\bar{x} = \frac{\sum x}{n} \qquad S_{xx} = \sum (x - \bar{x})^2 = \sum x^2 - \frac{\left(\sum x\right)^2}{n}
```

```latex
\sigma = \sqrt{\frac{S_{xx}}{n}} = \sqrt{\frac{\sum x^2}{n} - \bar{x}^2} \qquad s = \sqrt{\frac{S_{xx}}{n-1}}
```

For a frequency table, replace Σx by Σfx, Σx² by Σfx², and n by Σf. For grouped data, use class midpoints (the answers are estimates).

- (Edexcel) Coding: if y = (x − a)/b, then ȳ = (x̄ − a)/b and the standard deviation of y is (sd of x)/b.
- (Edexcel) Find the median and percentiles of grouped data by linear interpolation.

#### Explained

Use the median and IQR when the data are skewed or have outliers, because they are not affected by extreme values. Use the mean and standard deviation for roughly symmetrical data; they use every value.

**Interpolation for the median.** Find the position n/2. Find the class containing it. Median = lower class boundary + [(n/2 − cumulative frequency before the class)/(class frequency)] × class width.

**Coding.** Adding or subtracting a constant shifts the mean but does not change the spread. Multiplying or dividing scales both.

#### Worked example

n = 20, Σx = 340, Σx² = 6100. Find the mean and standard deviation.

1. x̄ = 340/20 = 17.
2. Variance (divisor n) = 6100/20 − 17² = 305 − 289 = 16, so σ = 4.
3. With divisor n − 1: Sₓₓ = 6100 − 340²/20 = 320, s² = 320/19 = 16.84, s = 4.10.

Grouped data: 0–10 (f = 12), 10–15 (f = 18), 15–30 (f = 24). Estimate the mean, the standard deviation (divisor n) and the median.

1. Midpoints 5, 12.5, 22.5. Σf = 54. Σfx = 60 + 225 + 540 = 825. x̄ = 15.28.
2. Σfx² = 300 + 2812.5 + 12 150 = 15 262.5. Variance = 15 262.5/54 − 15.278² = 49.23. SD = 7.02.
3. Median: position 27. Cumulative frequencies 12, 30, 54, so it is in 10–15. Median ≈ 10 + (27 − 12)/18 × 5 = 14.17.

(Edexcel) Data are coded with y = (x − 100)/5. The coded mean is 2.4 and the coded standard deviation is 1.2. Find the mean and standard deviation of x.

1. x = 5y + 100. Mean of x = 5(2.4) + 100 = 112.
2. SD of x = 5 × 1.2 = 6.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting to subtract x̄² (writing Σx²/n as the variance).
- Applying the added constant to the standard deviation when decoding.

### L4 Outliers, choosing presentations and cleaning data

*Refs: AQA L4 · Edexcel Statistics 2.4 · OCR 2.02h, 2.02i, 2.02j · MEI MD9, MD13, MD14. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Recognise and interpret possible outliers in data sets and diagrams.
- Select or criticise ways of presenting data for a given problem.
- Clean data: deal with missing data, errors and outliers.

#### Explained

**Outlier rules.**

```latex
x < Q_1 - 1.5 \times \text{IQR} \quad \text{or} \quad x > Q_3 + 1.5 \times \text{IQR}
```

```latex
x < \bar{x} - 2\sigma \quad \text{or} \quad x > \bar{x} + 2\sigma
```

An outlier is not automatically wrong. Decide whether it is an error (remove or correct it) or a genuine extreme value (keep it, and perhaps comment on it). Removing outliers changes the mean and standard deviation more than the median and IQR.

**Cleaning data.** Check for missing values (blank, "n/a"), impossible values (a negative rainfall, a 300 kg car), inconsistent units, and codes such as "tr" for a trace amount. Decide how each will be treated and say so.

**Choosing a diagram.** Box plots compare distributions; histograms show the shape of continuous data; scatter diagrams show relationships; pie charts show proportions of a whole. A diagram can mislead through a truncated axis, unequal class widths drawn with heights as frequencies, or 3D effects.

#### Worked example

For a data set, Q₁ = 20, Q₃ = 32. Is 55 an outlier using the 1.5 × IQR rule?

1. IQR = 12. Upper fence = 32 + 18 = 50.
2. 55 > 50, so 55 is an outlier.

The mean of a data set is 26 and the standard deviation is 6. Which values are outliers using the 2σ rule?

1. 26 ± 12: values below 14 or above 38.

A student cleaning weather data finds a daily rainfall of "tr", another of "n/a", and one of −2 mm. How should each be treated?

1. "tr" means a trace (less than the smallest measurable amount); it can be treated as 0 for calculations, and this should be stated.
2. "n/a" means no reading: leave that day out.
3. −2 mm is impossible, so it is an error: remove it (or correct it if the true value can be found).

#### Common mistakes

- Removing an outlier without saying why it is an error.
- Using the 2σ rule when the question asks for the IQR rule, or the reverse.

### The large data set

*Refs: all boards' requirement to use a large data set (DfE "use of data in statistics"); AQA 3.21. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Each board publishes a large data set (LDS) that you must explore before the exam, using a spreadsheet or statistical software.
- Questions may assume you know the context, the variables, their units and typical values, and the main features of the data, without explaining them.
- You may be asked to criticise a sample drawn from the LDS, clean part of it, or interpret summary statistics or diagrams based on it.

#### Explained

The data set is different for each board:

| Board | Data set | What to know |
| --- | --- | --- |
| AQA 7357 | Published on the AQA website only. For exams from 2020 it is a set of car data: details of individual vehicles (make, propulsion type, engine size, mass, CO2 and other emissions, region, and registration year, with two years to compare). | What each variable means and its units; typical values; how the two years and the propulsion types differ; missing and impossible values |
| Edexcel 9MA0 | Weather data from Met Office stations in the UK and overseas, daily readings for May to October of 1987 and of 2015 (temperature, rainfall, sunshine, wind speed and direction, cloud cover, humidity, visibility, pressure). | Where each station is (coastal or inland, north or south); units (knots, oktas, mm); codes such as "tr" for trace and "n/a"; how 1987 compares with 2015 |
| OCR H240 | OCR's pre-released set, drawn from census data on local authorities (for example, age structure and method of travel to work). | What each column counts; working with proportions rather than raw counts; regional differences |
| OCR MEI H640 | MEI's data sets. Until 2026 the exam year rotated through several sets; from September 2026 all two-year students use a single set published by OCR/MEI. | Whichever set your exam year uses: its context, variables, units and main features |

The AQA, Edexcel and OCR descriptions in this table are not stated in the specification PDFs and should be checked against the board's current data set before teaching.

Good habits with the LDS:

1. Read the metadata: what each variable means, units, how it was measured.
2. Look at typical values and the spread of each variable. Know roughly what a normal value looks like, so you can spot errors.
3. Draw a few diagrams with software and note anything surprising.
4. Practise taking random, systematic and stratified samples from it.
5. Practise cleaning it: missing values, codes, outliers.

#### Worked example

(Edexcel weather data) A student takes the first 10 days of daily mean wind speed at one station in 2015 to compare with 1987. Give two criticisms.

1. The first 10 days of May are not typical of the whole May to October period, so the sample is unlikely to be representative.
2. Ten days is a small sample, so the comparison may be dominated by chance; a random sample of, say, 30 days from each year would be better.

(AQA car data) A summary shows a car with a mass of 15 kg. What should be done?

1. 15 kg is impossible for a car, so it is an error, probably a data entry slip. Remove it from the analysis (or correct it if the true value is known), and say so.

#### Common mistakes

- Answering an LDS question without using its context, for example not knowing what units a variable uses.

### Check questions

1. On a histogram, the class 20–30 has frequency 15 and its bar is 3 cm tall. The bar for 30–40 is 5 cm tall and the bar for 40–60 is 2 cm tall. Find the frequencies of these two classes. [3 marks]
2. n = 12, Σx = 96, Σx² = 840. Find the mean, and the standard deviation using divisor n. Then find s using divisor n − 1. [4 marks]
3. Times (minutes): 0–5 (8), 5–10 (14), 10–20 (18), 20–40 (10). (a) Estimate the mean. (b) Estimate the median using interpolation. (c) Comment on the skew. [6 marks]
4. (Edexcel) Data are coded with y = (x − 50)/2. For 12 values, Σy = 36 and Σy² = 300. Find the mean and standard deviation of x. [4 marks]
5. For a data set, Q₁ = 14 and Q₃ = 22. Show that 37 is an outlier. [2 marks]
6. A regression line for daily mean temperature y (°C) on hours of sunshine x is y = 11.2 + 0.6x, for 0 ≤ x ≤ 14. (a) Interpret 0.6. (b) Estimate y when x = 8. (c) Explain why it would be unwise to use the line when x = 18. [4 marks]
7. In a town, the number of cafés and the number of road accidents per year both increased from 2010 to 2020, with strong positive correlation. Explain why this does not show that cafés cause accidents. [2 marks]
8. In your large data set, give one example of a value you would remove when cleaning the data, and one example of a value you would keep even though it is extreme. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Equal widths, so frequency ∝ height for 30–40: 15 × 5/3 = 25 (1); frequency density for 20–30 is 1.5 per 3 cm, so 2 cm ↔ 1.0 (1); 40–60: 1.0 × 20 = 20 (1).
2. Mean 8 (1); variance 840/12 − 64 = 6 (1); σ = 2.45 (1); Sₓₓ = 72, s = √(72/11) = 2.56 (1).
3. (a) Midpoints 2.5, 7.5, 15, 30 (1); Σfx = 695, mean 13.9 (1). (b) 25th value in 10–20 (1); 10 + (25 − 22)/18 × 10 = 11.7 (1). (c) Mean > median (1); positive skew (1).
4. ȳ = 3 (1); variance of y = 300/12 − 9 = 16, sd 4 (1); x̄ = 2(3) + 50 = 56 (1); sd of x = 8 (1).
5. IQR = 8, upper fence 22 + 12 = 34 (1); 37 > 34 (1).
6. (a) Each extra hour of sunshine is linked to about 0.6 °C higher mean temperature (1). (b) 16.0 °C (1). (c) 18 is outside the range of the data (1); extrapolation, so the relationship may not hold (1).
7. Correlation does not imply causation (1); both may be linked to a third factor, such as population growth (1).
8. An impossible value, e.g. a negative measurement or an impossible mass (1); a genuine extreme value, e.g. a very wet day or a very heavy vehicle that is real (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/maths/21-probability.md -->

## Probability

This file covers the language and notation of events, mutually exclusive and independent events, Venn diagrams, tree diagrams and two-way tables, conditional probability, and modelling with probability.

### Overview

AS: mutually exclusive and independent events, and using diagrams to calculate probabilities. A-level only: conditional probability with the formula P(A|B) = P(A ∩ B)/P(B), testing independence with it, and modelling with probability.

#### Board differences

- AQA M1 to M3; Edexcel Statistics 3.1 to 3.3.
- OCR H240: 2.03a, 2.03b (AS); 2.03c to 2.03e (A-level only).
- OCR MEI H640: Mu1 to Mu3 (AS); Mu4 to Mu7 (A-level only). In MEI, Venn diagrams and the general addition rule are in the A-level part (Mu4, Mu5); in OCR H240 diagrams are AS (2.03b). MR13 (modelling with probability and distributions) is also covered here and in file 22.

### M1 Mutually exclusive and independent events

*Refs: AQA M1 · Edexcel Statistics 3.1 · OCR 2.03a, 2.03b · MEI Mu1, Mu2, Mu3, Mu4, Mu5. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Notation: A ∩ B (A and B), A ∪ B (A or B or both), A′ (not A).
- Mutually exclusive events cannot happen together: P(A ∩ B) = 0, so P(A ∪ B) = P(A) + P(B).
- Independent events do not affect each other: P(A ∩ B) = P(A) × P(B).
- The general addition rule:

```latex
P(A \cup B) = P(A) + P(B) - P(A \cap B)
```

- Use Venn diagrams, tree diagrams, sample space diagrams and two-way tables.
- Link to distributions: for a discrete distribution, add probabilities of separate outcomes; for a continuous one, probabilities are areas under a curve.

#### Explained

**Venn diagrams.** Fill in from the middle outwards: put P(A ∩ B) in the overlap first, then the rest of each circle, then the region outside both. The whole rectangle has probability 1.

**Tree diagrams.** Multiply along branches for "and"; add the end results of different branches for "or". Branches from each point add to 1.

**Mutually exclusive versus independent.** They are different ideas. Two events with non-zero probabilities cannot be both: if they are mutually exclusive, knowing one happened tells you the other did not, so they are dependent.

**Testing independence.** Check whether P(A ∩ B) = P(A) × P(B).

#### Worked example

P(A) = 0.4 and P(B) = 0.3. Find P(A ∪ B) if (a) A and B are mutually exclusive; (b) A and B are independent.

1. (a) 0.4 + 0.3 = 0.7.
2. (b) P(A ∩ B) = 0.12, so P(A ∪ B) = 0.4 + 0.3 − 0.12 = 0.58.

P(A) = 0.5, P(B) = 0.35 and P(A ∪ B) = 0.65. Find P(A ∩ B) and decide whether A and B are independent.

1. 0.65 = 0.5 + 0.35 − P(A ∩ B), so P(A ∩ B) = 0.2.
2. P(A) × P(B) = 0.175 ≠ 0.2, so A and B are not independent.
3. Venn regions: A only 0.3, overlap 0.2, B only 0.15, outside 0.35.

A bag has 5 red and 3 blue counters. Two are taken without replacement. Find P(both the same colour).

1. P(RR) = 5/8 × 4/7 = 20/56. P(BB) = 3/8 × 2/7 = 6/56.
2. P(same) = 26/56 = 13/28.

#### Common mistakes

- Adding probabilities of events that are not mutually exclusive.
- Assuming independence without being told or checking.
- Forgetting the region outside both circles in a Venn diagram.

### M2 Conditional probability

*Refs: AQA M2 · Edexcel Statistics 3.2 · OCR 2.03c, 2.03d · MEI Mu6, Mu7. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) P(A | B) means the probability of A given that B has happened.

```latex
P(A \mid B) = \frac{P(A \cap B)}{P(B)} \qquad P(A \cap B) = P(A \mid B)\,P(B)
```

- (A-level only) Use tree diagrams, Venn diagrams and two-way tables for conditional probability.
- (A-level only) A and B are independent if and only if P(A | B) = P(A) (equivalently P(B | A) = P(B)).

#### Explained

"Given B" shrinks the sample space to B. On a Venn diagram, P(A | B) is the overlap divided by the whole of circle B. In a two-way table, it is the cell divided by the row (or column) total for B.

On a tree diagram, the second set of branches already are conditional probabilities. To reverse the condition (find P(first | second)), work out P(second) by adding all routes to it, then divide.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) Using P(A) = 0.5, P(B) = 0.35, P(A ∩ B) = 0.2 from above, find P(A | B) and P(B | A′).

1. P(A | B) = 0.2/0.35 = 4/7.
2. P(B ∩ A′) = 0.15 and P(A′) = 0.5, so P(B | A′) = 0.3.

(A-level only) 120 students: 55 take Physics. Of the 50 who take Chemistry, 30 also take Physics. One student is picked at random. Find P(Physics | Chemistry) and decide whether taking the two subjects is independent.

1. P(Physics | Chemistry) = 30/50 = 0.6.
2. P(Physics) = 55/120 = 0.458. These differ, so the events are not independent.

(A-level only) In the counter example (5 red, 3 blue, two taken without replacement), find P(first red | both the same colour).

1. P(RR) = 20/56. P(same) = 26/56.
2. P(first red | same) = (20/56)/(26/56) = 10/13.

(A-level only) 2% of people have a condition. A test is positive for 95% of people with the condition and for 10% of people without it. Find the probability that someone who tests positive has the condition.

1. P(condition and positive) = 0.02 × 0.95 = 0.019.
2. P(no condition and positive) = 0.98 × 0.10 = 0.098.
3. P(positive) = 0.117.
4. P(condition | positive) = 0.019/0.117 = 0.162.
5. Most positive results are false positives, because the condition is rare.

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) Confusing P(A | B) with P(B | A).
- (A-level only) Dividing by P(A) instead of P(B) in P(A | B).
- (A-level only) Using the "with replacement" probabilities when items are not replaced.

### M3 Modelling with probability

*Refs: AQA M3 · Edexcel Statistics 3.3 · OCR 2.03e · MEI MR13. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) Use probability to model real situations, criticise the assumptions made (such as independence or equally likely outcomes), and judge the likely effect of more realistic assumptions.

#### Explained

Probability models rely on assumptions that are rarely exactly true. The probability that a train is late may not be the same every day (it depends on weather and time of day), and whether it is late today may affect tomorrow (staff shortages last several days). If an assumption fails, say how the answer would change: assuming independence when bad days cluster together usually underestimates the chance of several bad days in a row.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) A model says each day independently has a probability of 0.2 of rain. (a) Find the probability of no rain in a 7-day week. (b) Criticise the model.

1. (a) 0.8⁷ = 0.210.
2. (b) Rain is more likely in some seasons, so 0.2 will not suit every week. Wet days tend to come in runs, so days are not independent; the chance of a fully dry week is probably higher than the model says, because dry spells also cluster.

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) Criticising a model without reference to the context.

### Check questions

1. P(A) = 0.3, P(B) = 0.45 and A, B are independent. Find P(A ∩ B), P(A ∪ B) and P(A′ ∩ B). [4 marks]
2. In a class of 30, 18 play football, 12 play tennis and 5 play both. (a) Draw a Venn diagram. (b) Find the probability that a student picked at random plays neither. (c) (A-level only) Find P(tennis | football). [5 marks]
3. A bag has 4 green and 6 yellow balls. Three are taken without replacement. Find the probability that exactly two are green. [3 marks]
4. (A-level only) P(A) = 0.6, P(B | A) = 0.3 and P(B | A′) = 0.5. Find P(B) and P(A | B). [5 marks]
5. (A-level only) Events C and D have P(C) = 0.4, P(D) = 0.25 and P(C | D) = 0.4. Are C and D independent? Explain. [2 marks]
6. (A-level only) A factory has two machines. Machine X makes 70% of items and 3% of its items are faulty. Machine Y makes the rest and 5% of its items are faulty. An item is found to be faulty. Find the probability it came from machine Y. [4 marks]
7. (A-level only) A model assumes that each of 10 buses arrives on time independently with probability 0.9. Give one reason why independence might not hold. [1 mark]

### Mark scheme

1. P(A ∩ B) = 0.135 (1); P(A ∪ B) = 0.3 + 0.45 − 0.135 = 0.615 (1); P(A′ ∩ B) = 0.45 − 0.135 (1) = 0.315 (1).
2. (a) Football only 13, both 5, tennis only 7, neither 5 (2). (b) 5/30 = 1/6 (1). (c) 5/18 (2).
3. Arrangements GGY: 3 orders (1); each 4/10 × 3/9 × 6/8 = 72/720 (1); total 216/720 = 0.3 (1).
4. P(A ∩ B) = 0.18 (1); P(A′ ∩ B) = 0.4 × 0.5 = 0.2 (1); P(B) = 0.38 (1); P(A | B) = 0.18/0.38 (1) = 9/19 or 0.474 (1).
5. P(C | D) = 0.4 = P(C) (1); so independent (1).
6. P(Y and faulty) = 0.3 × 0.05 = 0.015 (1); P(faulty) = 0.021 + 0.015 = 0.036 (1); P(Y | faulty) = 0.015/0.036 (1) = 0.417 (1).
7. e.g. traffic delays affect several buses at once, so one being late makes others more likely to be late (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/maths/22-statistical-distributions.md -->

## Statistical distributions: binomial and Normal

This file covers discrete probability distributions, the binomial distribution as a model, the Normal distribution as a model, standardising and inverse Normal problems, the Normal approximation to the binomial, and choosing a suitable distribution.

### Overview

AS: simple discrete distributions (including the discrete uniform distribution) and the binomial distribution. A-level only: the Normal distribution and its links to histograms, the binomial and points of inflection, and selecting between models. Use your calculator's binomial and Normal functions throughout.

#### Board differences

- AQA N1 to N3; Edexcel Statistics 4.1 to 4.3.
- OCR H240: 2.04a to 2.04c (AS); 2.04d (mean np) and 2.04e to 2.04h (A-level only). OCR expects the facts that about two-thirds of values lie within one standard deviation of the mean, about 95% within two, and almost all within three; the equation of the Normal curve is excluded.
- OCR MEI H640: MR1 to MR7 (AS, including mean = np and expected frequencies); MR8 to MR13 (A-level only). MEI includes the continuity correction and recognising from its shape when a binomial can be approximated by a Normal, but excludes knowing formal conditions for that approximation.
- AQA's N1 excludes calculating the mean and variance of general discrete random variables.
- Edexcel 4.1 names the discrete uniform distribution; Edexcel 4.2 includes approximating B(n, p) by N(np, np(1 − p)) with a continuity correction.

### N1 Discrete distributions and the binomial distribution

*Refs: AQA N1 · Edexcel Statistics 4.1 · OCR 2.04a, 2.04b, 2.04c, 2.04d · MEI MR1, MR2, MR3, MR4, MR5, MR6, MR7. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- A discrete random variable X takes separate values, each with a probability; the probabilities add to 1. It can be given as a table or a formula (probability function).
- The discrete uniform distribution: n equally likely values.
- The binomial distribution X ~ B(n, p) models the number of successes in n trials when:
  - there is a fixed number n of trials;
  - each trial has two outcomes (success or failure);
  - the probability of success p is the same for every trial;
  - the trials are independent.

```latex
P(X = r) = \binom{n}{r} p^r (1-p)^{n-r}, \quad r = 0, 1, \dots, n
```

- Find P(X = r), P(X ≤ r) and other cumulative probabilities with a calculator.
- (OCR: A-level only; MEI: AS) The mean of B(n, p) is np. Expected frequencies are n × probability.

#### Explained

**Cumulative probabilities.** Calculators give P(X ≤ r). Convert other forms:

| Want | Use |
| --- | --- |
| P(X < r) | P(X ≤ r − 1) |
| P(X ≥ r) | 1 − P(X ≤ r − 1) |
| P(X > r) | 1 − P(X ≤ r) |
| P(a ≤ X ≤ b) | P(X ≤ b) − P(X ≤ a − 1) |

**Criticising a binomial model.** Ask whether each condition really holds. Selecting people from a small group without replacement changes p; seeds planted in the same tray may not grow independently.

#### Worked example

X takes values 1, 2, 3, 4 with P(X = x) = kx. Find k and P(X ≥ 3).

1. k(1 + 2 + 3 + 4) = 1, so k = 1/10.
2. P(X ≥ 3) = 3/10 + 4/10 = 0.7.

X ~ B(12, 0.3). Find P(X = 4), P(X ≤ 4), P(X > 5) and P(2 ≤ X ≤ 6).

1. P(X = 4) = ¹²C₄ × 0.3⁴ × 0.7⁸ = 0.2311.
2. P(X ≤ 4) = 0.7237.
3. P(X > 5) = 1 − P(X ≤ 5) = 1 − 0.8822 = 0.1178.
4. P(2 ≤ X ≤ 6) = P(X ≤ 6) − P(X ≤ 1) = 0.9614 − 0.0850 = 0.8764.

15% of light bulbs from a factory are faulty. A box holds 20 bulbs. State a suitable model, find the probability that exactly 3 are faulty and that at least 5 are faulty, and state the expected number of faulty bulbs.

1. X ~ B(20, 0.15), assuming bulbs are faulty independently with the same probability.
2. P(X = 3) = 0.2428.
3. P(X ≥ 5) = 1 − P(X ≤ 4) = 0.1702.
4. Expected number: 20 × 0.15 = 3.

#### Common mistakes

- Using P(X ≤ 5) for P(X < 5).
- Using the binomial when trials are not independent or p changes.

### N2 The Normal distribution

*Refs: AQA N2 · Edexcel Statistics 4.2 · OCR 2.04e, 2.04f, 2.04g · MEI MR8, MR9, MR10, MR11, MR12. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) X ~ N(μ, σ²) is a continuous distribution: bell-shaped, symmetrical about μ, with points of inflection at μ ± σ. Probability is area under the curve, and the total area is 1.
- (A-level only) About 68% of values lie within μ ± σ, 95% within μ ± 2σ and 99.7% within μ ± 3σ.
- (A-level only) Standardise with

```latex
Z = \frac{X - \mu}{\sigma}, \qquad Z \sim N(0, 1)
```

- (A-level only) Find probabilities, and use the inverse Normal to find values or unknown μ and σ.
- (A-level only) Link to histograms of real data, and approximate a binomial B(n, p) by N(np, np(1 − p)) when n is large and p is near 0.5, using a continuity correction.

#### Explained

For a continuous distribution, P(X = a) = 0, so P(X < a) and P(X ≤ a) are the same.

**Finding μ or σ.** Write each piece of information as (x − μ)/σ = z, using the inverse Normal on the standard Normal to find z. One unknown needs one equation; two unknowns need two simultaneous equations.

**Normal approximation.** A binomial with large n has a roughly bell-shaped histogram. Each integer value r is represented by the interval r − 0.5 to r + 0.5 under the Normal curve (the continuity correction). For example, P(X ≤ 35) ≈ P(Y < 35.5) and P(X ≥ 45) ≈ P(Y > 44.5).

**Is a Normal model sensible?** Check the data are roughly symmetrical and bell-shaped, that about 95% lie within two standard deviations, and that the model does not give large probabilities to impossible values (such as negative lengths).

#### Worked example

(A-level only) X ~ N(50, 16). Find P(X < 56), P(45 < X < 55) and the value a with P(X > a) = 0.1.

1. σ = 4. P(X < 56) = P(Z < 1.5) = 0.9332.
2. P(45 < X < 55) = P(−1.25 < Z < 1.25) = 0.7887.
3. P(X > a) = 0.1, so (a − 50)/4 = 1.2816, a = 55.13.

(A-level only) X is Normal with P(X < 30) = 0.2 and P(X > 50) = 0.1. Find μ and σ.

1. (30 − μ)/σ = −0.8416 and (50 − μ)/σ = 1.2816.
2. Subtract: 20/σ = 2.1232, so σ = 9.42.
3. μ = 30 + 0.8416 × 9.42 = 37.9.

(A-level only) X ~ B(100, 0.4). Use a Normal approximation to estimate P(X ≤ 35).

1. np = 40, np(1 − p) = 24. Y ~ N(40, 24).
2. P(X ≤ 35) ≈ P(Y < 35.5) = P(Z < −0.9186) = 0.1792.
3. (The exact binomial value is 0.1795, so the approximation is very good.)

(A-level only) The lengths of 200 leaves have a symmetrical, bell-shaped histogram with mean 8.2 cm and standard deviation 1.1 cm. Explain why N(8.2, 1.1²) is a suitable model, and use the histogram to find the points of inflection.

1. The shape is symmetrical and bell-shaped, matching a Normal curve.
2. Points of inflection at 8.2 ± 1.1, that is 7.1 cm and 9.3 cm.

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) Using σ² instead of σ when standardising: N(50, 16) has σ = 4.
- (A-level only) Getting the sign of z wrong for values below the mean.
- (A-level only) Leaving out the continuity correction in a Normal approximation.

### N3 Choosing a distribution

*Refs: AQA N3 · Edexcel Statistics 4.3 · OCR 2.04h · MEI MR13. Level: AS & A (part A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- Choose a suitable distribution for a context, with reasons, including recognising when a binomial model is not appropriate.
- (A-level only) Recognise when a Normal model is not appropriate, and choose between binomial and Normal models.

#### Explained

| Situation | Model |
| --- | --- |
| a count of successes in a fixed number of independent trials, constant p | binomial |
| a continuous measurement, symmetrical and bell-shaped | Normal |
| several equally likely discrete outcomes | discrete uniform |

A Normal model is not appropriate for strongly skewed data (such as incomes or waiting times), or where it would give a large probability to impossible values. A binomial model is not appropriate when the number of trials is not fixed, or the trials affect each other.

#### Worked example

Suggest a model for each, with a reason: (a) the number of sixes in 30 rolls of a fair die; (b) the heights of adult women in the UK; (c) the number of customers arriving at a shop in an hour.

1. (a) B(30, 1/6): fixed number of independent trials, constant probability.
2. (b) (A-level only) Normal: a continuous measurement that is roughly symmetrical about a mean.
3. (c) Not binomial: there is no fixed number of trials. (A different model, met in Further Mathematics, is used.)

(A-level only) Waiting times at a clinic have mean 12 minutes and standard deviation 9 minutes. Explain why a Normal model is unsuitable.

1. Under N(12, 9²), P(T < 0) = P(Z < −1.33) ≈ 0.09, a large probability for an impossible negative time. Waiting times are also likely to be positively skewed.

#### Common mistakes

- Choosing a model without giving a reason linked to the context.

### Check questions

1. P(X = x) = k(x + 1) for x = 0, 1, 2, 3. Find k and P(X ≥ 2). [3 marks]
2. X ~ B(20, 0.15). Find (a) P(X = 3); (b) P(X ≤ 2); (c) P(X ≥ 5). [4 marks]
3. A student says the number of girls in a family of 4 children follows B(4, 0.5). Give one reason why this model may not be suitable. [1 mark]
4. (A-level only) X ~ N(120, 25). Find P(X > 128) and P(110 < X < 125). [4 marks]
5. (A-level only) The masses of bags of flour are Normal with mean 500 g. 5% of bags weigh less than 490 g. Find the standard deviation. [3 marks]
6. (A-level only) X is Normal with P(X < 20) = 0.3 and P(X > 35) = 0.15. Find μ and σ. [5 marks]
7. (A-level only) X ~ B(80, 0.5). Use a suitable approximation to estimate P(X ≥ 45), stating the distribution you use. [4 marks]
8. (A-level only) Give two features of a histogram of data that would suggest a Normal model is suitable. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. 10k = 1, k = 0.1 (1); P(X = 2) + P(X = 3) = 0.3 + 0.4 (1); = 0.7 (1).
2. (a) 0.2428 (1). (b) 0.4049 (1). (c) 1 − P(X ≤ 4) (1) = 0.1702 (1).
3. e.g. the sexes of children in one family may not be independent, or the probability of a girl is not exactly 0.5 (1).
4. σ = 5 (1); P(Z > 1.6) = 0.0548 (1); P(−2 < Z < 1) (1) = 0.8186 (1).
5. (490 − 500)/σ = −1.6449 (2); σ = 6.08 g (1).
6. (20 − μ)/σ = −0.5244 (1); (35 − μ)/σ = 1.0364 (1); 15 = 1.5608σ (1); σ = 9.61 (1); μ = 25.0 (1).
7. N(40, 20) (1); continuity correction: P(Y > 44.5) (1); P(Z > 1.006) (1) = 0.157 (1).
8. Symmetrical (1); bell-shaped / single peak, with about 95% of values within 2 standard deviations of the mean (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/maths/23-hypothesis-testing.md -->

## Statistical hypothesis testing

This file covers the language of hypothesis testing, binomial tests for a proportion (one-tailed and two-tailed, with critical regions and p-values), tests for the mean of a Normal distribution with known variance, and tests for correlation using a given correlation coefficient.

### Overview

AS: the language (null and alternative hypotheses, significance level, test statistic, one- and two-tailed tests, critical value, critical and acceptance regions, p-value) and the binomial test. A-level only: the distribution of the sample mean, the Normal test for a mean, and correlation tests.

#### Board differences

- AQA O1 to O3; Edexcel Statistics 5.1 to 5.3.
- OCR H240: 2.05a to 2.05c (AS); 2.05d to 2.05g (A-level only).
- OCR MEI H640: MH1 to MH6 (AS); MH7 to MH11 (A-level only).
- All boards: you are not asked to calculate a correlation coefficient. You interpret a given coefficient against a given critical value or p-value. OCR and MEI refer to Pearson's product-moment correlation coefficient; AQA and Edexcel refer to correlation coefficients as measures of how close points lie to a straight line.

### O1 The language of hypothesis testing; correlation tests

*Refs: AQA O1 · Edexcel Statistics 5.1 · OCR 2.05a, 2.05f, 2.05g · MEI MH1, MH2, MH4, MH10, MH11. Level: AS & A (part A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- **Null hypothesis H₀**: the assumption being tested, stating a parameter value (for example p = 0.3).
- **Alternative hypothesis H₁**: what you accept if the evidence is strong enough (p > 0.3, p < 0.3, or p ≠ 0.3).
- **One-tailed test**: H₁ has > or <. **Two-tailed test**: H₁ has ≠; split the significance level between the two tails.
- **Significance level**: the probability of rejecting H₀ when it is in fact true; the threshold for "strong enough evidence".
- **Test statistic**: the value calculated from the sample (for example the number of successes).
- **Critical region**: the set of values of the test statistic that lead to rejecting H₀. **Critical value**: the first value in the critical region. **Acceptance region**: the rest.
- **p-value**: the probability, assuming H₀ is true, of a result at least as extreme as the one observed. Reject H₀ if the p-value is less than the significance level.
- (A-level only) A correlation coefficient measures how close points lie to a straight line (−1 to 1). Test for correlation using a given coefficient and a given critical value or p-value.

#### Explained

Hypotheses are always about the population parameter (p, μ or ρ), never about the sample.

A conclusion has two parts: a statistical decision ("reject H₀" or "do not reject H₀"), and a statement in context that is not too definite ("there is sufficient evidence at the 5% level to suggest that the proportion has increased"). Not rejecting H₀ does not prove it true.

The actual significance level of a binomial test is the probability of the critical region, which is usually a little less than the stated level because the distribution is discrete.

**(A-level only) Correlation tests.** The population correlation coefficient is ρ.

- H₀: ρ = 0 (no correlation in the population).
- H₁: ρ > 0, ρ < 0 (one-tailed) or ρ ≠ 0 (two-tailed).
- Compare the sample value r with the critical value from tables (for the sample size n and significance level). Reject H₀ if |r| is bigger than the critical value (and r has the sign in H₁). Or compare a given p-value with the significance level.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) For 20 days, the correlation between daily rainfall and hours of sunshine has r = −0.42. The critical value for a one-tailed test at 5% with n = 20 is 0.3783. Test whether there is negative correlation.

1. H₀: ρ = 0. H₁: ρ < 0.
2. |−0.42| = 0.42 > 0.3783, and r is negative.
3. Reject H₀. There is evidence at the 5% level of negative correlation between rainfall and sunshine.

(A-level only) A test for correlation in a sample of 12 gives r = 0.52 and a two-tailed p-value of 0.083. Test at 5%.

1. H₀: ρ = 0. H₁: ρ ≠ 0.
2. 0.083 > 0.05. Do not reject H₀.
3. Insufficient evidence at the 5% level of correlation.

#### Common mistakes

- Writing hypotheses in terms of the sample (x̄ or r) instead of the parameter.
- Concluding "H₀ is true" or "this proves" instead of "insufficient evidence".
- (A-level only) Comparing r with the critical value for the wrong number of tails.

### O2 Binomial tests for a proportion

*Refs: AQA O2 · Edexcel Statistics 5.2 · OCR 2.05b, 2.05c · MEI MH3, MH5, MH6. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Test a proportion p using X ~ B(n, p) under H₀, and interpret the result in context.
- Find critical regions for one- and two-tailed tests, and the actual significance level.
- A sample is being used to make an inference about a population; the significance level is the probability of wrongly rejecting H₀.

#### Explained

Steps:

1. Define X (the number of successes in the sample) and state its distribution under H₀.
2. Write H₀ and H₁ using p.
3. Either find the p-value, P(X ≥ observed) for an upper tail or P(X ≤ observed) for a lower tail, or find the critical region.
4. Compare with the significance level (half of it in each tail for a two-tailed test).
5. Conclude in context.

For an upper-tail critical region at level α, find the smallest c with P(X ≥ c) ≤ α. For a lower tail, find the largest c with P(X ≤ c) ≤ α.

#### Worked example

A website says 30% of visitors buy something. After a redesign, 10 of 20 visitors buy something. Test at the 5% level whether the proportion has increased.

1. X = number of buyers in 20. Under H₀, X ~ B(20, 0.3).
2. H₀: p = 0.3. H₁: p > 0.3.
3. P(X ≥ 10) = 1 − P(X ≤ 9) = 0.0480.
4. 0.0480 < 0.05, so reject H₀.
5. There is evidence at the 5% level that the proportion of buyers has increased.

Find the critical region for this test, and its actual significance level.

1. P(X ≥ 10) = 0.0480 ≤ 0.05; P(X ≥ 9) = 0.1133 > 0.05.
2. Critical region X ≥ 10. Actual significance level 4.80%.

A coin is tossed 25 times to test whether it is fair, at the 5% level. Find the critical region.

1. H₀: p = 0.5. H₁: p ≠ 0.5. X ~ B(25, 0.5). 2.5% in each tail.
2. Lower: P(X ≤ 7) = 0.0216 ≤ 0.025; P(X ≤ 8) = 0.0539. So X ≤ 7.
3. Upper: P(X ≥ 18) = 0.0216; P(X ≥ 17) = 0.0539. So X ≥ 18.
4. Critical region X ≤ 7 or X ≥ 18. Actual significance level 0.0432.

40% of people in a town own a dog. In a sample of 30 from one street, 6 own a dog. Test at 5% whether the proportion is lower in that street.

1. H₀: p = 0.4. H₁: p < 0.4. X ~ B(30, 0.4).
2. P(X ≤ 6) = 0.0172 < 0.05. Reject H₀.
3. There is evidence at the 5% level that dog ownership is lower in that street.

#### Common mistakes

- Using P(X = observed) instead of P(X ≥ observed).
- For an upper tail, using 1 − P(X ≤ observed) instead of 1 − P(X ≤ observed − 1).
- Forgetting to halve the significance level in a two-tailed test.

### O3 Testing the mean of a Normal distribution

*Refs: AQA O3 · Edexcel Statistics 5.3 · OCR 2.05d, 2.05e · MEI MH7, MH8, MH9. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) If X ~ N(μ, σ²), the mean of a random sample of size n has distribution

```latex
\bar{X} \sim N\!\left(\mu, \frac{\sigma^2}{n}\right)
```

- (A-level only) Test H₀: μ = μ₀ against a one- or two-tailed H₁ when σ is known, given or assumed.
- (A-level only) Find the critical region for x̄.

#### Explained

The sample mean varies less than single values: its standard deviation is σ/√n. Standardise:

```latex
z = \frac{\bar{x} - \mu_0}{\sigma / \sqrt{n}}
```

Compare z with the critical value from the standard Normal (1.6449 for 5% one-tailed, ±1.96 for 5% two-tailed, 2.3263 for 1% one-tailed, ±2.5758 for 1% two-tailed), or find the p-value.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) Bags of flour have masses N(μ, 4²) grams, with μ = 50 claimed. A sample of 25 bags has mean 51.8 g. Test at 5% whether the mean is greater than 50.

1. H₀: μ = 50. H₁: μ > 50.
2. Under H₀, X̄ ~ N(50, 16/25), standard deviation 0.8.
3. z = (51.8 − 50)/0.8 = 2.25 > 1.6449. (p-value = P(Z > 2.25) = 0.0122.)
4. Reject H₀. There is evidence at the 5% level that the mean mass is greater than 50 g.

(A-level only) Find the critical region for x̄ in that test.

1. x̄ > 50 + 1.6449 × 0.8 = 51.32.

(A-level only) IQ scores have σ = 15. A sample of 36 people from one area has mean 115.2. Test at 1% whether the mean differs from 120.

1. H₀: μ = 120. H₁: μ ≠ 120.
2. z = (115.2 − 120)/(15/6) = −1.92.
3. Critical values ±2.5758. |−1.92| < 2.5758, so do not reject H₀.
4. Insufficient evidence at the 1% level that the mean in this area differs from 120.

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) Using σ instead of σ/√n.
- (A-level only) Using 1.6449 for a two-tailed 5% test.

### Check questions

1. Explain what is meant by (a) a critical region; (b) the significance level of a test. [2 marks]
2. A seed company claims 60% of its seeds germinate. A gardener plants 15 seeds and thinks the rate is lower. (a) Find the critical region for a test at the 5% level. (b) 5 seeds germinate. State the conclusion. [6 marks]
3. A spinner should land on red with probability 0.25. In 40 spins it lands on red 15 times. Test at the 5% level whether the probability of red is greater than 0.25. [5 marks]
4. It is thought that 20% of a population carry a gene. Find the critical region for a two-tailed test at the 5% level using a sample of 50, and state the actual significance level. [5 marks]
5. (A-level only) The times to complete a task are N(μ, 6²) minutes, and μ has been 80. After training, a sample of 16 times has mean 83.1. Test at the 5% level whether the mean time has increased. Give the p-value. [6 marks]
6. (A-level only) The lengths of rods are Normal with σ = 1.2 cm. A sample of 30 rods has mean 24.3 cm. Test at the 5% level whether the mean length differs from 25 cm. [6 marks]
7. (A-level only) For 30 pairs of data, r = 0.33. The critical value for a one-tailed test at 5% is 0.3061. Test for positive correlation. [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. (a) The set of values of the test statistic that lead to rejecting H₀ (1). (b) The probability of rejecting H₀ when H₀ is true (1).
2. (a) H₀: p = 0.6, H₁: p < 0.6, X ~ B(15, 0.6) (1); P(X ≤ 5) = 0.0338 (1); P(X ≤ 6) = 0.0950 (1); critical region X ≤ 5 (1). (b) 5 is in the critical region, reject H₀ (1); evidence that the germination rate is lower than 60% (1).
3. H₀: p = 0.25, H₁: p > 0.25 (1); X ~ B(40, 0.25) (1); P(X ≥ 15) = 0.0544 (1); 0.0544 > 0.05, do not reject H₀ (1); insufficient evidence that the probability of red is greater than 0.25 (1).
4. X ~ B(50, 0.2), 2.5% each tail (1); P(X ≤ 4) = 0.0185 and P(X ≤ 5) = 0.0480, so X ≤ 4 (1); P(X ≥ 17) = 0.0144 and P(X ≥ 16) = 0.0308, so X ≥ 17 (1); critical region X ≤ 4 or X ≥ 17 (1); actual significance 0.0329 (1).
5. H₀: μ = 80, H₁: μ > 80 (1); X̄ ~ N(80, 36/16) (1); z = 3.1/1.5 = 2.07 (1); p-value = 0.0194 (1); < 0.05, reject H₀ (1); evidence that the mean time has increased (1).
6. H₀: μ = 25, H₁: μ ≠ 25 (1); standard error 1.2/√30 = 0.219 (1); z = −3.20 (1); critical values ±1.96 (1); reject H₀ (1); evidence that the mean length is not 25 cm (1).
7. H₀: ρ = 0, H₁: ρ > 0 (1); 0.33 > 0.3061 (1); reject H₀: evidence of positive correlation (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/maths/24-mechanics-quantities-units-and-modelling.md -->

## Mechanics: quantities, units and modelling

This file covers the SI units used in mechanics, derived quantities and their units, converting units, and the standard modelling words (particle, light, smooth, and so on) with what each assumption means.

### Overview

All AS, except the unit of a moment, which is needed only for moments (file 29, A-level only). The modelling vocabulary here is used in every mechanics question, and the overarching themes OT3.1 and OT3.5 (forming a model, and using modelling assumptions) are met here as well as in file 01.

#### Board differences

- AQA P1; Edexcel Mechanics 6.1; OCR H240 3.01a to 3.01c; OCR MEI H640 Mp31 to Mp35.
- MEI lists the language of modelling assumptions (Mp31) and the particle model (Mp32) as separate outcomes.
- Edexcel states that its default value of g is 9.8 m s⁻², and that a question may ask for a different value. AQA asks you to know g to varying degrees of accuracy and that it depends on location.

### P1 Quantities and units

*Refs: AQA P1, OT3.1, OT3.5 · Edexcel Mechanics 6.1 · OCR 3.01a, 3.01b, 3.01c · MEI Mp31, Mp32, Mp33, Mp34, Mp35. Level: AS & A (part A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- Fundamental (base) SI quantities and units: length (metre, m), time (second, s), mass (kilogram, kg).
- Derived quantities and units:

| Quantity | Unit | In base units |
| --- | --- | --- |
| velocity, speed | m s⁻¹ | m s⁻¹ |
| acceleration | m s⁻² | m s⁻² |
| force, weight | newton (N) | kg m s⁻² |
| (A-level only) moment | newton metre (N m) | kg m² s⁻² |

- Convert between units, such as km h⁻¹ and m s⁻¹.
- Understand and use modelling assumptions and the particle model.

#### Explained

**Converting.** Multiply by conversion factors written as fractions equal to 1:

```latex
72\ \text{km h}^{-1} = 72 \times \frac{1000\ \text{m}}{3600\ \text{s}} = 20\ \text{m s}^{-1}
```

To go from km h⁻¹ to m s⁻¹, divide by 3.6.

**Checking units.** Both sides of a formula must have the same units. F = ma gives kg × m s⁻² = N, which is why 1 N = 1 kg m s⁻². A wrong unit is a sign of a wrong formula.

**Modelling words.**

| Word | Meaning | Effect on the model |
| --- | --- | --- |
| particle | object with mass but no size | ignore rotation and air resistance; all forces act at one point |
| rigid body / rod | object with size that does not bend | needed for moments (file 29) |
| uniform | mass spread evenly | weight acts at the centre |
| light | mass is negligible | ignore its weight; tension is the same all along a light string |
| inextensible | does not stretch | connected objects have the same speed and acceleration |
| smooth | no friction | contact force is the normal reaction only |
| rough | friction acts | include friction (file 28) |
| lamina | flat object with negligible thickness | |
| bead | particle threaded on a wire or string | |
| peg | support with negligible size; may be smooth or rough | |

Every assumption makes the maths simpler and the answer less exact. For example, treating a car as a particle ignores air resistance, which would really reduce its acceleration.

**g.** The acceleration due to gravity near the Earth's surface is about 9.8 m s⁻². It is not a universal constant: it varies slightly with location, and is quite different on the Moon. Give answers to 2 or 3 significant figures when g = 9.8 is used, since 9.8 is only given to 2 significant figures.

#### Worked example

Convert 54 km h⁻¹ to m s⁻¹, and 0.35 m s⁻¹ to km h⁻¹.

1. 54 ÷ 3.6 = 15 m s⁻¹.
2. 0.35 × 3.6 = 1.26 km h⁻¹.

A car accelerates at 1800 km h⁻². Convert this to m s⁻².

1. 1800 × 1000 m / (3600 s)² = 1 800 000/12 960 000 = 0.139 m s⁻² (3 s.f.).

Show that the units of ½mv² are the same as the units of force × distance.

1. ½mv²: kg × (m s⁻¹)² = kg m² s⁻².
2. Force × distance: N × m = kg m s⁻² × m = kg m² s⁻².
3. They match.

A parcel is pulled across a floor by a string. State the modelling assumptions in "the parcel is modelled as a particle and the string as light and inextensible", and what each lets you do.

1. Particle: the parcel's size and shape are ignored, so all forces act at one point and it does not rotate.
2. Light string: its mass is ignored, so the tension is the same throughout.
3. Inextensible: the string does not stretch, so the parcel moves as soon as the string is pulled and with the same acceleration as the end of the string.

#### Common mistakes

- Multiplying by 3.6 when converting km h⁻¹ to m s⁻¹.
- Giving answers to more significant figures than g = 9.8 justifies.
- Listing an assumption without saying how it simplifies the problem or affects the answer.

### Check questions

1. Convert 90 km h⁻¹ to m s⁻¹. [1 mark]
2. Convert 12 m s⁻¹ to km h⁻¹. [1 mark]
3. Write the newton in terms of kg, m and s. [1 mark]
4. A density is 2.7 g cm⁻³. Convert it to kg m⁻³. [2 marks]
5. A ball is dropped from a tall building. State two modelling assumptions that make the motion simple to analyse, and say for each whether the real ball would take more or less time to fall. [4 marks]
6. Explain the meaning of "light" and "inextensible" when applied to a string connecting two particles. [2 marks]
7. (A-level only) State the unit of a moment, and show it has the same base units as work done. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. 25 m s⁻¹ (1).
2. 43.2 km h⁻¹ (1).
3. 1 N = 1 kg m s⁻² (1).
4. 2.7 g = 0.0027 kg and 1 cm³ = 10⁻⁶ m³ (1); 2700 kg m⁻³ (1).
5. Ball as a particle / no air resistance (1); real ball takes more time, because air resistance slows it (1); g constant at 9.8 m s⁻² (1); in reality g changes slightly with height and place, so the time changes very little (1).
6. Light: the string's mass is ignored, so tension is the same throughout (1). Inextensible: it does not stretch, so both particles have the same speed and acceleration (1).
7. N m (1); N m = kg m s⁻² × m = kg m² s⁻², the same as force × distance (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/maths/29-moments.md -->

## Moments

This file covers the moment of a force, the conditions for a rigid body to be in equilibrium, and problems with beams, rods, see-saws, tilting and ladders.

### Overview

All A-level only. Objects are now rigid bodies (with size) rather than particles, so the point where a force acts matters.

#### Board differences

- AQA S1; Edexcel Mechanics 9.1; OCR H240 3.04a to 3.04c (and 3.01c for the unit N m); OCR MEI H640 MF13 to MF16 (and Mp35 for the unit).
- Edexcel's guidance includes rigid bodies in equilibrium under parallel and non-parallel coplanar forces, for example ladders, and problems on the point of tilting. MEI's MF16 states that the weight of a body acts at its centre of mass when finding its moment.

### S1 Moments in simple static contexts

*Refs: AQA S1 · Edexcel Mechanics 9.1 · OCR 3.01c, 3.04a, 3.04b, 3.04c · MEI Mp35, MF13, MF14, MF15, MF16. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) The moment of a force about a point is its turning effect:

```latex
\text{moment} = F \times d
```

where d is the perpendicular distance from the point to the line of action of the force. The unit is N m. State the sense (clockwise or anticlockwise).

- (A-level only) For a force F applied at distance r from the pivot at angle θ to the rod, the moment is Fr sin θ.
- (A-level only) A rigid body is in equilibrium when both: the resultant force is zero, and the total moment about any point is zero.
- (A-level only) The weight of a uniform body acts at its centre; for a non-uniform body it acts at its centre of mass, which may need to be found.
- (A-level only) A body on the point of tilting about a support has zero reaction at the other support.

#### Explained

**Choosing the point.** Take moments about a point where an unknown force acts: that force then has zero moment and disappears from the equation. Resolve vertically (and horizontally if needed) for the remaining equation.

**Beams on two supports.** Two unknown reactions. Take moments about one support to find the other reaction, then resolve vertically.

**Tilting.** As a load moves towards the end of a beam, the reaction at the far support decreases. When it reaches zero, the beam is about to tilt about the near support.

**Ladders.** A ladder against a smooth wall has a normal reaction S from the wall (horizontal). On rough ground, there is a normal reaction R (vertical) and friction F (horizontal, towards the wall). Resolve both ways and take moments about the foot. In limiting equilibrium, F = μR (file 28).

#### Worked example

(A-level only) A force of 15 N acts at the end of a 2 m rod, at 40° to the rod. Find its moment about the other end.

1. Perpendicular component: 15 sin 40°.
2. Moment = 15 sin 40° × 2 = 19.3 N m.

(A-level only) A uniform beam AB, 6 m long and of mass 40 kg, rests horizontally on supports at C (1 m from A) and D (1 m from B). A person of mass 70 kg stands 2 m from B. Find the reactions at the supports.

1. Positions from A: C at 1, beam's weight at 3, person at 4, D at 5.
2. Moments about C: R_D × 4 = 40g × 2 + 70g × 3 = 290g, so R_D = 72.5g = 710.5 N.
3. Vertically: R_C + R_D = 110g, so R_C = 37.5g = 367.5 N.

(A-level only) How far beyond D can the person in the last example stand before the beam tilts?

1. On the point of tilting about D, R_C = 0.
2. Moments about D: 40g × 2 = 70g × x, where x is the distance beyond D.
3. x = 80/70 = 1.14 m. B is only 1 m beyond D, so the person can stand anywhere up to B without the beam tilting.

(A-level only) A non-uniform rod AB of length 4 m and weight 117.6 N hangs horizontally from two vertical strings at A and B. The tension at A is 70 N. Find the position of the centre of mass.

1. Vertically: T_B = 117.6 − 70 = 47.6 N.
2. Moments about A: 47.6 × 4 = 117.6 × x, so x = 1.62 m from A.

(A-level only) A uniform ladder of length 5 m and mass 20 kg rests against a smooth vertical wall at 60° to rough horizontal ground. Find the least value of μ for the ladder to stay in equilibrium.

1. Forces: weight 20g at the midpoint; S from the wall (horizontal); R and F at the foot.
2. Vertically: R = 20g = 196 N. Horizontally: F = S.
3. Moments about the foot: S × 5 sin 60° = 20g × 2.5 cos 60°, so S = 56.6 N.
4. F = 56.6 ≤ μ × 196, so μ ≥ 0.289 (exactly 1/(2√3)).

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) Multiplying by the distance along the rod instead of the perpendicular distance.
- (A-level only) Mixing clockwise and anticlockwise moments in one sum without signs.
- (A-level only) Forgetting the weight of the beam itself.
- (A-level only) Taking moments about a point and then also using the forces at that point.

### Check questions

1. (A-level only) A uniform plank pivoted at its centre balances with a 30 kg child 1.5 m from the pivot on one side. How far from the pivot must a 25 kg child sit on the other side? [2 marks]
2. (A-level only) A uniform beam AB of length 8 m and mass 50 kg rests on supports at A and B. A 20 kg load is placed 2 m from A. Find the reactions at A and B. [4 marks]
3. (A-level only) A uniform rod AB, 3 m long and of mass 6 kg, rests horizontally on supports at A and C, where AC = 2 m. A weight W N hangs from B. Find the value of W for which the rod is on the point of tilting about C. [4 marks]
4. (A-level only) A non-uniform rod AB of length 5 m and weight 120 N hangs horizontally from vertical strings at A and B. The tension at A is twice the tension at B. Find the distance of the centre of mass from A. [4 marks]
5. (A-level only) A force of 25 N acts on a rod 4 m from a pivot, at 30° to the rod. Find its moment about the pivot. [2 marks]
6. (A-level only) A uniform ladder of length 4 m and mass 15 kg rests against a smooth vertical wall with its foot on rough horizontal ground (μ = 0.4). The ladder is about to slip. Find the angle between the ladder and the ground. [6 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. 30g × 1.5 = 25g × x (1); x = 1.8 m (1).
2. Moments about A: R_B × 8 = 50g × 4 + 20g × 2 (1); R_B = 30g = 294 N (1); R_A + R_B = 70g (1); R_A = 40g = 392 N (1).
3. On the point of tilting, R_A = 0 (1); moments about C: 6g × 0.5 = W × 1 (2); W = 3g = 29.4 (1).
4. T_A = 2T_B, 3T_B = 120, so T_B = 40 N, T_A = 80 N (1); moments about A: 40 × 5 = 120x (2); x = 5/3 m (1).
5. 25 × 4 × sin 30° (1) = 50 N m (1).
6. R = 15g, F = S (1); limiting: F = 0.4 × 15g = 58.8 N (1); moments about the foot: S × 4 sin θ = 15g × 2 cos θ (2); tan θ = 1.25 (1); θ = 51.3° (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/maths/25-kinematics.md -->

## Kinematics: graphs, constant acceleration and calculus

This file covers the language of motion, displacement-time and velocity-time graphs, the constant acceleration (suvat) formulae, vertical motion under gravity, and using calculus when acceleration varies. Projectiles are in file 26.

### Overview

AS: motion in a straight line, including vertical motion under gravity. A-level only: the same ideas in two dimensions using vectors.

#### Board differences

- AQA Q1 to Q4; Edexcel Mechanics 7.1 to 7.4.
- OCR H240: 3.02a to 3.02d and 3.02f (AS); 3.02e and 3.02g (A-level only).
- OCR MEI H640: Mk1 to Mk8 (AS); Mk9 to Mk12 (A-level only). Mk11 asks for the Cartesian equation of a path given the position vector in terms of time.
- The DfE content asks you to derive the constant acceleration formulae as well as use them (AQA Q3 and Edexcel 7.3 say "derive").

### Q1 The language of kinematics

*Refs: AQA Q1 · Edexcel Mechanics 7.1 · OCR 3.02a · MEI Mk1, Mk2, Mk3. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Position: where an object is, relative to a fixed origin.
- Displacement: change in position; a vector (has direction). Distance travelled: total length of path; a scalar.
- Velocity: rate of change of displacement; a vector. Speed: the magnitude of velocity; a scalar.
- Acceleration: rate of change of velocity; a vector.
- Average speed = total distance ÷ total time. Average velocity = displacement ÷ time.

#### Explained

In one dimension, direction is shown by sign: choose a positive direction and stick to it. A ball thrown up and caught at the same height has displacement 0, but has travelled a positive distance.

A negative acceleration does not always mean slowing down: if the velocity is also negative, the object is speeding up in the negative direction.

#### Worked example

A runner goes 300 m east in 60 s, then 100 m west in 40 s. Find the average speed and average velocity.

1. Distance 400 m in 100 s: average speed 4 m s⁻¹.
2. Displacement 200 m east in 100 s: average velocity 2 m s⁻¹ east.

#### Common mistakes

- Using displacement where the question asks for distance travelled.

### Q2 Kinematics graphs

*Refs: AQA Q2 · Edexcel Mechanics 7.2 · OCR 3.02b, 3.02c · MEI Mk4. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Displacement-time graph: gradient = velocity.
- Velocity-time graph: gradient = acceleration; area between the graph and the time axis = displacement (area below the axis counts as negative displacement).

#### Explained

On a velocity-time graph, straight sections mean constant acceleration, and the area splits into triangles, rectangles and trapezia. Distance travelled is the total area counting all parts as positive; displacement subtracts areas below the axis.

Many questions give a journey in words and ask you to sketch the graph first, then use areas to find an unknown time or speed.

#### Worked example

A train accelerates uniformly from rest to 12 m s⁻¹ in 4 s, travels at 12 m s⁻¹ for 10 s, then decelerates uniformly to rest in 6 s. Find the accelerations and the total distance.

1. Accelerations: 12/4 = 3 m s⁻² and −12/6 = −2 m s⁻².
2. Area: ½ × 4 × 12 + 10 × 12 + ½ × 6 × 12 = 24 + 120 + 36 = 180 m.

A car starts at 5 m s⁻¹, accelerates uniformly to 17 m s⁻¹ in 6 s, travels at 17 m s⁻¹ for T s, then decelerates at 1.7 m s⁻² to rest. The total distance is 559 m. Find T.

1. First part: ½(5 + 17) × 6 = 66 m.
2. Last part: time 17/1.7 = 10 s, distance ½ × 10 × 17 = 85 m.
3. Middle: 17T = 559 − 66 − 85 = 408, so T = 24 s.

#### Common mistakes

- Reading the gradient of a velocity-time graph as velocity.
- Adding areas below the axis when the question asks for displacement.

### Q3 Constant acceleration formulae

*Refs: AQA Q3 · Edexcel Mechanics 7.3 · OCR 3.02d, 3.02e · MEI Mk7, Mk8, Mk9, Mk10. Level: AS & A (part A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- For constant acceleration in a straight line:

```latex
v = u + at \qquad s = \frac{(u+v)}{2}t \qquad s = ut + \tfrac{1}{2}at^2 \qquad s = vt - \tfrac{1}{2}at^2 \qquad v^2 = u^2 + 2as
```

- Derive them from a velocity-time graph or from the definitions.
- Vertical motion under gravity: a = g = 9.8 m s⁻² downwards (or −9.8 if up is positive).
- (A-level only) In two dimensions with vectors: v = u + at, r = ut + ½at² (with r the displacement), and the others with vectors where they make sense.

#### Explained

**Derivation.** A straight-line v-t graph from u to v over time t has gradient a, so a = (v − u)/t, giving v = u + at. The area is a trapezium: s = ½(u + v)t. Substituting v = u + at gives s = ut + ½at². Substituting u = v − at gives s = vt − ½at². Eliminating t gives v² = u² + 2as.

**Method.** List s, u, v, a, t; mark the three you know and the one you want; choose the formula that does not contain the fifth. Choose a positive direction and give every vector quantity a sign.

**Vertical motion.** Taking up as positive, a = −9.8. At the highest point v = 0. If an object returns to its starting level, s = 0.

**(A-level only) Vectors.** The same formulae work with vector u, v, a, s. Find the speed as the magnitude of v.

#### Worked example

A car moving at 8 m s⁻¹ accelerates at 2 m s⁻² for 5 s. Find its final speed and the distance covered.

1. v = 8 + 2 × 5 = 18 m s⁻¹.
2. s = 8 × 5 + ½ × 2 × 25 = 65 m.

A stone is thrown vertically upwards from ground level at 14.7 m s⁻¹. Find the greatest height and the time until it lands.

1. Up positive, a = −9.8. At the top v = 0: 0 = 14.7² − 2 × 9.8 × s, so s = 11.0 m.
2. Lands when s = 0: 0 = 14.7t − 4.9t², so t = 3 s.

A ball is thrown upwards at 7 m s⁻¹ from a point 20 m above the ground. Find when it hits the ground.

1. Up positive. s = −20, u = 7, a = −9.8.
2. −20 = 7t − 4.9t², so 4.9t² − 7t − 20 = 0.
3. t = (7 + √441)/9.8 = 28/9.8 = 2.86 s (rejecting the negative root).

Car A starts from rest with acceleration 2 m s⁻² just as car B passes it at a constant 15 m s⁻¹. When and where does A catch B?

1. A: s = t². B: s = 15t.
2. t² = 15t, so t = 15 s (t = 0 is the start).
3. Distance 225 m.

(A-level only) A particle has initial velocity (2i − 3j) m s⁻¹ and constant acceleration (0.5i + j) m s⁻². Find its velocity, speed and displacement after 4 s.

1. v = (2i − 3j) + 4(0.5i + j) = 4i + j. Speed √17 = 4.12 m s⁻¹.
2. r = 4(2i − 3j) + ½ × 16 × (0.5i + j) = (8i − 12j) + (4i + 8j) = 12i − 4j m.

#### Common mistakes

- Mixing directions: using a = 9.8 with up as positive.
- Using suvat when the acceleration is not constant.

### Q4 Calculus in kinematics

*Refs: AQA Q4 · Edexcel Mechanics 7.4 · OCR 3.02f, 3.02g · MEI Mk5, Mk6, Mk11, Mk12. Level: AS & A (part A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- When acceleration varies:

```latex
v = \frac{dr}{dt} \qquad a = \frac{dv}{dt} = \frac{d^2r}{dt^2} \qquad r = \int v\,dt \qquad v = \int a\,dt
```

- Use initial conditions to find the constants of integration.
- (A-level only) In two dimensions, differentiate and integrate each component of a vector separately.

#### Explained

**Differentiate** to go down the chain displacement → velocity → acceleration. **Integrate** to go up it, adding a constant each time (found from the starting position or velocity).

At rest means v = 0. Maximum velocity is when a = 0. To find distance travelled when the particle changes direction, find when v = 0, integrate over each section separately, and add the sizes.

(A-level only) If r = x(t)i + y(t)j, then v = x′(t)i + y′(t)j. The particle moves parallel to i when the j component of v is 0. Eliminating t from x and y gives the Cartesian equation of the path.

#### Worked example

A particle moves in a straight line with v = 3t² − 12t + 9 m s⁻¹ for t ≥ 0. Find when it is at rest, its acceleration then, and the distance travelled in the first 3 s.

1. 3(t − 1)(t − 3) = 0, so t = 1 and t = 3.
2. a = 6t − 12: −6 m s⁻² at t = 1, +6 m s⁻² at t = 3.
3. s = t³ − 6t² + 9t (starting at s = 0). s(1) = 4, s(3) = 0.
4. Distance: 4 m out and 4 m back, so 8 m (although the displacement is 0).

A particle has a = 6 − 2t m s⁻² and starts with velocity 4 m s⁻¹. Find its maximum velocity.

1. v = 6t − t² + 4 (using v = 4 at t = 0).
2. Maximum when a = 0: t = 3, v = 18 − 9 + 4 = 13 m s⁻¹.

(A-level only) r = (t³ − 3t)i + (2t² + 1)j. Find the velocity and acceleration at t = 2, and the speed then.

1. v = (3t² − 3)i + 4tj. At t = 2: 9i + 8j.
2. a = 6ti + 4j. At t = 2: 12i + 4j.
3. Speed √(81 + 64) = √145 = 12.0 m s⁻¹.

(A-level only) A particle has position r = 2ti + (t² − 1)j. Find the Cartesian equation of its path.

1. x = 2t, so t = x/2.
2. y = x²/4 − 1.

#### Common mistakes

- Using suvat with a variable acceleration.
- Forgetting the constant of integration.
- Giving displacement when the particle turns round and the question asks for distance.

### Check questions

1. A car starts at 5 m s⁻¹ and accelerates uniformly to 17 m s⁻¹ in 6 s. It then travels at 17 m s⁻¹ for T s and decelerates at 1.7 m s⁻² to rest. The total distance is 559 m. Sketch the velocity-time graph and find T. [5 marks]
2. A train slows uniformly from 30 m s⁻¹ to 12 m s⁻¹ over 315 m. Find the deceleration and the time taken. [4 marks]
3. A ball is projected vertically upwards at 19.6 m s⁻¹ from a point 2 m above the ground. Find the length of time for which it is more than 15 m above the ground. [5 marks]
4. A particle moves in a straight line with v = 6t − t² for 0 ≤ t ≤ 6. Find its greatest speed, its acceleration when t = 4, and the distance travelled. [5 marks]
5. Particle A starts from rest at O and accelerates at 2 m s⁻² towards B, 100 m away. At the same moment particle B moves from B towards O at a constant 5 m s⁻¹. Find when they meet. [4 marks]
6. (A-level only) A particle starts at O with velocity (4i − 2j) m s⁻¹ and constant acceleration (−i + 3j) m s⁻². Find (a) when it is moving parallel to i; (b) its distance from O when t = 2. [5 marks]
7. (A-level only) r = (2t² − 5t)i + (t³ − 12t)j. Find the time when the particle is moving parallel to i, and its position then. [4 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Graph with three sections (1); 66 m and 85 m for the first and last parts (2); 17T = 408 (1); T = 24 (1).
