2. 12² = 30² + 2a(315) (1); a = −1.2, deceleration 1.2 m s⁻² (1); 12 = 30 − 1.2t (1); t = 15 s (1).
3. Displacement +13 m from the start (1); 13 = 19.6t − 4.9t² (1); t = 0.839 and 3.161 (2); time 2.32 s (1).
4. Greatest speed at t = 3 (1): 9 m s⁻¹ (1); a = 6 − 2t = −2 m s⁻² at t = 4 (1); v ≥ 0 throughout, so distance = ∫₀⁶(6t − t²) dt (1) = 36 m (1).
5. A: s = t²; B has gone 5t (1); t² + 5t = 100 (1); t = (−5 + √425)/2 (1) = 7.81 s (1).
6. (a) v = (4 − t)i + (−2 + 3t)j (1); j component 0 at t = 2/3 s (1). (b) r = (4t − ½t²)i + (−2t + 1.5t²)j (1); r = 6i + 2j at t = 2 (1); distance √40 = 6.32 m (1).
7. v = (4t − 5)i + (3t² − 12)j (1); 3t² − 12 = 0, t = 2 (1); r = (8 − 10)i + (8 − 24)j (1) = −2i − 16j (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/maths/26-projectiles.md -->

## Projectiles

This file covers motion under gravity in a vertical plane: splitting the motion into horizontal and vertical parts, time of flight, range and greatest height, the equation of the path, and finding the initial velocity.

### Overview

All A-level only. It uses the constant acceleration formulae (file 25) in each direction separately, and vectors (file 18).

#### Board differences

- AQA Q5; Edexcel Mechanics 7.5; OCR H240 3.02h, 3.02i; OCR MEI H640 My1 to My5.
- Edexcel's guidance includes deriving formulae for the time of flight, range and greatest height, and the equation of the path. MEI's My4 asks you to eliminate t to find the path.

### Q5 Projectiles

*Refs: AQA Q5 · Edexcel Mechanics 7.5 · OCR 3.02h, 3.02i · MEI My1, My2, My3, My4, My5. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) A projectile is modelled as a particle moving under gravity alone (no air resistance, no spin).
- (A-level only) Horizontal motion: constant velocity (no horizontal acceleration). Vertical motion: constant acceleration g downwards.
- (A-level only) For speed U at angle θ above the horizontal, the initial components are U cos θ (horizontal) and U sin θ (vertical). At time t:

```latex
x = Ut\cos\theta \qquad y = Ut\sin\theta - \tfrac{1}{2}gt^2
```

```latex
\dot{x} = U\cos\theta \qquad \dot{y} = U\sin\theta - gt
```

- (A-level only) In vector form, with i horizontal and j vertically upwards:

```latex
\mathbf{r} = \mathbf{u}t - \tfrac{1}{2}gt^2\,\mathbf{j} \qquad \mathbf{v} = \mathbf{u} - gt\,\mathbf{j}
```

- (A-level only) Results for a projectile launched from and landing on horizontal ground:

```latex
\text{time of flight } T = \frac{2U\sin\theta}{g} \qquad \text{range } R = \frac{U^2\sin 2\theta}{g} \qquad \text{greatest height } H = \frac{U^2\sin^2\theta}{2g}
```

- (A-level only) The equation of the path:

```latex
y = x\tan\theta - \frac{gx^2}{2U^2\cos^2\theta}
```

#### Explained

**Method.** Work in two columns, horizontal and vertical. Time is the link between them: often you find t from one direction and use it in the other.

- Greatest height: vertical velocity = 0.
- Lands at the same level: vertical displacement = 0.
- Lands below the start (off a cliff): vertical displacement is negative.
- Velocity at any time: find both components, then the speed √(ẋ² + ẏ²) and the angle tan⁻¹(ẏ/ẋ) to the horizontal.

**Deriving the results.** Time of flight: set y = 0, so t(U sin θ − ½gt) = 0 and T = 2U sin θ/g. Range: x at time T, R = U cos θ × 2U sin θ/g = U² sin 2θ/g. The range is greatest at θ = 45°. Greatest height: ẏ = 0 at t = U sin θ/g; substitute into y. Path: t = x/(U cos θ), substitute into y.

**Limitations.** Air resistance reduces the range and height. The object is not really a particle, and it may spin. g is taken as constant.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) A ball is kicked from ground level at 20 m s⁻¹ at 30° above the horizontal. Find the time of flight, the range and the greatest height.

1. Components: horizontal 20 cos 30° = 17.32, vertical 20 sin 30° = 10.
2. Lands when 10t − 4.9t² = 0: t = 10/4.9 = 2.04 s.
3. Range = 17.32 × 2.041 = 35.3 m.
4. Greatest height: 0 = 10² − 2 × 9.8 × h, so h = 5.10 m.

(A-level only) A stone is thrown horizontally at 12 m s⁻¹ from the top of a cliff 45 m high. Find how far from the foot of the cliff it lands, and its speed and direction on impact.

1. Vertical: u = 0, s = 45 (down positive), a = 9.8. 45 = 4.9t², so t = 3.03 s.
2. Horizontal distance = 12 × 3.030 = 36.4 m.
3. Vertical velocity at impact = 9.8 × 3.030 = 29.7 m s⁻¹.
4. Speed √(12² + 29.7²) = 32.0 m s⁻¹, at tan⁻¹(29.7/12) = 68.0° below the horizontal.

(A-level only) A ball is projected from ground level at 25 m s⁻¹ at angle α above the horizontal, where tan α = 3/4. A wall 10 m high is 30 m away. Does the ball clear the wall?

1. sin α = 3/5, cos α = 4/5. Components 20 and 15.
2. Reaches the wall when 20t = 30, t = 1.5 s.
3. Height then: 15(1.5) − 4.9(1.5)² = 22.5 − 11.025 = 11.475 m.
4. 11.475 > 10, so it clears the wall by about 1.5 m.

(A-level only) A ball is projected from ground level and lands 40 m away after 2.5 s. Find the speed and angle of projection.

1. Horizontal: 40 = 2.5uₓ, so uₓ = 16.
2. Vertical: 0 = 2.5u_y − 4.9(2.5)², so u_y = 12.25.
3. Speed √(16² + 12.25²) = 20.2 m s⁻¹, at tan⁻¹(12.25/16) = 37.4° above the horizontal.

(A-level only) A particle is projected with velocity (12i + 14.7j) m s⁻¹ from ground level. Find when it is 9.8 m above the ground, and its horizontal distance then.

1. 14.7t − 4.9t² = 9.8, so t² − 3t + 2 = 0, t = 1 or t = 2.
2. Horizontal distances 12 m (going up) and 24 m (coming down).

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) Giving the horizontal velocity an acceleration.
- (A-level only) Using U instead of U sin θ for the vertical start.
- (A-level only) Forgetting that the displacement is negative when landing below the start, with up as positive.
- (A-level only) Giving only the speed when the velocity (speed and direction) is asked for.

### Check questions

1. (A-level only) A ball is projected from ground level at 30 m s⁻¹ at 40° above the horizontal. Find (a) the greatest height; (b) the time of flight; (c) the range. [6 marks]
2. (A-level only) A ball rolls off a table 1.2 m high with speed 8 m s⁻¹. Find how far from the table it lands and its speed on landing. [5 marks]
3. (A-level only) Show that a projectile launched from level ground with speed U at angle θ has range U² sin 2θ / g. Hence find the angle that gives the greatest range. [5 marks]
4. (A-level only) A golf ball is hit from level ground at 45° and lands 80 m away. Find the speed of projection. [3 marks]
5. (A-level only) A stone is thrown from the top of a 20 m cliff at 15 m s⁻¹ at 30° above the horizontal. Find the time until it hits the sea, and how far from the foot of the cliff it lands. [6 marks]
6. (A-level only) A particle is projected with velocity (12i + 14.7j) m s⁻¹ from ground level. Find the Cartesian equation of its path. [3 marks]
7. (A-level only) State two assumptions made in the projectile model and say how each affects the predicted range. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Components 22.98 and 19.28 (1); (a) 0 = 19.28² − 19.6h (1), h = 19.0 m (1); (b) t = 2 × 19.28/9.8 (1) = 3.94 s (1); (c) 22.98 × 3.935 = 90.4 m (1).
2. 1.2 = 4.9t², t = 0.495 s (1); distance 8 × 0.495 = 3.96 m (1); vertical velocity 9.8 × 0.495 = 4.85 (1); speed √(64 + 4.85²) (1) = 9.36 m s⁻¹ (1).
3. Vertical: 0 = Ut sin θ − ½gt² (1); T = 2U sin θ/g (1); R = U cos θ × T (1); = U² × 2 sin θ cos θ/g = U² sin 2θ/g (1); greatest when sin 2θ = 1, θ = 45° (1).
4. 80 = U² sin 90°/9.8 (1); U² = 784 (1); U = 28 m s⁻¹ (1).
5. Components 12.99 and 7.5 (1); −20 = 7.5t − 4.9t² (1); 4.9t² − 7.5t − 20 = 0 (1); t = 2.93 s (1); distance 12.99 × 2.926 (1) = 38.0 m (1).
6. x = 12t, so t = x/12 (1); y = 14.7(x/12) − 4.9(x/12)² (1); y = 1.225x − 0.034x² (1).
7. No air resistance, which makes the predicted range too large (1); particle / no spin, or g constant, with a sensible effect (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/maths/27-forces-and-newtons-laws.md -->

## Forces and Newton's laws

This file covers what a force is, the common types of force, force diagrams, Newton's three laws, weight and g, equilibrium, connected particles and pulleys, resolving forces, and motion in a plane. Friction is in file 28 and moments in file 29.

### Overview

AS: forces and Newton's laws in a straight line, with forces in two perpendicular directions or given as simple 2D vectors; weight; equilibrium of a particle; connected particles and smooth pulleys. A-level only: resolving forces at an angle (including on inclined planes), equilibrium under several coplanar forces, and dynamics in a plane.

#### Board differences

- AQA R1 to R5; Edexcel Mechanics 8.1 to 8.5.
- OCR H240: 3.03a to 3.03d, 3.03f to 3.03k, 3.03n (AS); 3.03e, 3.03l, 3.03m, 3.03o to 3.03q (A-level only).
- OCR MEI H640: MF1 to MF5 and Mn1 to Mn6 (AS); MF6 to MF9 and Mn7 (A-level only). MF8 mentions the closed polygon of forces for equilibrium.
- Edexcel's default value of g is 9.8 m s⁻².

### R1 Forces and Newton's first law

*Refs: AQA R1 · Edexcel Mechanics 8.1 · OCR 3.03a, 3.03b, 3.03i, 3.03j · MEI MF1, MF3, Mn1. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- A force is a push or pull; it is a vector, measured in newtons.
- Types of force: weight, normal reaction (perpendicular to a surface), tension (in a string, pulling), thrust (in a rod, pushing), friction, resistance, driving force.
- Draw force diagrams showing every force on an object.
- Newton's first law: an object stays at rest or moves with constant velocity unless a resultant force acts on it.

#### Explained

A force diagram shows only the forces acting on the object you are studying, each as an arrow from the object with its name or size. Do not show forces the object exerts on other things, and do not draw "ma" as a force.

Constant velocity (including at rest) means the resultant force is zero: the forces balance.

#### Worked example

A box rests on a horizontal floor while a horizontal force of 20 N pulls it but it does not move. Draw a force diagram and explain why it does not move.

1. Forces: weight W down, normal reaction R up, 20 N pull, friction F opposing the pull.
2. At rest, so the resultant is zero: F = 20 N and R = W.

#### Common mistakes

- Including the force the object exerts on the floor in its own diagram.

### R2 Newton's second law

*Refs: AQA R2 · Edexcel Mechanics 8.2 · OCR 3.03c, 3.03d, 3.03e · MEI Mn2, Mn3, Mn7, MF6. Level: AS & A (part A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- Resultant force = mass × acceleration:

```latex
\mathbf{F} = m\mathbf{a}
```

- Apply it in a straight line with forces in two perpendicular directions, or with forces given as 2D vectors.
- (A-level only) Resolve forces at an angle into perpendicular components, then apply F = ma in each direction.

#### Explained

Write the equation of motion in the direction of the acceleration: (sum of forces in the direction of motion) − (sum of forces against it) = ma. Perpendicular to the motion, the forces balance.

**(A-level only) Resolving.** A force F at angle θ to a direction has component F cos θ along it and F sin θ perpendicular to it. On an inclined plane at angle α, the weight mg has components mg sin α down the slope and mg cos α into the slope.

**Vectors.** If forces are given as vectors, add them to get the resultant, then a = F/m.

#### Worked example

Forces (3i + 4j) N and (−i + 2j) N act on a particle of mass 2 kg. Find its acceleration and the magnitude of the acceleration.

1. Resultant 2i + 6j N. a = (i + 3j) m s⁻².
2. |a| = √10 = 3.16 m s⁻².

(A-level only) A 5 kg box is pulled up a smooth slope inclined at 20° by a force P parallel to the slope. The box accelerates at 1 m s⁻². Find P and the normal reaction.

1. Parallel to the slope: P − 5g sin 20° = 5 × 1, so P = 5 + 16.76 = 21.8 N.
2. Perpendicular: R = 5g cos 20° = 46.0 N.

#### Common mistakes

- Putting ma on the force diagram as if it were a force.
- (A-level only) Using cos for the component down a slope. Down the slope is mg sin α.

### R3 Weight and gravity

*Refs: AQA R3 · Edexcel Mechanics 8.3 · OCR 3.03f, 3.03g · MEI MF2. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Weight W = mg, acting vertically downwards.
- g is about 9.8 m s⁻² near the Earth's surface. It is not a universal constant: it varies slightly with location, and may be given to different accuracies (9.8, 9.81, 10).
- Motion in a straight line under gravity (see file 25).

#### Explained

Mass (kg) is the amount of matter and does not change with location. Weight (N) is the gravitational force and does depend on where you are.

#### Worked example

Find the weight of a 70 kg person on Earth (g = 9.8) and on the Moon (g = 1.6).

1. Earth: 686 N. Moon: 112 N.

#### Common mistakes

- Writing a mass in newtons or a weight in kilograms.

### R4 Newton's third law, equilibrium and connected particles

*Refs: AQA R4 · Edexcel Mechanics 8.4 · OCR 3.03h, 3.03k, 3.03l, 3.03m, 3.03n, 3.03o · MEI MF5, MF7, MF8, MF9, Mn4, Mn5, Mn6. Level: AS & A (part A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- Newton's third law: if A exerts a force on B, then B exerts an equal and opposite force on A.
- A particle is in equilibrium when the resultant force is zero.
- Connected particles: two objects joined by a light inextensible string or rod, or one pushing another, or a lift with a person; objects joined over a smooth pulley.
- (A-level only) Resolve forces in two dimensions; a particle under several coplanar forces is in equilibrium if and only if the components in two perpendicular directions each sum to zero.

#### Explained

**Connected particles.** Either treat the whole system as one object (internal forces such as tension cancel), or write an equation for each part. A good method: use the whole system to find the acceleration, then one part to find the tension.

Over a smooth pulley with a light inextensible string, the tension is the same on both sides, and both particles have the same size of acceleration. The force on the pulley is the vector sum of the two tensions (2T if the string hangs vertically on both sides).

**Lifts.** For a person of mass m in a lift with acceleration a upwards, the floor pushes up with R: R − mg = ma.

**(A-level only) Equilibrium with angles.** Resolve horizontally and vertically (or along and perpendicular to a slope) and set each sum to zero. Three forces in equilibrium form a closed triangle, so the sine rule can also be used on that triangle.

#### Worked example

A lift of mass 600 kg carries a person of mass 70 kg and accelerates upwards at 0.5 m s⁻². Find the tension in the cable and the force between the person and the floor.

1. Whole system: T − 670g = 670 × 0.5, so T = 670 × 10.3 = 6901 N.
2. Person: R − 70g = 70 × 0.5, so R = 721 N.

A car of mass 1200 kg tows a trailer of mass 400 kg with a light rigid towbar. The driving force is 2500 N, and resistances are 300 N on the car and 200 N on the trailer. Find the acceleration and the tension in the towbar.

1. Whole system: 2500 − 300 − 200 = 1600a, so a = 1.25 m s⁻².
2. Trailer: T − 200 = 400 × 1.25, so T = 700 N.

Particles of mass 5 kg and 3 kg are joined by a light inextensible string over a smooth fixed pulley and released from rest. Find the acceleration, the tension, and the force on the pulley.

1. 5 kg: 5g − T = 5a. 3 kg: T − 3g = 3a.
2. Add: 2g = 8a, a = 2.45 m s⁻².
3. T = 3(g + a) = 36.75 N.
4. Force on pulley = 2T = 73.5 N.

A 4 kg block on a smooth horizontal table is joined by a string over a smooth pulley at the edge to a 1 kg mass hanging freely. Find the acceleration and the tension.

1. Hanging: g − T = a. Block: T = 4a.
2. g = 5a, a = 1.96 m s⁻². T = 7.84 N.

(A-level only) A 2 kg particle hangs in equilibrium from two strings at 30° and 60° to the horizontal. Find the tensions.

1. Horizontal: T₁ cos 30° = T₂ cos 60°, so T₂ = √3 T₁.
2. Vertical: T₁ sin 30° + T₂ sin 60° = 2g, so T₁/2 + (3/2)T₁ = 19.6.
3. T₁ = 9.8 N, T₂ = 17.0 N.

#### Common mistakes

- Using different tensions on the two sides of a smooth pulley.
- Applying the whole system method to find tension (tension cancels in the whole system).
- (A-level only) Resolving a force in one direction and forgetting its component in the other.

### R5 Resultant forces and dynamics in a plane

*Refs: AQA R5 · Edexcel Mechanics 8.5 · OCR 3.03p, 3.03q · MEI MF4. Level: AS & A (part A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- Add forces to find the resultant, using components.
- (A-level only) The resultant of two or more forces at angles; dynamics of a particle moving in a plane under such forces.

#### Explained

Resolve every force into i and j components (or horizontal and vertical), add each set, then find the magnitude √(X² + Y²) and direction tan⁻¹(Y/X) of the resultant. Then F = ma gives the acceleration as a vector, which can be used in the vector suvat formulae (file 25).

#### Worked example

(A-level only) Forces of 10 N (along the positive x-axis) and 8 N (at 60° to it) act on a particle. Find the resultant.

1. X = 10 + 8 cos 60° = 14. Y = 8 sin 60° = 6.93.
2. Magnitude √(14² + 6.93²) = 15.6 N, at tan⁻¹(6.93/14) = 26.3° to the x-axis.

(A-level only) A particle of mass 4 kg is acted on by forces (2i − j) N, (pi + 5j) N and (−3i + qj) N, and accelerates at (0.5i + j) m s⁻². Find p and q.

1. Resultant: (p − 1)i + (q + 4)j = 4(0.5i + j) = 2i + 4j.
2. p = 3, q = 0.

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) Adding magnitudes of forces that act in different directions.

### Check questions

1. A lift of mass 500 kg carries a person of mass 80 kg. It is moving up and slowing down at 1.2 m s⁻². Find the tension in the cable and the reaction on the person. [4 marks]
2. Particles of mass 6 kg and 4 kg hang on either side of a smooth pulley, joined by a light inextensible string, and are released from rest. Find the acceleration, the tension, and the speed after 1.5 s. [5 marks]
3. A car of mass 1000 kg tows a caravan of mass 600 kg. The resistances are 400 N on the car and 300 N on the caravan. The car accelerates at 0.8 m s⁻². Find the driving force and the tension in the tow bar. [4 marks]
4. A particle is in equilibrium under forces 4i N, 3j N and F. Find F and its magnitude. [2 marks]
5. (A-level only) A 3 kg box is pulled up a smooth slope at 30° to the horizontal by a rope parallel to the slope with tension 20 N. Find its acceleration. [3 marks]
6. (A-level only) A particle of mass 4 kg moves under forces (2i − j) N, (pi + 5j) N and (−3i + qj) N with acceleration (0.5i + j) m s⁻². Find p and q. [3 marks]
7. (A-level only) A weight of 50 N hangs in equilibrium from two strings making angles of 40° and 50° with the horizontal. Find the tension in each string. [5 marks]
8. Explain why the tension is the same on both sides of a pulley in the standard model. [1 mark]

### Mark scheme

1. Whole system: T − 580g = 580 × (−1.2) (1); T = 4988 N (1); person: R − 80g = 80 × (−1.2) (1); R = 688 N (1).
2. 6g − T = 6a and T − 4g = 4a (1); a = 1.96 m s⁻² (1); T = 47.0 N (1); v = 1.96 × 1.5 (1) = 2.94 m s⁻¹ (1).
3. D − 700 = 1600 × 0.8 (1); D = 1980 N (1); T − 300 = 600 × 0.8 (1); T = 780 N (1).
4. F = −4i − 3j (1); |F| = 5 N (1).
5. 20 − 3g sin 30° = 3a (2); a = 1.77 m s⁻² (1).
6. Resultant (p − 1)i + (q + 4)j = 2i + 4j (1); p = 3 (1); q = 0 (1).
7. T₁ cos 40° = T₂ cos 50° (1); T₁ sin 40° + T₂ sin 50° = 50 (1); solving (1); T₁ = 32.1 N (for the 40° string) (1); T₂ = 38.3 N (1).
8. The pulley is smooth and the string is light, so there is nothing to change the tension (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/maths/28-friction.md -->

## Friction

This file covers the friction model F ≤ μR, the coefficient of friction, limiting friction, and motion and equilibrium on rough horizontal and inclined surfaces, including connected particles.

### Overview

A-level only, except that OCR H240 puts the basic concept of a frictional force (3.03r) in its AS (Stage 1) content. It builds on Newton's laws and resolving forces (file 27).

#### Board differences

- AQA R6; Edexcel Mechanics 8.6; OCR H240 3.03r (AS) and 3.03s to 3.03v (A-level only); OCR MEI H640 MF10 to MF12.
- OCR's 3.03s and MEI's MF10 ask you to see the overall contact force between two rough surfaces as having two components, friction and normal reaction.

### R6 Friction

*Refs: AQA R6 · Edexcel Mechanics 8.6 · OCR 3.03r, 3.03s, 3.03t, 3.03u, 3.03v · MEI MF10, MF11, MF12. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Friction acts along the surface, opposing motion (or the tendency to move).
- (A-level only) The friction model:

```latex
F \le \mu R
```

where R is the normal reaction and μ is the coefficient of friction (μ ≥ 0; μ = 0 for a smooth surface).

- (A-level only) When the object is moving, or on the point of moving (limiting equilibrium), F = μR.
- (A-level only) When the object is at rest and not on the point of moving, F takes whatever value is needed for equilibrium, up to μR.
- (A-level only) The contact force from a rough surface is the resultant of F and R.
- (A-level only) Solve problems on rough horizontal surfaces and rough inclined planes, including connected particles.

#### Explained

**Finding R first.** Friction depends on R, so always resolve perpendicular to the surface first to find R. R is not always mg: a force pulling upwards at an angle reduces R, and on a slope R = mg cos α plus any perpendicular components of other forces.

**Does it move?** Work out the force trying to move the object and the maximum friction μR. If the driving force is at most μR, the object stays at rest, and friction equals the driving force. If it is more, the object accelerates with F = μR opposing.

**Direction of friction.** Opposite to the direction of motion, or of the motion that would happen without friction. On a slope, a block that would slide down has friction acting up the slope; a block being pushed up has friction acting down the slope.

**Limiting equilibrium on a slope.** A block on a rough slope at angle α is about to slide when mg sin α = μ mg cos α, that is μ = tan α. If tan α ≤ μ it stays put on its own.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) A 10 kg box on a rough horizontal floor (μ = 0.3) is pulled by a horizontal force of 50 N. Find its acceleration.

1. R = 10g = 98 N. Maximum friction = 0.3 × 98 = 29.4 N.
2. 50 > 29.4, so it moves. 50 − 29.4 = 10a, so a = 2.06 m s⁻².

(A-level only) An 8 kg sledge on rough horizontal ground (μ = 0.4) is pulled by a rope at 25° above the horizontal with tension 40 N. Find its acceleration.

1. Vertical: R + 40 sin 25° = 8g, so R = 78.4 − 16.90 = 61.50 N.
2. Friction = 0.4 × 61.50 = 24.60 N.
3. Horizontal: 40 cos 25° − 24.60 = 8a, so 36.25 − 24.60 = 8a, a = 1.46 m s⁻².

(A-level only) A 4 kg block slides down a rough slope at 30° (μ = 0.2). Find its acceleration.

1. R = 4g cos 30° = 33.95 N. Friction = 6.79 N up the slope.
2. 4g sin 30° − 6.79 = 4a, so 19.6 − 6.79 = 4a, a = 3.20 m s⁻².

(A-level only) A block rests on a rough slope that is gradually tilted. It starts to slip when the angle reaches 25°. Find μ.

1. In limiting equilibrium, μ = tan 25° = 0.466.

(A-level only) A 6 kg block is on a rough slope at 35° (μ = 0.3). Find the least force parallel to the slope that will stop it sliding down, and the least force that will push it up the slope.

1. Weight component down the slope: 6g sin 35° = 33.73 N. Maximum friction: 0.3 × 6g cos 35° = 14.45 N.
2. To stop it sliding down, friction acts up the slope: P = 33.73 − 14.45 = 19.3 N.
3. To push it up, friction acts down the slope: P = 33.73 + 14.45 = 48.2 N.

(A-level only) A 5 kg block on a rough horizontal table (μ = 0.25) is joined by a light inextensible string over a smooth pulley to a 3 kg mass hanging freely. Find the acceleration and the tension.

1. Friction on the block = 0.25 × 5g = 12.25 N.
2. Hanging mass: 3g − T = 3a. Block: T − 12.25 = 5a.
3. Add: 3g − 12.25 = 8a, so 29.4 − 12.25 = 8a, a = 2.14 m s⁻².
4. T = 3(g − a) = 3(9.8 − 2.144) = 23.0 N.

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) Using F = μR when the object is at rest and not about to move.
- (A-level only) Using R = mg when another force has a vertical component, or when on a slope.
- (A-level only) Putting friction in the wrong direction.

### Check questions

1. (A-level only) A 20 kg crate on a rough horizontal floor (μ = 0.35) is pushed by a horizontal force of 80 N. Find its acceleration. [3 marks]
2. (A-level only) A 15 kg sled moves at constant speed across rough horizontal snow, pulled by a rope at 20° above the horizontal with tension 60 N. Find μ. [5 marks]
3. (A-level only) A particle is released from rest on a rough slope at 40° (μ = 0.5). Find its acceleration and the distance it travels in 2 s. [5 marks]
4. (A-level only) A 2 kg block is placed at rest on a rough plane at 20° to the horizontal (μ = 0.4). Show that it does not slide, and find the friction force. [4 marks]
5. (A-level only) A particle is projected up a rough slope at 15° (μ = 0.2) with speed 8 m s⁻¹. Find how far up the slope it travels, and decide whether it then slides back down. [6 marks]
6. (A-level only) A 4 kg block on a rough horizontal table (μ = 0.2) is joined by a light inextensible string over a smooth pulley to a 2 kg mass hanging freely. Find the acceleration and the tension. [5 marks]
7. (A-level only) Explain why a friction force can be less than μR. [1 mark]

### Mark scheme

1. R = 20g, Fmax = 0.35 × 196 = 68.6 N (1); 80 − 68.6 = 20a (1); a = 0.57 m s⁻² (1).
2. Constant speed so forces balance (1); horizontally F = 60 cos 20° = 56.4 N (1); vertically R = 15g − 60 sin 20° = 126.5 N (1); μ = 56.4/126.5 (1) = 0.446 (1).
3. R = mg cos 40°, F = 0.5mg cos 40° (1); mg sin 40° − 0.5mg cos 40° = ma (1); a = 2.55 m s⁻² (1); s = ½ × 2.55 × 4 (1) = 5.09 m (1).
4. Component down the slope 2g sin 20° = 6.70 N (1); Fmax = 0.4 × 2g cos 20° = 7.37 N (1); 6.70 < 7.37 so it stays at rest (1); friction = 6.70 N up the slope (1).
5. Deceleration g(sin 15° + 0.2 cos 15°) (2) = 4.43 m s⁻² (1); 0 = 64 − 2(4.43)s, s = 7.22 m (1); tan 15° = 0.268 > 0.2 (1); so it slides back down (1).
6. Friction 0.2 × 4g = 7.84 N (1); 2g − T = 2a and T − 7.84 = 4a (1); a = 1.96 m s⁻² (1); T = 2(9.8 − 1.96) (1) = 15.7 N (1).
7. μR is the maximum; when the object is at rest and not on the point of slipping, friction only needs to balance the other forces (1).
